The final day of the period of **METHEORTA** (Afterfeast), on which the liturgical observance of a feast comes to an end. The order of service on the day of the Apodosis is fundamentally identical to that of the feast day itself. The Apodosis of a major feast usually occurs eight days after the actual feast day (on its octave). The Apodosis of Pascha occurs on the fortieth day, on the eve of the Ascension. (TB and FL).

**APOKRISIS** (*ἀπόκρισις, ἡ*): The liturgical section in the Divine Liturgy following the Megalynarion, beginning with the presbyter's petition **“Among the first, remember, O Lord...”** (*Ἐν πρώτοις, μνήσθητι, Κύριε...*) and concluding with the choral proclamation **“One is holy, one is the Lord...”** (*Εἷς Ἅγιος, εἷς Κύριος...*), sung immediately prior to the Communion Hymn.

**APOLYSIS** (*Ἀπόλυσις, ἡ*): The Dismissal; the concluding prayer and blessing pronounced by the priest (or officiating bishop) at the close of every divine service.

**DISMISSAL TROPARION** (historically termed *Apolytikion*; Gr. *Ἀπολυτίκιον, τό*; *pl.* *Ἀπολυτίκια, τά*; lit. 'Dismissal Troparion' or 'Dismissal Hymn'). See also **TROPARION**: The principal hymn of a feast day setting forth in concise outline the life of the saint or the theological mystery of the sacred[^575]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Dismissal Troparion Concluded – Troparion of the Day – Aposticha – Apostolikon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1010 / TD-31]

event celebrated on that day. According to liturgical commentators, it is termed the "Dismissal Troparion" (lit. 'Dismissal Hymn') because it is chanted at the conclusion of Vespers just before the Dismissal (*ἀπόλυσις*). It serves as the primary hymn of the day or feast, and is often designated as the 'Troparion of the Day.' It is repeated throughout all the Canonical Hours and during the Divine Liturgy.

**DISMISSAL TROPARION FOR...** (name of saint or event) (lit. 'Apolytikion for...'): This liturgical title designates a common Dismissal Troparion belonging to a general category of saints sharing a similar life or martyr's struggle. This classification corresponds to the saint's title in the Synaxarion, such as “martyr,” “confessor,” “venerable monastic,” or “hieromartyr.”

The Dismissal Troparion is also sung at the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy, at Matins, and at the Small Hours. The Dismissal Troparion (lit. 'Apolytikion') changes according to the liturgical calendar to commemorate the feast of the day. For example, the Dismissal Troparion of the Nativity of Christ is: **“Thy Nativity, O Christ our God, hath shone upon the world the light of knowledge...”** (*«Ἡ Γέννησίς Σου, Χριστὲ ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν...»*). The Dismissal Troparion for the Annunciation is: **“Today is the fountainhead of our salvation and the manifestation of the eternal mystery: the Son of God becometh the Son of the Virgin, and Gabriel announceth the good tidings of grace. Therefore, let us also cry aloud with him unto the Mother of God: Rejoice, full of grace, the Lord is with thee!”** (*«Σήμερον τῆς σωτηρίας ἡμῶν τὸ κεφάλαιον...»*).

**DISMISSAL TROPARION OF...** (name of saint) (*Ἀπολυτίκιον τοῦ (δεῖνος)*; lit. 'Apolytikion of...'): This rubric denotes a proper Dismissal Troparion composed uniquely for the specific saint or commemoration named in the title.

**DISMISSAL TROPARION OF THE TEMPLE** (*Ἀπολυτίκιον τοῦ Ναοῦ*; lit. 'Apolytikion of the Temple'): See **TU NAU**. The Dismissal Troparion of the patron saint or sacred feast to which a particular parish church (*naos*) is dedicated and consecrated.

**APOSTICHA** (*Ἀπόστιχα, τά*) *Slav.* *Stikhiry na stikhovne* (*Стихиры на стиховнѣ*): Hymnographic verses (**STICHERA**) chanted with intercalated Psalm verses. They are sung with appointed Psalm versicles before the Canticle of Simeon (**"Now lettest Thou"**) at Vespers, and near the conclusion of Daily and Lenten Matins. (From APL).

**APOSTOLIKON** (*Ἀποστολικόν, τό*; *pl.* *Ἀποστολικά, τά*): A hymn or collection of hymns celebrating and

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Apostolikon Concluded – Apostolos – Archidiakonos – Archieratikon – Archierefs – Archimandrite Part 1) [Demos, p. 1011 / TD-32]

magnifying the holy Apostles.

**APOSTOLOS** (*Ἀπόστολος, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Apostol* (*Апостолъ*): The liturgical lectionary containing the New Testament pericopes drawn from the Acts of the Apostles and the Epistles of the Holy Apostles (Paul, Peter, John, James, and Jude), arranged for reading throughout the Ecclesiastical Year. It is commonly designated as the Epistle Book. The appointed pericope is read as the first scriptural lection at the Divine Liturgy prior to the Holy Gospel. The Apostolos also contains the **PROKIMENA** and **ALLELUIAS** with their verses sung before and after the Epistle lection.

**ARCHIDIAKONOS** (*Ἀρχιδιάκονος, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Arkhidiakon* (*Архидіаконъ*): The Archdeacon or senior deacon serving the hierarch. See **GREAT ARCHDEACON**.

**ARCHIERATIKON** (*Ἀρχιερατικόν, τό*) *Slav.* *Chinovnik* (*Чиновникъ*); *Roman Pontifical*: In Greek, the 'Book of the Hierarch.' It is the pontifical liturgical book used by the **BISHOP** during a Hierarchical Divine Liturgy and other episcopal rites. It corresponds functionally to the presbyteral **IERATIKON** (*Sluzhebnik*), but contains the ceremonial rubrics and pontifical prayers reserved exclusively to bishops. In addition to the orders for the All-Night Vigil and the Divine Liturgies of St. John Chrysostom, St. Basil the Great, and the Presanctified Gifts, the Archieratikon contains the pontifical ordinals for major orders (deacon, presbyter, bishop) and minor orders (reader, subdeacon), along with the orders for the consecration of holy antiminsia and the dedication of churches.[^576]

**ARCHIEREFS** (*Ἀρχιερεύς, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Arxierey* or *Arxipastyr*: The literal Greek term for 'High Priest' or Chief Hierarch. See **BISHOP**.

**ARCHIMANDRITE** (*Ἀρχιμανδρίτης, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Arkhimandrit* (*Архимандритъ*) (derived from Gr. *archo*, 'I govern,' and *mandra*, 'a sheepfold' or cloister): In Eastern monasticism, the Archimandrite is a celibate presbyter of high dignity, from whose ranks bishops are historically selected. The superior of a major monastery or Laura, or of a congregation of monasteries, is usually an archimandrite. The title arose during the fourth century in the Christian Orient (Mesopotamia, Syria, Persia) and spread to Egypt and Asia Minor. By the fifth century it appears frequently in patristic writings and in

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Archimandrite Continued) [Demos, p. 1012 / TD-33]

the conciliar acts of the ecumenical synods. The dignity was also adopted widely in the Christian West, persisting in regular usage until the ninth century. Originally the archimandrite was simply the superior or abbot of his own monastery; gradually, however, he acquired supervisory jurisdiction over multiple monastic communities, and by the eleventh century archimandrites of great centers such as Mount Athos and Mount Olympus in Bithynia functioned as monastic abbots-general. In modern Greek practice two classes of archimandrites exist: monastic archimandrites governing their monasteries, and honorary or chancery archimandrites—celibate presbyters holding administrative posts in patriarchal or diocesan chanceries. Archimandrites are elevated by the authority of a Patriarch, Metropolitan, or Bishop, and in certain monasteries by monastic election. An archimandrite possesses the liturgical right to wear a pectoral cross and the **EPIGONATION** (*palitsa*) during the Divine Liturgy, and to prefix the sign of the cross to his signature after the manner of hierarchs. Monastic archimandrites also bear the pastoral staff (*rabdos* / *posokh*) and wear the monastic mantle embellished with four embroidered *tabulae* representing the Old and New Law (“the tables of the law”). Their vesture includes the black veil (*epanokalimavkion*) worn over the cylindrical kamilavkion and draping down the back. Archimandrites hold precedence over all other presbyters; among themselves precedence is determined by the date of elevation, though an archimandrite blessed by a patriarch outranks those elevated by lower hierarchs. A solemn liturgical ordinal governs their installation. The dignity exists across the Greek, Ukrainian, Melkite, Italo-Greek, Russian, and Bulgarian Churches, but is not attested among the non-Chalcedonian traditions (Copts, West Syrians, Ethiopians, Armenians). An important historical survival of its use[^577]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Archimandrite Concluded – Artoforion – Artoklasia – Asmatikon – Dormition – Asterisk Part 1) [Demos, p. 1013 / TD-34]

in the West is preserved in Sicily and Southern Italy, where after the reign of Roger II (1130–1154), the Archimandrite of the Basilian Abbey of San Salvatore in Messina exercised quasi-episcopal jurisdiction over Italo-Greek monasteries. (Adapted from the *Catholic Encyclopedia*).

**ARTOFORION** (*Ἀρτοφόριον, τό*) *Slav.* *Artofor, Darokhranitel’nitsa* (*Дарохранительница*), *Kovcheg* or *Kivot* (Tabernacle): The Tabernacle reserved upon the Holy Table. It serves to reserve the Pre-consecrated Gifts for the Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts during Great Lent, as well as the Holy Eucharist consecrated on Great and Holy Thursday and reserved throughout the year for conveyance to the sick (Viaticum).

**ARTOKLASIA** (*Ἀρτοκλασία, ἡ*) *Slav.* *Blagoslovenie khlebov* (*Благословеніе хлѣбовъ*): The liturgical rite of the Blessing of Loaves (lit. 'breaking of bread,' from Gr. *klasis*, breaking, and *artos*, leavened bread) celebrated before the Dismissal Troparion (lit. 'Apolytikion') at Great Vespers during an All-Night **VIGIL** or Litiya. In parish practice it is often celebrated on major feasts at **VESPERS** or **MATINS**, or at the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy. The ceremony commemorates Christ’s miraculous multiplication of the five loaves to feed the five thousand men who had assembled to hear Him preach (Matthew 14:13–21; Mark 6:30–44; Luke 9:11–17; John 6:1–14). The five loaves of wheat bread, accompanied by vessels of wheat, wine, and olive oil, are blessed by the priest with the prayer of Artoklasia, recalling that the Lord Himself is the true “Bread of Life” descended from Heaven.[^578]

**ASMATIKON** (*ᾀσματικόν*): A choral composition chanted in an ornate, melismatic style—such as the Asmatic Trisagion at the conclusion of the Great Doxology, the *Dynamis*, or the *Tu Vimatos* at the Divine Liturgy.

**ASSUMPTION**: See **DORMITION**.

**ASTERISK** (*Ἀστερίσκος, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Zvezditsa* (*Звѣздица*): A sacred liturgical implement consisting of two arched metal bands crossed and joined at the center by a pivot so as to form a four-pointed star. It is placed upon the **PATEN** (Diskos) over the Holy Bread to support the veil (*proskalymma*) and prevent it from touching the sacred particles of the

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Asterisk Concluded – Baldachin – Bastuni – Beatitudes – Beautiful Gate – Belt – Bema – Benedictions Part 1) [Demos, p. 1014 / TD-35]

**LAMB** (*Amnos*) and commemorating portions. The Asterisk (Star) symbolizes the star of Bethlehem which led the Magi to Christ's Nativity and stood over the place where the young Child lay.

**BALDACHIN**: Also termed *baldachino*, *baldaquin*, or *ciborium*; a fixed architectural canopy constructed of stone, wood, metal, or rich fabric erected above the Holy Table and supported by columns. It is also found as an episcopal canopy erected over the bishop's throne (*cathedra*).

**BASTUNI** (*μπαστούνι, τό*): A plain walking cane or staff used by a bishop during everyday pastoral travel. See also **CHAZRANION**.

**BEATITUDES** (*Μακαρισμοί, οἱ*): The eight Gospel blessings pronounced by Christ in His Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5:3–12), sung as the Third Antiphon at the Divine Liturgy with appointed troparia. The term also provides the official honorific title addressed to an Eastern Patriarch or Major Archbishop (“Your Beatitude”). (From FL).

**BEAUTIFUL GATE** (*Ὡραῖα Πύλη, ἡ* / *Royal Doors*): The double doors in the center of the **ICONOSTASIS**, designated in the *Typikon* as the Beautiful Gate or Royal Doors, through which only ordained clergy may pass while vested for divine service. In Eastern liturgical theology, the Beautiful Gate symbolizes the gates of Paradise opened by the Resurrection and the virginal womb of the Most Holy Theotokos through which Christ entered the world. Behind the doors hangs the liturgical veil or curtain (*katapetasma*), which is drawn closed at prescribed moments, especially during the communion of the clergy. The side doors on either side of the Iconostasis are termed the Angel Doors because icons of the Archangels Michael and Gabriel are depicted upon them; they are also called the Deacon's Doors because the deacons enter and exit through them during litanies and censings. When lay servers are blessed to enter the sanctuary, they use the Angel Doors.[^579]

**BELT** (*ζώνη, ἡ*): The liturgical cincture or zone. See **ZONI**.

**BEMA** (*βῆμα, τό*) *Slav.* *Altar'*: In ancient classical architecture, an elevated tribunal or dais. See **VIMA** and **SANCTUARY**.

**BENEDICTIONS** (*Εὐλογητάρια, τά*): See also **EVLOGITARIA**. The Benedictions are a cycle of resurrectional or memorial troparia introduced

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Benedictions Concluded – Biography – Bishop – Bow – Bright Week Part 1) [Demos, p. 1015 / TD-36]

with the Psalm versicle: **“Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes”** (*«Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε, δίδαξόν με τὰ δικαιώματά Σου»*, Psalm 118:12 LXX [MT 119:12]). This hymnographic series follows Psalm 118 (**POLYELEOS**) and is chanted at Sunday Matins, where the hymns are known as the Evlogitaria of the Resurrection (*εὐλογητάρια ἀναστάσιμα*). A second series is sung on Soul Saturdays and at Funerals and Memorial Services; these are the Funeral Evlogitaria (*Εὐλογητάρια νεκρώσιμα*).

**BIOGRAPHY**: See **SYNAXARION** and **MEGAS SYNAXARISTIS**.

**BISHOP** (*Ἐπίσκοπος, ὁ*): *Lit.* 'Overseer.' The Bishop or **HIERARCH** is the chief shepherd, high priest, and spiritual leader of a local eparchy or diocese. He holds hierarchical precedence over archimandrites, priests, and deacons. A bishop possesses the exclusive sacramental power to ordain candidates to the holy priesthood and major orders. He also administers the temporal and spiritual affairs of his diocese. In the apostolic period of the New Testament, the titles of bishop (*episkopos*) and elder (*presbyteros*) were used interchangeably for pastoral leaders of the local churches. However, by the late first and early second century (as witnessed in the epistles of St. Ignatius of Antioch), the Church reserved the title of bishop exclusively for the single chief shepherd consecrated to succeed the Apostles in their office of pastoral oversight (cf. Acts 1:15–26; 14:23; 1 Tim. 3:1–7; Titus 1:5–9). (Adapted from Rev. John W. Morris, Ph.D., *Glossary of Orthodox Terminology*).

**BOW** (*ὑπόκλισις, ἡ* · *ὑποκλίνομαι*): A liturgical bow of reverence (metania), or the rubrical action 'I bow.'

**BRIGHT WEEK**: Bright Week is also termed Renewal Week (*διακαινήσιμος ἑβδομάς, ἡ* / *Светлая седмица*): The festive seven-day period immediately following the celebration of the Holy Resurrection of Our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ—the Feast of Feasts—celebrated yearly as **PASCHA**. The period concludes with **VESPERS** of the following Sunday, the Sunday of St. Thomas (Antipascha). The hymnology of this week is distinguished by the daily rotation of each Byzantine Tone throughout the week, beginning with Tone 1 sung on Pascha Sunday and continuing each day with a differ-[^580]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Bright Week Concluded – Byzantine Tone – Canon – Canonarch – Canticle Part 1) [Demos, p. 1016 / TD-37]

ent Tone, concluding with Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal) on the Sunday of St. Thomas (Antipascha). In this sequence of Tones, Tone 7 (Grave Tone / Mode Varys) is omitted because of its grave, sorrowful character, which is considered unsuitable for this joyous Paschal period. For Christians of the Eastern tradition, Bright Week begins the great period of festal celebration that continues for fifty days until Pentecost.

**BYZANTINE TONE** (lit. 'Byzantine Mode'): See **TONE** (Mode).

**CANON** (*κανών, ὁ*; *pl.* *κανόνες, οἱ*; See also **KANON**, **KANONES**) *Slav.* *Kanon*: A principal structural component in the Matins (Orthros) service, although it also occurs at Compline, Midnight Office, and in various trebniki (services of supplication or *parakleseis*). A Canon is a lengthy poetic hymnographic composition structured into nine Odes, with each Ode consisting of multiple troparia, the exact number and appointment of which are regulated by the *Typikon*. Each Ode takes its overarching theme from, and is modeled upon, one of the nine Biblical Canticles (for example, Ode 1 is linked to the Canticle of Moses in Exodus 15:1–19, which serves as its biblical prototype). In parish churches, unlike monasteries, the full verses of the Biblical Canticles (apart from the Magnificat) are customarily omitted, and brief liturgical refrains (such as **"Glory to Thee, our God, glory to Thee"** or **"Most Holy Theotokos, save us"**) are chanted in place of the scriptural verses. In daily liturgical practice, most Canons contain only eight Odes, because Ode 2 is appointed solely on Tuesdays during Great Lent, although the poetic acrostics of the canons frequently indicate that nine odes were originally composed. Normally three canons, and on Sundays four canons, are prescribed, appointed in such a manner that each Ode, including the Heirmos, comprises 14 troparia. (Excerpted from PL and AE).[^581]

**CANONARCH** (*κανονάρχης, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Kanonarkh*: The Canonarch is a tonsured liturgical chanter or reader assigned to ensure that the choir chants the prescribed liturgical texts in the appointed Tone and with the proper melody. He performs this function by reciting, in a solemn recitative cadence, each phrase of a slow sticheron or hymn immediately before the chanter or choir chants it with its full melody and meter. The Canonarch holds precedence higher than an ordinary Reader and lower than a Subdeacon or Deacon. The Canonarch is tonsured by the Bishop through the cheirothesia for minor orders. In early Byzantine practice, this tonsure was linked with that of the subdiaconate or reader. The Slavonic *Typikon* prescribes that a monastic canonarch fulfills his liturgical office with his head covered (that is, wearing the monastic klobuk).

**CANTICLE** (*ᾠδή, ἡ*; ODE OR SONG) *Slav.* *Pesn'*: The English term is derived from the Latin *canticulum* (a diminutive of *canticum*, song, from *cantare*, to sing). It is employed in Western biblical terminology as the equivalent of the Vulgate *canticum*. The Authorized King James Version generally translates both Hebrew and Greek terms simply as "song."

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Canticle The Nine Biblical Odes) [Demos, p. 1017 / TD-38]

from *canticulum*, a diminutive of *canticum* (song), derived from the Latin *cantare* (to sing). It is used in Catholic English translations of the Sacred Scriptures as the equivalent of the Latin Vulgate *canticum* in most instances. The Authorized Version does not maintain this distinction, translating both Hebrew and Greek equivalents simply as "song."

The Biblical Odes (also termed Canticles) are nine scriptural hymns taken directly from the Sacred Scriptures (the Old and New Testaments). They are chanted at Matins and form the biblical foundation of the Canon, a principal component of Byzantine morning prayer.

The title Canticle is applied: (a) to specific scriptural prayers and poetic compositions outside the Psalter; and (b) to the nine (in daily practice, eight) poetic subdivisions of the liturgical Canon, originally composed to accompany the chanting of the Biblical Canticles.

There are Nine Biblical Canticles appointed for liturgical use at Matins (*Ὄρθρος* / Orthros). In the Eastern liturgical tradition, these canticles are appended to the liturgical Psalter as follows:[^582]

1. The (First) Song of Moses (**"Let us sing unto the Lord"**, Exodus 15:1–19)
2. The (Second) Song of Moses (**"Give ear, O heavens"**, Deuteronomy 32:1–43)
3. The Prayer of Hannah, the mother of Samuel the Prophet (**"My heart is established in the Lord"**, 1 Kingdoms 2:1–10 LXX [1 Samuel 2:1–10])
4. The Prayer of the Prophet Habakkuk (**"O Lord, I have heard Thy report, and was afraid"**, Habakkuk 3:2–19 LXX)
5. The Prayer of the Prophet Isaiah (**"From night until morning my spirit seeketh Thee early"**, Isaiah 26:9–20 LXX)
6. The Prayer of the Prophet Jonah (**"I cried in mine affliction unto the Lord"**, Jonah 2:2–9 LXX)
*7. The Prayer of the Three Holy Children in the Furnace (**"Blessed art Thou, O Lord God of our fathers"**, Daniel 3:26–56 LXX)
*8. The Song of the Three Holy Children (**"Bless the Lord, all ye works of the Lord"** / *The Benedicite*, Daniel 3:57–88 LXX)

*(The above two Odes are preserved in the canonical Septuagint Greek text of Daniel; their versification conforms to the liturgical Psalter, differing slightly from modern Hebrew-based translations).*

9. The Song of the Theotokos (the Magnificat: **"My soul doth magnify the Lord"**, Luke 1:46–

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Canticle Concluded – Cassock Part 1) [Demos, p. 1018 / TD-39]

55) and the Canticle of Zechariah, father of the Holy Forerunner (the *Benedictus*, Luke 1:68–79).

Originally, all nine Biblical Canticles were chanted in their entirety at Matins every day, with brief psalm refrains intercalated between each verse.

Eventually, short liturgical hymns (**TROPARIA**) were composed to replace these simple refrains—a hymnographic development traditionally inaugurated by Saint Andrew of Crete (c. 660–740).

In daily practice, the Second Ode is omitted from the Canon because of its severe, penitential denunciations of human sin. The primary exception to this universal rule is the Great Penitential Canon of Saint Andrew of Crete, which contains all nine Odes and is chanted during Great Lent.

Over the centuries, the direct chanting of the full scriptural verses of the Biblical Canticles was gradually discontinued (except for the Magnificat), and only the composed troparia of the canon were chanted, tied to the themes and rhythm of the biblical canticle by the opening **HEIRMOS** (lit. 'irmos'). During Great Lent, however, the ancient practice is preserved and the full Biblical Canticles are still chanted.

(Adapted from *The Festal Menaion* by Mother Mary and Metropolitan Kallistos Ware, and the *Catholic Encyclopedia*).[^583]

**CASSOCK** (*ράσον, τό* · *χιτών, ὁ* · *ἱμάτιον, τό*) *Slav.* *Podryasnik* / *Ryasa*: The cassock is the basic clerical and monastic garment of the Eastern Church, existing in two primary forms: the inner cassock (*anteri* / *podryasnik*) and the outer cassock (*raso* / *ryasa*). The inner cassock is an ankle-length tunic with closely fitted sleeves. The outer cassock is a loose, flowing robe with wide, open sleeves. Both inner and outer cassocks are worn by bishops, priests, deacons, monastics, and tonsured clergy. In modern parish practice, blessing to wear the cassock is also granted to seminarians, novices, subdeacons, and altar servers.

The latter two Greek terms in the heading above (*χιτών* and *ἱμάτιον*) designate ancient garments (the inner tunic and the outer mantle). These terms are of special liturgical interest because both are employed in the baptismal hymn chanted when clothing the newly baptized in the white christening robe: **“A robe of divine light bestow upon me, O Thou that for vesture arrayest Thyself with Light; and bestow many mercies, O Christ our God, Who art plenteous in mercy.”**

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Cassock Concluded – Celebrant – Censer – Ceremony) [Demos, p. 1019 / TD-40]

(*«Χιτῶνά μοι παράσχου φωτεινόν, ὁ ἀναβαλλόμενος φῶς ὡς ἱμάτιον, πολυέλεε Χριστὲ ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν.»*) This identical baptismal troparion is sung for either male or female candidates receiving Holy Baptism.

**CELEBRANT** (*ἱεροτελεστής, ὁ*): The Bishop or Priest who validly celebrates the Holy Eucharist (Divine Liturgy). The Deacon may never celebrate the Eucharist, but ministers and assists the celebrant throughout the sacred rite.

**CENSER** (*θυμιατόν, τό* or *θυμιατήριον, τό*) *Slav.* *Kadilo* (*Кадило*): The liturgical vessel used for burning incense. In the Eastern Church the censer is suspended by four chains (three representing the Holy Trinity and one representing the Unity of the Godhead), often adorned with twelve bells symbolizing the proclamation of the twelve Apostles. Censers are used constantly throughout all liturgical services, especially at Vespers, Matins, and the Divine Liturgy. When a deacon serves, he performs the rubrical censings; otherwise, the celebrating priest censes. Tonsured altar servers may prepare and carry the censer, but only ordained clergy may swing it during public liturgical prayers. Censing symbolizes the sweet fragrance of the Holy Spirit, the sanctification of the faithful and the temple, and the ascending prayers of the Christian community rising up to heaven (cf. Revelation 8:3–4). The standard psalm verse chanted during censing at Vespers is: **“Let my prayer be set forth before Thee as incense, the lifting up of my hands as an evening sacrifice.”** (*«Κατευθυνθήτω ἡ προσευχή μου, ὡς θυμίαμα ἐνώπιόν σου· ἔπαρσις τῶν χειρῶν μου, θυσία ἑσπερινή.»*, Psalm 140:2 LXX). Eastern Christians also utilize a small hand censer (**KATZION** / *κατζίον*), which has no suspending chains or bells and is swung in the form of a cross before sacred icons or deceased persons.

In domestic prayer, faithful use granulated natural incense placed upon quick-lighting charcoal briquettes in a home censer.[^584]

**CEREMONY** (*τελετή, ἡ*): See **TELETI**. A sacred liturgical rite, solemn service, or sacramental ordinance within the liturgical tradition of the Church.

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Chasmodia – Chazranion – Chief Secretary – Choneftirion – Chorostaton Archierefs – Chorostatundes Archieris – Chromatic Scale – Chrism Part 1) [Demos, p. 1020 / TD-41]

**CHASMODIA** (*χασμῳδία, ἡ*): Liturgical cacophony or discordant singing resulting from disorder, rhythmic confusion, and lack of musical clarity during church services. This vocal disorder is specifically prohibited by Canon XV of the Council of Laodicea, which ordained that only canonical cantors (*psaltai*) should chant from the ambo.

**CHAZRANION** (*χαζράνιον, τό*): A plain, unadorned wooden walking stick used by a bishop during everyday travel or informal pastoral processions. See also **BASTOUNI** and **PABERITSA**.

**CHIEF SECRETARY** (*Ἀρχιγραμματεύς, ὁ*): The Chancellor or Chief Secretary of a Patriarchate, Synod, or Eparchy who oversees official correspondence, archives, and chancery scribes.

**CHONEFTIRION** (*χωνευτήριον, τό*): A sacrarium or sacred drain (piscina) located within or adjacent to the sanctuary (*bema*). It contains a sink or washbasin where the clergy wash their hands prior to the Proskomide and after Holy Communion, and into which holy water or consecrated matter discarded from the Sacred Mysteries is reverently poured directly into the earth. See also **THALASSIDION**.

**CHOROSTATON ARCHIEREFS, O** (*Χοροστατῶν Ἀρχιερεύς, ὁ*): The presiding Hierarch (Bishop, Metropolitan, or Patriarch) who presides chorally from the episcopal throne (*cathedra*) during a service (such as Vespers or Matins) without fully vesting as the principal celebrating priest.

**CHOROSTATUNDES ARCHIERIS, I** (*Χοροστατοῦντες Ἀρχιερεῖς, οἱ*): Plural of the above: Officiating or presiding Hierarchs presiding from the choir or thrones.

**CHROMATIC SCALE** (*χρωματικόν*): In Byzantine chant theory, a modal scale employing chromatic intervals (augmented seconds and half steps) outside the diatonic scale. In modern Western musical terms, these tones correspond to the altered pitches (sharps and flats) of a keyboard. For example, while the diatonic Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal) uses the natural diatonic scale (C–D–E–F–G–A–B–C), the chromatic form of Tone 6 (Mode II Plagal) utilizes intervals with augmented steps (such as C–D♭–E–F–G–A♭–B–C).

**CHRISM** (*Χρῖσμα, τό* / Holy Myron) *Slav.* *Svyate Myro* (*Святое Мѵро*): The consecrated holy oil compounded of pure olive oil, white wine, and aromatic gums, resins, and essences, used for the Mystery of Chrismation and the solemn consecration of church altars, antiminsia, and sacred vessels. Historically it has been known by various titles, including “oil of thanksgiving,” “heavenly chrism,” “mystical chrism,” and “Holy and Great Myron.” Holy Chrism is consecrated exclusively by the Primate (Patriarch or Major Archbishop) and the Holy Synod during Holy Week (typically on Great and Holy Thursday), usually every ten to twelve years according to the needs of the church. The official formula for compounding the Holy Myron in the Great Church of Constantinople...[^585]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Chrism Concluded – Church Architecture Part 1) [Demos, p. 1021 / TD-42]

consecrated at the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople is rooted in the biblical formula of the sacred anointing oil given to Moses (Exodus 30:22–25) and currently incorporates 64 specific ingredients, including pure olive oil, red wine, mastic, balsam of Mecca, rose water, and numerous fragrant botanicals and essences.

**CHURCH** (*ἐκκλησία, ἡ* or *ἱερὸς ναός, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Tserkov'* / *Khram*: In Greek liturgical usage, the term *ecclesia* primarily denotes the Christian assembly or church building, while *hieros naos* (Holy Temple) emphasizes its consecrated liturgical function as the dwelling place of God. Christian architecture has historically developed in dialogue with theological doctrine, spiritual symbolism, and the cultural environment of each era. The earliest Christians assembled for the Eucharist in private homes (*domus ecclesiae*); during periods of imperial persecution, they gathered covertly in catacombs, caves, and secluded dwellings. Following the Edict of Milan issued by Constantine the Great in 313, Christians began erecting monumental public basilicas.

Among these sacred monuments, the most renowned and influential is the Great Church of the Holy Wisdom of God (**Hagia Sophia**) in Constantinople. Originally founded by Constantine the Great, rebuilt after fire, and destroyed during the Nika riots following the era of Saint John Chrysostom, the present magnificent cathedral was erected by the Emperor Justinian I. Consecrated on December 27, 537, Hagia Sophia became the theological and architectural paradigm for church construction throughout Eastern Christendom.

In Eastern Christian tradition, churches are typically designed according to a cruciform plan crowned by a central dome, symbolizing heaven descending to earth and the cosmic sovereignty of Christ the Pantokrator. The interior is divided into three distinct liturgical areas: the **NARTHEX** (entrance vestibule), the **NAVE** (*naos*, where the faithful stand for worship), and the **SANCTUARY** (*bema* or Holy Table area, separated by the **ICONOSTASIS**). This tripartite division mirrors the ancient Tabernacle and Temple of Jerusalem (the Outer Court, the Holy Place, and the Holy of Holies). The architectural lines lead the eyes upward toward the dome, enriched by a hierarchical iconographic program reflecting the heavenly hierarchy and the redeemed cosmos. The structures frequently bear a golden Cross at their highest point, often illuminated through-[^586]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Church Concluded – Chyma – Codex – Commemoration Part 1) [Demos, p. 1022 / TD-43]

out the night, shining forth to dispel the darkness.

The central theological purpose of an Eastern church building is to manifest the divine presence and create a sacred threshold where heaven and earth unite. Byzantine theology maintains no artificial dichotomy between spiritual contemplation and sensory perception: architecture, iconography, incense, hymnody, and liturgical vestments together engage the whole human person in the adoration of the Creator. An ancient historical account recorded in the Primary Chronicle (*Tale of Bygone Years*) relates that Prince Vladimir of Kyiv sent envoys across the world to investigate various religious faiths. When the envoys attended the Divine Liturgy in Hagia Sophia at Constantinople, they reported back to Kyiv:
> “We knew not whether we were in heaven or on earth, for on earth there is no such splendor or such beauty, and we are at a loss to describe it. We know only that God dwells there among men, and their service surpasses the worship of all other places.”

**CHYMA** (*χῦμα*): *Lit.* 'in a poured, flowing stream.' A liturgical rubric specifying that a text is to be read in a simple, continuous speaking tone or steady recitative rather than chanted with elaborate melismatic melodies. On occasion, the term also denotes a continuous, flowing recitative chant, such as the priest's chanted petitions. The *Typikon* explicitly distinguishes between these modes of execution, employing the directives **“it is said chyma”** (*λέγεται χῦμα*, simple reading) and **“it is chanted chyma”** (*ψάλλεται χῦμα*, straight recitative chanting).

**CODEX** (*κῶδιξ, ὁ*; *pl.* **CODICES** / *κώδικες, οἱ*): Originally designating a bound collection of wooden tablets coated with wax for writing, the term *codex* came to denote a manuscript book composed of bound sheets of papyrus or parchment. The widespread adoption of the codex format was fundamentally spurred by early Christianity. While pagan and classical texts long continued to be written on scrolls (*volumina*), Christians embraced the parchment codex in the first and second centuries for liturgical and scriptural use, because a codex permitted writing on both sides of the folio (recto and verso), facilitated rapid cross-referencing between Old and New Testament passages, and was far more portable. By the fourth century, the codex had completely superseded the scroll for lengthy ecclesiastical documents and biblical manuscripts. In Christian iconography, Old Testament prophets are traditionally depicted holding scrolls, whereas the holy Evangelists hold bound codices.[^587]

**COMMEMORATION** (*μνημόσυνον, τό*) *Slav.* *Pominaniye* (*Поминаніе*): In the liturgical life of the Church,

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Commemoration Concluded – Compline – Confessor Part 1) [Demos, p. 1023 / TD-44]

the liturgical act and prayer of remembering before God the living and the deceased. During sacred feasts, the Church commemorates the saving events of salvation history and the triumphs of holy martyrs, hierarchs, and venerated saints.

**COMPLINE** (*ἀπόδειπνον, τό*) *Slav.* *Povecheriye* (*Повечеріе*): *Lit.* 'after-supper service.' See also **APODIPNON**. The English term *compline* derives from the Latin *completorium*, denoting the completion of the working day. Compline is the final liturgical office of the daily cycle, prayed in the evening prior to nocturnal rest.

In Eastern liturgical practice, Compline exists in two distinct forms: **Great Compline** (*Μέγα Ἀπόδειπνον* / *Velikoye Povecheriye*) and **Small Compline** (*Μικρὸν Ἀπόδειπνον* / *Maloye Povecheriye*). Small Compline is celebrated on ordinary days throughout the liturgical year and includes an appointed Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos in the Tone of the week (from the *Oktoechos*). Commemorations of saints from the *Menaion* that cannot be celebrated on their appointed calendar day may be transferred and prayed at Compline, reading their proper canon alongside the canon of the Theotokos and singing their appointed aposticha stichera from Vespers. In addition, special canons are appointed for Compline during Forefeasts, Afterfeasts, and the Pentecostarion period. Compline concludes with the solemn Mutual Asking of Forgiveness between the celebrant and the congregation. In monastic tradition, notably on the Holy Mountain of Athos, the community venerates the holy relics and icons of the church at the conclusion of Compline and receives the blessing of the abbot or officiating priest.[^588]

**CONFESSOR** (*ὁμολογητής, ὁ*) *Slav.* *Ispovednik* (*Исповѣдникъ*): An official category of saint applied to holy men and women who courageously witnessed to and defended the Orthodox Catholic faith before persecutors and heretics, enduring torture, imprisonment, or exile for the name of Christ, yet without suffering the actual physical crown of death during their perse-

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Confessor Concluded – Creed Part 1) [Demos, p. 1024 / TD-45]

cution, such as Saint Maximos the Confessor, commemorated on January 21.

**CREED** (*Σύμβολον τῆς Πίστεως, τό* / The Symbol of Faith) *Slav.* *Simvol Very* (*Сѵмволъ Вѣры*): Derived from the Latin *credo* (“I believe”). In Greek Christian tradition, the Creed is termed the **Symbol of Faith** (*Σύμβολον τῆς Πίστεως*), denoting the authoritative gathering and harmonization of the apostolic teachings by which Christians profess their common faith in the Triune God.

The official Creed of the Eastern Church is the **Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed**, formulated at the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea (325) and completed and ratified at the Second Ecumenical Council in Constantinople (381).

In the early Church, diverse baptismal confessions of faith existed across various apostolic sees. These confessions were rooted in the baptismal liturgy, wherein catechumens were required to solemnly profess their personal faith prior to descending into the baptismal font. The earliest apostolic confession was the concise profession that Jesus is the Christ and the Lord. Through this public confession, the catechumen was baptized into Christ, dying and rising with Him into the new life of the Kingdom of God in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit (cf. Matthew 28:19; Romans 6:3–4).

As the Church expanded, local churches developed more detailed baptismal creeds to articulate the apostolic faith against emergent heresies. In the fourth century, the Arian crisis convulsed the Christian world regarding the eternal divinity of the Son of God (the divine Logos). The heresiarch Arius maintained that the Son was a created being, saying “there was a time when He was not.” Orthodox fathers, led by Saint Athanasius the Great, confessed that the Son is eternal, uncreated, and consubstantial (*homoousios*) with the Father.

This doctrinal conflict was resolved by the 318 Holy Fathers convened by Emperor Constantine at the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea in 325, who promulgated the authoritative Symbol of Faith.[^589]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (The Symbol of Faith / Creed Concluded) [Demos, p. 1025 / TD-46]

menical Council, which confessed the following sacred text:
> “I believe in one God, the Father, the Almighty, Creator of heaven and earth, and of all things visible and invisible.
> And in one Lord, Jesus Christ, the only-begotten Son of God, begotten of the Father before all ages; Light of Light, true God of true God, begotten, not created, of one essence with the Father, through Whom all things were made;
> Who for us and for our salvation came down from the heavens and was incarnate by the Holy Spirit and the Virgin Mary, and became man.
> Crucified for us under Pontius Pilate, He suffered and was buried;
> Rising on the third day according to the Scriptures,
> And ascending into the heavens, He is seated at the right hand of the Father;
> And coming again in glory to judge the living and the dead, His Kingdom shall have no end;
> And in the Holy Spirit, the Lord, the Giver of life, Who proceeds from the Father, Who together with the Father and the Son is worshipped and glorified, Who spoke through the prophets;”

Connected with the controversy about the Son of God, the Divine Word, was the dispute about the procession of the Holy Spirit. The following definition of the Council in Constantinople in 381, which has come to be known as the Second Ecumenical Council was added to the Nicene statement above:
> “And [I believe] in the Holy Spirit, the Lord, the Giver of Life, Who proceeds from the Father; Who together with the Father and the Son is worshipped and glorified, Who spoke through the prophets. In one Holy, Catholic, and Apostolic Church I acknowledge one baptism for the remission of sins. I expect the resurrection of the dead; and the life of the age to come. Amen.”

This now complete Symbol of Faith was ultimately adopted throughout the entire Orthodox Church. It was put into the first person form “I believe” and used for the formal and official confession of faith made by a person (or his or her sponsor-godparent) at their baptism. It is also used as the formal statement of faith by a non-[^590]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (The Symbol of Faith Concluded – Cuffs – Order of Deacons Part 1) [Demos, p. 1026 / TD-47]

Orthodox Christian entering the communion of the Orthodox Church. In the same way, the Creed became part of the life of Orthodox Christians and an essential element of the Divine Liturgy of the Orthodox Church, at which each person formally and officially accepts and renews his baptism and membership in the Church. Thus, the Symbol of Faith is the only part of the Liturgy (repeated in another form just before Holy Communion) which is formulated in the first person singular. Most other hymns and prayers of the Liturgy are plural, beginning with “we”. The creedal confession begins with “I,” because faith is at first personal, and only then corporate and communal.[^591]

**CUFFS** (*᾿Επιμανίκια, τά* / Epimanikia; Slav. *Narukavnytsi* or *Poruchi*): See **EPIMANIKIA**.

**DEACON(S), SECOND, THIRD, etc.** (*Διάκονος, Δευτερεύων, Τριτεύων, κλπ.*; Slav. *Diakon*):

The Deacon is the third in order of the major sacred orders of clergy in the Orthodox Church, following the **BISHOP** and the **PRIEST**. The word deacon signifies a minister or server, originally designating one who waited upon tables (cf. Acts 6:1–6).

In the contemporary Church, the deacon ministers to the bishop and priest in the divine services and assists in the celebration of the Holy Mysteries (Sacraments) of the Church. A deacon may not, however, celebrate the Holy Mysteries by himself.

With the blessing of the celebrating bishop or priest, the deacon leads the faithful in the collective prayers (the litanies or ectenies) and reads from the Holy Scriptures (the Holy Gospel) during the divine services. He is likewise responsible for the ceremonial decorum of public worship, calling the assembly to attention at the appointed liturgical moments.

In addition, the deacon may perform other duties pertaining to Church life according to pastoral circumstance, with the blessing and at the direction of his bishop or priest: for example, the distribution of alms to the needy, or the distribution of the Holy Eucharist to the faithful from a second chalice at the Divine Liturgy. The deacon, however, may never consecrate the Holy Gifts.

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Order of Deacons Concluded – Despotikon / Despotika Part 1) [Demos, p. 1027 / TD-48]

In the Orthodox Church, the diaconate is not merely a transitional step toward the priesthood; numerous deacons embrace it as a permanent vocation, serving the ministry of the Church either full-time or in conjunction with secular occupations.

While early deacons of the Church primarily assisted bishops in charitable administration and works of mercy, in later centuries the diaconate evolved into a predominantly liturgical office wherein deacons assist at the celebration of liturgical services. Today, deacons frequently direct educational initiatives and youth ministries, visit hospitals, engage in missionary outreach, and oversee charitable programs.

Sacramentally, all deacons share equal clerical grace. Nevertheless, they are ranked and serve by precedence according to the date of their ordination.

Just as with bishops and presbyters, administrative distinctions of precedence exist among deacons. A senior deacon of a cathedral or principal church may be awarded the dignity of **PROTODEACON** (*πρωτοδιάκονος*, after the prototype of the Holy Protomartyr and Archdeacon Stephen), obtaining precedence when serving with other deacons. The chief deacon who attends specifically upon a bishop is designated an **ARCHDEACON** (*ἀρχιδιάκονος*). A deacon who has received monastic tonsure is called a **hierodeacon** (*ἱεροδιάκονος*).

In formal correspondence and ecclesiastical protocol (for instance, in epistolary salutations or formal introductions), a deacon is addressed as “The Reverend Deacon [Name].” The title is customarily abbreviated Dcn. or Dn. (though Dn. is likewise employed as an abbreviation for dean).

In informal address and ordinary conversation, it is customary to address a deacon as “Deacon [Name],” although in certain local traditions he is addressed simply as “Father”—for example, “Father Deacon [Name]” or “Father [Name].”

For an architectural and liturgical description of the deacon’s sacred vestments, see **VESTMENTS**.[^592]

**DESPOTIKON, DESPOTIKA** (*Δεσποτικόν, τό; Δεσποτικά, τά*):

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Despotikon Concluded – Despotis – Deuterocanon – Diakonikon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1028 / TD-49]

A liturgical hymn or cycle of hymns celebrating God as the Sovereign Master (*Despotes* / *Δεσπότης*) of all creation.

**DESPOTIS** (*Δεσπότης, ὁ*): See **BISHOP**.

**DEUTEROCANON** (*ἀναγινωσκώμενα, τά* / *Anaginoskomena*, "books read"): The ecclesiastical designation for the deuterocanonical books of the Sacred Scriptures. These comprise the holy books recognized by the Eastern Orthodox and Catholic Churches as inspired, canonical components of the Christian Old Testament, which are absent from the rabbinic Hebrew Masoretic canon. The term *deuterocanonical* derives from the Greek meaning “pertaining to the second canon.” Although the etymology might suggest a secondary status, reflecting the historical deliberations before their universal conciliar ratification, the term in Catholic and Orthodox theology does not imply inferior authority or non-canonicity, despite occasional conflation by outside commentators with the Apocrypha.

Protestant communions do not classify these sacred books as canonical Scripture; they generally omit them from published editions or segregate them under the heading of Apocrypha. The divergence in nomenclature often generates confusion between Catholic and Orthodox canonical corpora and non-canonical pseudepigrapha.

**DIAKONIKON** (*διακονικόν, τό*; Slav. *Diakonik*): The Diakonikon is the sacred vestry and liturgical repository situated on the south side within the **SANCTUARY**, where sacred vessels, vestments, and liturgical appointments are stored. The holy icon traditionally enshrined here depicts either the Resurrection (**"The Resurrection"** / *῾Η ᾿Ανάστασις*) or the Extreme Humility of Christ (**"The Extreme Humility"** / *ἄκρα ταπείνωσις*). It is likewise in the Diakonikon that icons brought by the faithful for blessing remain deposited for forty days.[^593]

### The PROTHESIS, the DIAKONIKON, and the SKEVOPHYLAKION

The **SANCTUARY** of the Church is the Holy of Holies, wherein man, through the grace of God, unites with the angelic hosts in offering unceasing thanksgiving and glory to our Heavenly Father. Notwithstanding its mystical transcendence, the Sanctuary functions with precise liturgical efficiency. Like the **NARTHEX** and the **NAVE**, the Sanctuary is architecturally disposed to serve the ritual actions of the clergy who officiate at the Divine Services.

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diakonikon Concluded – Skevophylakion – Diataxis Part 1) [Demos, p. 1029 / TD-50]

Looking through the central **BEAUTIFUL GATE** (*Ὡραία Πύλη* / Royal Doors) into the Sanctuary, one observes a recessed marble table or niche on either side of the eastern wall. The table situated on the north (liturgical left) is the **PROTHESIS** (Table of Oblation / *Proskomide*), while that on the south (liturgical right) is the **DIAKONIKON**. The distinctive functions of these two sanctuary chambers are defined by the sacred vessels resting upon them and the sacred iconography adorning their respective walls.

The **PROTHESIS** is the sacred table upon which the Eucharistic Gifts of bread and wine are prepared during the Proskomide office, performed during the morning Matins (*Ὄρθρος* / Orthros) prior to the celebration of the Divine Liturgy. In addition, the holy vessels and implements—the Holy Chalice, the Holy Paten (*Diskos*), the liturgical veils and Aer, the Asterisk, the Spear, and the Communion Spoon—are vested and preserved upon it. Upon the wall behind the Prothesis is customarily depicted the icon of the Nativity of Christ, commemorating that Christ is the sacrificial Lamb offered “on behalf of all and for all.”

In the early centuries of the Church, these subsidiary tables were not located within the sanctuary proper. Instead, the Eucharistic Gifts were prepared and the liturgical treasures preserved in an adjacent, independent structure designated the **SKEVOPHYLAKION** (*σκευοφυλάκιον*, vessel-guardhouse). From this external treasury the Holy Gospel and the Eucharistic Offerings were solemnly carried into the temple at the appointed liturgical moments by the clergy—a ceremonial movement preserved today in the processions of the Small Entrance with the Gospel and the Great Entrance with the Gifts. Over time, as liturgical architecture evolved, the separate *skevophylakion* fell into disuse, replaced by the integrated Prothesis and Diakonikon niches within the sanctuary itself.

**DIATAXIS** (*διάταξις, ἡ*): Lit. arrangement, order, or rubrical disposition. In historical and contemporary liturgical usage, a Diataxis comprises precise rubrical directions for the celebrants, deacons, and chanters conducting the ceremonial rites of the Church. These rubrics (*rubrica*) were traditionally inscribed in red ink (cinnabar) to distinguish them from the black ink used for the recited prayers and liturgical texts. A **Patriarchal Diataxis** (*Πατριαρχικὴ Διάταξις*), specifically, codifies the ceremonial order observed when the Ecumenical Patriarch presides at the Divine Liturgy, detailing chancery protocol, hierarchical vesture, concelebration etiquette, and[^594]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 2: Gemistos Patriarchal Diataxis Abstract) [Demos, p. 1030 / TD-51]

the specifics of the various rites of ordination. They also carefully and completely describe the rite of the **PROTHESIS** (*Πρόθεσις*) or preparation, performed before the Divine Liturgy but only by a priest and deacon without the participation of the Patriarch.

The following scholarly summary by Fr. Alexander Rentel is taken from his doctoral dissertation abstract and is cited here:

> “One of the most important extant Patriarchal Diataxes is attributed to a leading figure of the chancery in the second half of the 14th century: the Protonotary (*Πρωτονοτάριος*), Kyr Deacon Dimitrios Gemistos (ca. 1397), later designated the **MEGA SAKELLARIOS** (*Μέγας Σακελλάριος*) of the Great and Most Holy Church of God. An important scribal annotation recorded in the upper margin of folio 3r in manuscript Athos Vatopedi 135 (AD 1390), the principal codex of the Diataxis, provides a precise date for its composition: August 1386. If indeed Dimitrios Gemistos composed this Diataxis, this does not mean Gemistos created something entirely novel out of nothing. A comparative analysis of his Diataxis with other liturgical witnesses reveals that he compiled it by synthesizing both local and external traditions: the cathedral traditions of the patriarchal rite in Constantinople and the Athonite monastic Diataxis for the Divine Liturgy formulated by Philotheos Kokkinos (ca. 1300–1377/78), twice Patriarch of Constantinople (1353–1354, 1364–1376).
> 
> The present dissertation presents, for the first time, a critical edition of Gemistos’ Diataxis, together with an investigation into its liturgical context and an exploration of the historical circumstances surrounding its composition.
> 
> Implicit in Anton Baumstark’s celebrated maxim that it is the nature of liturgy “to relate itself to the concrete situations of times and places,”[1] is the premise that the historical situation which gave rise to a liturgical monument can be deduced through rigorous analysis of that document. Thus, in the case of Gemistos’ Diataxis, historical currents emerge through close exegesis of the text and the liturgical reconstruction of the rites therein contained. Gemistos’ adoption of the Philothean Diataxis speaks volumes about the profound influence of Athonite hesychast monasticism upon the Constantinopolitan[^595]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 3: Gemistos Diataxis Sources and Philotheos) [Demos, p. 1031 / TD-52]

> patriarchal throne. Looking beyond the historical situation in which Gemistos’ Diataxis was created, one can already see developing the rough outline of the transformation the Byzantine Church will undergo, one that will allow it to survive through the long centuries of Ottoman domination. This is a transformation that will have at its core a strong central patriarchate with a powerful administrative bureaucracy utilizing all the ancient imperial and sacred symbols to convey its authority.
> 
> In order to discern these currents at work, the distinct strata of sources embedded within Gemistos’ Diataxis must be differentiated and identified. Subsequently, the manner in which he deployed his sources requires critical examination. Only at this juncture can we begin to comprehend why he selected the sources he employed. In other words, to ascertain precisely what Gemistos himself authored, what he adapted, and what he interpolated into his Diataxis, those sections displaying literary dependence must be compared systematically with their demonstrable exemplars (the Philothean Diataxis and the official Euchologion for ordination rites). An explanation must be formulated to account for their textual concordance as well as their variations. The findings of this analytical deconstruction illuminate the historical origins of the Diataxis.
> 
> The initial phase in analyzing Gemistos’ Diataxis requires disentangling the Philothean Diataxis from his text and establishing Gemistos’ relationship to this direct precursor in the Byzantine Diataxis tradition. Nowhere is Gemistos’ dependence upon the Philothean Diataxis more pronounced than in the Prothesis rite: approximately seventy-five percent of the Prothesis rite in Gemistos’ text is adopted directly from the Philothean Diataxis, either verbatim or with merely stylistic modifications. Throughout the remainder of his Diataxis, Gemistos utilizes Philotheos’ text far more sparingly, drawing primarily upon original compositions or unidentified sources. Evidently, one of Gemistos’ principal objectives in compiling his Patriarchal Diataxis was to synthesize the Philothean Diataxis—most notably its monastic Prothesis rite—with the patriarchal cathedral ceremonial and Constantinopolitan traditions. Yet while Gemistos’ intent to incorporate the Philothean Diataxis is manifest, his underlying motives remain complex. Philotheos, after all, was a deposed patriarch whose ecclesiastical legacy[^596]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 4: Dimitrios Gemistos Chancery Career) [Demos, p. 1032 / TD-53]

> was so controversial that his liturgical commemoration was prohibited by his immediate successor, Patriarch Makarios. Furthermore, Philotheos’ Diataxis represented the monastic tradition of Mount Athos. Its integration into the patriarchal cathedral liturgy marked the ascendancy of monasticism within the broader Byzantine liturgical tradition. Concurrently, it signifies that the patriarchal chancery not only adopted Philotheos’ Diataxis as an authoritative liturgical standard, but also effected a post-mortem rehabilitation of Philotheos within the patriarchate.
> 
> A distinct portrait of Dimitrios Gemistos emerges from the critical examination of his Diataxis and the differentiation of its sources. He was preeminently a Byzantine churchman, conservative in disposition and staunchly loyal to the Constantinopolitan patriarchate. Gemistos was likewise a career administrator, commencing his thirty-year tenure in the patriarchal chancery as a notary under Philotheos in the 1360s. By the close of his career he attained the high dignity of Mega Sakellarios (*Μέγας Σακελλάριος*), demonstrating distinguished administrative competence. As an experienced official, he possessed complete mastery of patriarchal chancery terminology and an intimate understanding of the bureaucratic hierarchy and traditions of the Great Church. The precision of his knowledge is corroborated by comparative analysis of surviving chancery records. Having served in the patriarchal administration for two decades prior to drafting his Diataxis, his administrative expertise was comprehensive. Gemistos’ institutional loyalty extended personally to the occupants of the patriarchal throne; his incorporation of the non-Constantinopolitan Philothean Diataxis was motivated not by an impulse toward innovation, but by institutional conservatism and reverence for Philotheos Kokkinos.
> 
> Gemistos’ erudition was not restricted to administrative chancery procedure; he was equally versed in the liturgical traditions of the[^597]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 5: Euchologion Fidelity and Hagia Sophia Rites) [Demos, p. 1033 / TD-54]

> Constantinopolitan Patriarchate. For example, he displays complete fluency in the specialized technical vocabulary required to delineate the solemn rites celebrated in Hagia Sophia. Moreover, Gemistos regularly provides marginal scholia containing alternate liturgical formulae, demonstrating extensive familiarity with customary practice. At times, these annotations address the patriarchal cathedral rite, while at other times they specify alternate rubrics for occasions when the Patriarch does not celebrate in person, or when the Liturgy is celebrated outside the Great Church.
> 
> An examination of Gemistos’ other primary source embedded within the Diataxis, the Euchologion (*Εὐχολόγιον*), further demonstrates his fidelity to Constantinopolitan tradition. When Gemistos’ Euchology texts are compared with anterior and contemporary witnesses, it is evident that he remained faithful to received Constantinopolitan Euchology, while adapting his material to the specific liturgical requirements of Hagia Sophia and the patriarchal office. Gemistos’ revisions reflect a conservative effort to clarify and systematize liturgical order rather than introduce novelty. In one remarkable marginal note, he observes that even when customary rites appear superfluous or redundant, it remains far safer that received customs be observed and preserved than threatened with abrogation. Here Gemistos defends the ancient practice of subdeacons intoning the proclamation **"All who are faithful!"** (*Ὅσοι πιστοί*) twice during the Anaphora—a ceremonial action whose original historical rationale had become obscured by his era.
> 
> Thorough analysis of Dimitrios Gemistos’ Diataxis reveals his programmatic intent: to establish an authoritative patriarchal Diataxis codifying the ceremonial roles of the Patriarch and chancery officials during the Divine Liturgy and ordination rites. Comparative analysis with contemporary Constantinopolitan documents—Euchologia, chancery formularies such as the *Ekthesis Nea*, and related Diataxeis—locates Gemistos firmly within the liturgical ethos of Constantinople. This coherence is natural, given Gemistos’ prominent standing within the patriarchal[^598]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 6: Manuscript Transmission and Four Recensions) [Demos, p. 1034 / TD-55]

> chancery and throughout the Byzantine Church. Rather than treating the Diataxis solely as a rubrical manual, examining its historical and ecclesial context provides crucial insights into fourteenth-century Byzantine liturgical life.
> 
> Gemistos’ Diataxis is preserved in nineteen extant manuscripts, spanning from Athos Vatopedi MS 135 (copied in November 1390, shortly after the text’s original composition in August 1386) to Athos Panteleimon MS 677 (AD 1890). These witnesses transmit four primary recensions: (1) the comprehensive archetype, representing the longer Patriarchal recension; (2) an abbreviated archival recension; (3) a subsequent archieratical (episcopal) redaction; and (4) an abbreviated Eucharistic redaction focused exclusively upon the celebration of the Divine Liturgy.
> 
> The original Patriarchal recension delineates the Prothesis rite, the ceremonial vesting of the Patriarch, the preliminary rite of metropolitan election and ordination, the ordination of readers and subdeacons, and the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, interspersed with ordination rites for metropolitans, presbyters, and deacons. For each rite, the complete Euchology prayers and diaconal petitions (*diakonika*) are provided in full, alongside detailed rubrics for chancery officials and specific references to the sacred architecture of Hagia Sophia.
> 
> The editor of the archival recension excised the Euchology prayers and diaconal petitions to economize parchment. The patriarchal and archieratical recensions are closely related in structure and substance; however, the archieratical redaction substitutes the general title *archiereus* (bishop) for *patriarch* and eliminates most topographical references to Hagia Sophia, adapting the Diataxis for diocesan episcopal use. The shorter patriarchal recension is more radical: while retaining references to the Patriarch, Hagia Sophia, and chancery personnel, it omits the Prothesis rubrics and Euchology prayers, incorporating certain liturgical elements not yet standard in pure Constantinopolitan[^599]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 7: Recension Taxonomy and Editorial Method) [Demos, p. 1035 / TD-56]

> tradition, while transposing the ordination rites to a separate section preceding the text of the Diataxis.
> 
> Textual evaluation of Gemistos’ manuscript tradition demonstrates that subsequent recensions arose in response to specific elements of the archetype. The original title explicitly announces that the Diataxis sets forth the order of the Patriarchal Divine Liturgy celebrated in the Great Church (Hagia Sophia) and “both how and when the order of ordinations takes place.” Consequently, authentic rubrics for the patriarchal liturgy and ordination offices constitute indispensable components of Gemistos’ original work. Because these core elements are missing in the Archieratical and Abbreviated Patriarchal “Eucharistic” recensions, both must be classified as secondary redactions.
> 
> The editor of the Archieratical recension adapted the Patriarchal archetype by substituting *archiereus* for *patriarch* and omitting concelebrating hierarchs, Hagia Sophia architectural rubrics, and chancery officials, thereby producing a manual serviceable for any diocesan hierarch. Similarly, the compiler of the Abbreviated Patriarchal “Eucharistic” recension deleted the Euchology prayers and diaconal petitions of Saint John Chrysostom as well as ordination rites, leaving only liturgical rubrics with incipits marking where prayers originally stood.
> 
> The guiding editorial principle of the critical edition is to reconstruct, as faithfully as the witnesses permit, the authentic archetype composed by Dimitrios Gemistos in 1386, eschewing hypothetical composite readings. Every constituent element must be evaluated as an integral part of Gemistos’ coherent patriarchal synthesis, distinguishing this edition from a generic critical edition of Byzantine ordination rites.[^600]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Part 8: Critical Apparatus and Reform Context) [Demos, p. 1036 / TD-57]

> of the Byzantine Church, or of the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom, or of the Philothean Diataxis, but simply a critical edition of the fourteenth-century patriarchal liturgical Diataxis of Dimitrios Gemistos.
> 
> All attempts have been made to provide a clear and straightforward critical apparatus following the normal methods for editing texts. I prepared the critical apparatus with an eye less toward philologists and more toward liturgiologists who are interested not only in the reconstruction of the original text but also its subsequent development. Consequently, the critical apparatus is fuller, and includes more variant readings than might be typical for a critical edition.
> 
> The register of sources allows the reader of the text to distinguish what exactly Gemistos wrote, what he took from his sources, and what the general nature of the sources was.
> 
> The first level of the critical apparatus contains the variant readings that serve to reconstruct the original text of the Diataxis. The second level of apparatus contains the variant readings from the archieratical recension of Gemistos’ Diataxis when these readings do not bear witness to the original text. The third level of apparatus is exclusively reserved for those instances where Habert deviated from the text of his exemplar, Paris Greek 1362. This level also includes the various titles he gave to different sections of the Diataxis. I have attempted to include all of Habert’s variations—all orthographical, all prayer variants, etc., in order to give an accurate report of the nature of his text.
> 
> Reflecting on Gemistos’ work, students of Byzantine liturgy might view Gemistos as an innovator. After all, he introduced the Athonite monastic Diataxis of Philotheos Kokkinos into the patriarchal cathedral rite of Constantinople, thus securing the victory of the monastic rite over the cathedral rite. In so doing, Gemistos unwittingly cemented a process that had been developing for centuries. Yet this view of Gemistos is only partially accurate and gained only in hindsight. Gemistos was only one actor in a drama that had been unfolding for almost a thousand years and involved countless individuals. In fact, the victory of the monastic rite is not a story of a few individuals, but of many. If he did reflect on what he was doing, Gemistos[^601]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diataxis Concluded – Diatonic Part 1) [Demos, p. 1037 / TD-58]

> probably saw himself as someone merely codifying existing traditions and liturgical practices. His view on this would be perfectly akin to another contemporary Byzantine liturgist, Archbishop Symeon of Thessalonika († 1429), who likewise reformed the liturgy. But Alexander Lingas, who has evaluated the content of Symeon’s reforms, concludes that far from being “revolutionary,” the reforms “represented the harmonization and systematization of existing precedents for the inclusion of texts and music from outside the Byzantine cathedral tradition.”[2] Only when the liturgical rites are viewed as static or unchangeable can these developments—or any developments—be considered as mere innovation and not simply the natural continuous evolution of the rites which is the substance of liturgical history.”
> 
> [1] Baumstark, *Comparative Liturgy*, p. 18.  
> [2] A. Lingas, *Sunday Matins in the Byzantine Cathedral Rite: Music and Liturgy*, Unpublished Doctoral Dissertation, University of British Columbia, 1996, p. 218.

**DIATONIC** (*Διατονικόν, τό* / Diatonikon): A diatonic scale is based on an octave divided into five tones (T) and two semitones (S), e.g., T-T-S-T-T-T-S. The major and natural minor scales are diatonic, as are the ecclesiastical modes. A diatonic interval is one found within the diatonic octave (Oxford Grove Music Encyclopedia; see also **SCALE**).

The notes that occur naturally within a scale, without being modified by accidentals other than in the key signature, is another definition from the Music Glossary of the Austin Symphony Orchestra.

A simple understanding is the natural scale or mode consisting of five whole tones and two semitones that can be played on the white keys of a keyboard instrument; e.g., the diatonic scale of Byzantine Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal): c–d–e–f–g–a–b–c.

In music theory, a diatonic scale is a scale whose notes are built on the natural staff positions of lines and spaces, without accidentals, with or without a key signature. These scales are based on concepts developed by Guido d’Arezzo, and are therefore some-[^602]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diatonic Concluded – Dikiria – Dikirotrikiron – Diptycha Part 1) [Demos, p. 1038 / TD-59]

times referred to as Guido scales. Diatonic music is composed primarily using notes from a diatonic scale; similarly, diatonic chords and diatonic intervals employ notes from such a scale. The antonym is chromatic.

Diatonic scales constitute a fundamental foundation of European musical tradition. The term is sometimes applied to all medieval ecclesiastical modes, but is generally restricted to the major and minor scales.

The natural scale consists of five whole tones and two semitones as produced upon the white keys of a keyboard instrument. A corresponding scale exists in each key. Music is termed “diatonic” if it is restricted to the notes of this scale, to the exclusion of chromatic tones. For example, in the key of C major, the melodic progression c–d–e and the chord d–a–c represent diatonic harmony. “Diatonicism” denotes music whose tonality is predominantly diatonic, that is, nonchromatic (see Powers, Harold S. and Jeffery, Peter in the bibliography).

**DIKIRIA** (*Δικήρια, τά*; Slav. *Dvusvechnik*, *Dikirij*): Two-branched candelabras.

**DIKIROTRIKIRON** (*Δικηροτρίκηρον, τό*): The pair of two-branched and three-branched candelabras (*Dikirion* and *Trikirion*), used simultaneously by an officiating hierarch during the Divine Liturgy.

**DIPTYCHA** (*Δίπτυχα, τά*; lit. "a pair of tablets"):

While the hymn **"It Is Truly Right"** (*Ἄξιόν ἐστιν* / Axion Estin) is chanted during the Divine Liturgy, the priest (or deacon) commemorates inaudibly the names of the departed and then the names of the living. In the early Church, these names were inscribed in special books or upon wooden leaves bound together so that they could be opened or folded like codex pages. These registers were termed *diptycha*. This commemoration of names concludes when the priest exhorts the congregation to pray for all those whom they hold in prayer, intoning aloud: **"Remember also, O Lord, those whom each of us calls to mind, and all Thy people"** («Καὶ ὧν ἕκαστος κατὰ διάνοιαν[^603]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Diptycha Concluded – Diskarion – Diskos – Dismissal Hymn – Dismissal Prayer – Divamvulon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1039 / TD-60]

ἔχει, καὶ πάντων καὶ πασῶν.» / "And those whom each one hath in mind, and all men and all women"). This marks the moment in the Divine Liturgy designated “at the Diptycha” when, on festal occasions, the **Megalynarion at the Diptycha** is sung (cf. Bogdanos, *The Byzantine Liturgy: Hymnology and Order*, p. 148).

An alternate definition denotes a two-leaved hinged tablet of metal, ivory, or wood, whose inner surface was covered with wax upon which inscriptions were inscribed with a stylus. Between the two leaves additional leaves were sometimes inserted, giving rise to the designations triptych, polyptych, etc. In the early Church, the names of faithful members, both living and deceased, were inscribed upon diptychs. Saint Cyprian of Carthage mentions them in the third century, and they remained in continuous liturgical use until the twelfth century in the West, and the fourteenth century in the East.

The “diptychs of the living” contained the names of patriarchs, bishops, illustrious persons, both clerical and lay, and ecclesiastical benefactors. From them originated the earliest ecclesiastical calendars and martyrologies. The “diptychs of the dead” contained the names of those who, while living, had been inscribed upon the diptychs of the living; from these arose later necrologies. Removal of a name from these lists constituted a grave ecclesiastical penalty (excommunication). The contents of the diptychs were read aloud from the ambo or altar, and traces of this fifth-century Roman practice survive in the *Memento* of the Canon of the Mass.

**DISKARION** (*Δισκάριον, τό*): See **PATEN** (lit. *Diskarion*).

**DISKOS** (*Δίσκος, ὁ*; Slav. *Diskos*): See **PATEN** (lit. *Diskos*).

**DISMISSAL HYMN** (*᾿Απολυτίκιον*): See **DISMISSAL TROPARION** (lit. *Apolytikion* / *᾿Απολυτίκιον*).

**DISMISSAL PRAYER** (*᾿Απόλυσις*): See **APOLYSIS**.

**DIVAMVULON** (*Διβάμβουλον, τό*): A ceremonial staff carried in solemn processions by a dignitary appointed by the Great Ecclesiarch, who walks before the Patriarch. These officials were customarily archons of the Great Church or other honored ecclesiastical dignitaries. This staff exists in two variants: the *monovamvulon* and the *divamvulon*. The latter designation derives from two bulbous decorative knobs, one[^604]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Divamvulon Concluded – Divine Liturgy – Dogmatikon – Domestikos – Dormition Part 1) [Demos, p. 1040 / TD-61]

at the summit and a second situated a short distance down the shaft. In contemporary practice, these decorative knobs are customarily fashioned of silver.

**DIVINE LITURGY** (*Θεία Λειτουργία, ἡ*): See **LITURGY**.

**DOGMATIKON** (*Δογματικόν, τό*, pl. *Δογματικά, τά* / Dogmatika): These are the festal *Theotokia* chanted in the Eight Tones that conclude the stichira at **"Lord, I have cried"** (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*) at Great Vespers on Saturday evenings. Their designation derives from the fact that they present concise, theological expositions of the dogma of the Incarnation, with special emphasis upon the perpetual virginity and divine maternity of the Most Holy Theotokos.

**DOMESTIKOS** (*Δομέστικος, ὁ*, pl. *Δομέστικοι, οἱ* / Domestikoi): In contemporary practice, as in antiquity, two ranks of Domestikoi minister in the Patriarchal Church of Saint George at the Phanar. The junior rank is that of Second Domestikos, which constitutes the initial grade within the order of cantors. The next rank in ascending order is First Domestikos, which is followed by the two principal choir leaders: the **Lambadarios** (*Λαμπαδάριος*, lead chanter directing the left choir at the analogion near the left end of the soleas) and the **Protopsaltis** (*Πρωτοψάλτης*, archcantor directing the right choir at the analogion near the right end of the soleas). The office of Second Domestikos is customarily assigned to younger singers noted for superior vocal agility and stamina.

**DORMITION** (*Κοίμησις, ἡ*; lit. "falling asleep"): Used figuratively for death, specifically designating the repose of a sacred personage, preeminently the **Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos** (August 15). Both the Eastern Orthodox and Catholic Churches profess that, following her sacred death, the Virgin Mary was assumed bodily into heaven. By the apostolic constitution *Munificentissimus Deus* of Pope Pius XII in 1950, the dogma of the bodily Assumption of the Virgin Mary was defined for the Catholic Church. In the Eastern tradition, preserved throughout patristic hymnography and the liturgical Typikon, the feast celebrates the bodily translation of the Mother of God to heaven after her death, with the Western Church customarily employing the title *Assumption* rather than[^605]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Dormition Concluded – Doxa ke Nyn – Doxastikon – Doxology Part 1) [Demos, p. 1041 / TD-62]

*Dormition*. See also **ASSUMPTION**.

**DOXA** (*Δόξα*) **KAI NYN** (*Καὶ νῦν*): Standard liturgical abbreviations indicating the chanting of the minor doxology preceding appointed prayers and hymns in all ecclesiastical liturgical books (Sluzhebnyky and Horologia). The first formula abbreviates: **"Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit"** («Δόξα Πατρὶ καὶ Υἱῷ καὶ Ἁγίῳ Πνεύματι»); the second formula abbreviates: **"Now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** («Καὶ νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν»). See also **DOXASTIKON** below.

**DOXASTIKON** (*Δοξαστικόν, τό*, pl. *Δοξαστικά, τά*; Slav. *Slavnik*): A stichiron hymn sung after the verse **"Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit"** (*Δόξα Πατρὶ καὶ Υἱῷ καὶ Ἁγίῳ Πνεύματι*) at the conclusion of a series of stichira at Vespers or Matins. It expounds in an extended, melismatic melody the sacred feast or saint commemorated on that day. Following the Doxastikon, a matching Theotokion is appointed with the verse: **"Now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** («Καὶ νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν»). Doxastika are chanted principally at the conclusion of the Lamplighting Sticheron verses (*Esperia*) and the Aposticha at Vespers, and following the Praises (*Ainoi*) at Matins (*Orthros*).

In the tradition of medieval Slavic chant, the Doxastika of major feast days were distinguished by being set to solemn, ornate melodies (*demestvenny*, *put’*, or *great znamenny* chant) or to early polyphony.
