**DOXOLOGY** (*Δοξολογία, ἡ*; lit. "a hymn of glorification"; Slav. *Slavoslovie*): A structured series of scriptural and hymnographic verses glorifying God, opening with the angelic proclamation: **"Glory to Thee, Who hast shown us the light"** («Δόξα σοι τῷ δείξαντι τὸ φῶς»), chanted toward the end of Matins (*Orthros*) before the Divine Liturgy.[^606]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Doxology Concluded – Eagle – Easter – Ecclesiastical Year – Efchologion to Mega – Ekklisia – Ekphonisis Part 1) [Demos, p. 1042 / TD-63]

This ancient hymn of praise is common to both East and West. In the Byzantine rite, it possesses two forms: the Great Doxology and the Lesser Doxology. The Great Doxology is sung solemnly at Matins on Sundays and Feasts; the Lesser Doxology is read at daily Matins and at Compline. Like the Latin *Te Deum*, the hymn concludes with a series of versicles drawn from the Psalter. The Great Doxology terminates with the singing of the Trisagion (*"Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, have mercy on us"*). The Lesser Doxology concludes with the prayer **"Vouchsafe, O Lord, to keep us this day without sin"**, which, at both daily Vespers and non-festal Matins, precedes the prayer of the bowing of heads by the presbyter. The designation *doxology* also applies to shorter ascriptions of glory to the Holy Trinity. See **DOXASTIKON** and **EKPHONISIS**.

**EAGLE** (*᾿Αετός, ὁ*): See **AETOS**.

**EASTER**: See **PASCHA**.

**ECCLESIASTICAL YEAR** (*᾿Εκκλησιαστικὸν Ἔτος, τό*; Church Calendar): The liturgical cycle established by the Byzantine Church in the late fourth century, commencing on September 1 and concluding on August 31.

**EFCHOLOGION TO MEGA** (*Εὐχολόγιον τὸ Μέγα* / Great Euchologion; Slav. *Trebnik* and *Sluzhebnik*; lit. "Great Book of Prayers"): The Slavonic term *Trebnik* signifies "Book of Needs" (*Treby*). The comprehensive Euchologion contains the sacerdotal prayers for the priest and deacon, and petitions for the reader at Vespers, Matins (*Orthros*), and the Divine Liturgies (the rites comprising the *Sluzhebnik* or *Liturgikon*), as well as the Holy Mysteries (Sacraments) and sacramental blessings for various pastoral occasions. An abbreviated sacerdotal edition containing the daily offices and Liturgies is termed the *Small Euchologion* (*Μικρὸν Εὐχολόγιον*) or *Sluzhebnik*.

**EKKLISIA** (*᾿Εκκλησία, ἡ*): The church edifice or temple. See **NAOS**.

**EKPHONISIS** (*᾿Εκφώνησις, ἡ*; Slav. *Vozglas*): A liturgical exclamation intoned aloud in a solemn, declamatory chant style. See also **EXCLAMATION**. Applied preeminently to the concluding trinitarian doxology that terminates silent priestly prayers, it is intoned aloud by the priest even when the preceding prayer is directed to be read secretly (*mystikos* / *μυστικῶς*).[^607]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Ekphonisis Concluded – Ektenis – Engolpion – Entrance – Enos / Eni Part 1) [Demos, p. 1043 / TD-64]

Traditional melodic formulas for *ekphonisis* were recorded in medieval manuscripts through a specialized system of ekphonetic neumes, and were likewise transmitted through oral cantoral tradition.

In melodic contour, *ekphonisis* occupies an intermediate position between simple psalmody—wherein inflections from the reciting tone (*ison* or *basis*) occur only at the beginnings and cadences of phrases—and ornate hymnody, which possesses defined melodic and rhythmic forms. In the Divine Liturgy, *ekphonisis* is utilized for the chanting of the Holy Gospel, Epistle, and Old Testament readings, as well as for the sacerdotal exclamations intoned by the celebrating priest or deacon.

**EKTENIS** (*᾿Εκτενής, ἡ*; lit. "fervent / extended"): See **LITANY**.

**ENGOLPION** (*᾿Εγκόλπιον, τό*; lit. "that which is worn on the breast"; Slav. *Panagia*): In early Western usage, the designation encompassed pectoral crosses; in the contemporary Eastern Church, it denotes an oval medallion bearing the sacred icon of the Theotokos (hence *Panagia*) or Christ the Savior, suspended around the neck by a decorative chain over the chest. It constitutes part of the pontifical vesture of an Eastern hierarch and is worn over the omophorion. When vesting with the Engolpion, the Bishop prays: **"Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me"** («Καρδίαν καθαρὰν κτίσον ἐν ἐμοί, ὁ Θεός, καὶ πνεῦμα εὐθὲς ἐγκαίνισον ἐν τοῖς ἐγκάτοις μου»—Psalm 50:12 [LXX 50:12 / MT 51:10]). See also **VESTMENTS**. The term *Engolpion* also designates a handy liturgical manual, such as the Reader's Handbook (*᾿Εγκόλπιον τοῦ ᾿Αναγνώστου*).

**ENTRANCE** (*Εἴσοδος, ἡ*; Slav. *Vkhod*): See **GREAT ENTRANCE** and **SMALL ENTRANCE**.

**ENOS, pl. ENI** (*Αἶνος, ὁ*, pl. *Αἶνοι, οἱ*; Slav. *Khvalitny*; lit. "Praise / Praises"; Western *Lauds*): The *Eni* comprise the final three psalms of the Septuagint Psalter (Psalms 148, 149, and 150), designated the "Psalms of Praise" because their verses open with the acclamation: **"Praise the Lord"** (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον* / Slav. *Khvalite Gospoda*). In monasteries, all verses of the three psalms are chanted in full; in parish practice, the initial verses are chanted, after which the choir proceeds directly to the concluding verses appointed for the insertion of the[^608]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Eni Concluded – Eothinon – Epanokalymafchon – Epigonation Part 1) [Demos, p. 1044 / TD-65]

Hymns of Praise (*Τροπάρια τῶν Αἴνων* / Stichira at the Praises). In Byzantine usage, these are chanted during Matins (*Orthros*) preceding the Great Doxology and the Divine Liturgy, immediately following the Exapostilarion.

**EOTHINON** (*῾Εωθινόν, τό*, pl. *῾Εωθινά, τά*; lit. "pertaining to the dawn"): Derived from the Greek *heos* ("dawn"). The term designates either: (a) one of the eleven Resurrectional Matins Gospels (*Eothina Evangelia*) read in cyclical order on Sundays at Matins; or (b) the eleven Idiomela Doxastika composed by Emperor Leo VI the Wise (reigned 866–912), chanted on Sundays at the conclusion of the Praises (*Ainoi* / *Lauds*) at the verse **"Glory to the Father..."**. Each idiomelon corresponds poetically to one of the eleven Matins Gospels, providing a poetic synthesis of the Resurrection appearance. In the ancient Constantinopolitan cathedral ("Asmatic" or "Sung") office described by Saint Symeon of Thessalonika († 1429), these hymns were chanted immediately preceding the reading of the Resurrection Gospel at Matins.

**EPANOKALYMAFCHON** (*᾿Επανωκαλύμαυχον, τό*; Slav. *Klobuk* or *Namitka*): A flowing black veil worn over the kamilavka (*Kalimafchion*) by bishops, archimandrites, and monastics. The veil attaches to the crown of the cylindrical black headdress. Secular priests and lower clergy wear the kamilavka without a veil.

**EPIGONATION** (*᾿Επιγονάτιον, τό*; Slav. *Palitsa*; cf. *Nabedrennik*): A liturgical vestment awarded as an ecclesiastical distinction. The term derives from the Greek *epi gonatos* ("upon the knee"). It consists of a stiff, diamond-shaped (rhomboid) fabric suspended from one corner by a ribbon worn over the left shoulder, hanging at the right knee below the waist. In the Greek tradition, it signifies that a presbyter holds advanced theological degrees and possesses the episcopal blessing to serve as a spiritual father (*Pnevmatikos*) and hear confessions. In the Slavic tradition, it is awarded as a liturgical honor for distinguished service; when a presbyter is awarded both the oblong *nabedrennik* (thigh-shield) and the rhomboid *palitsa* (*epigonation*), he wears the *palitsa* on the right hip and shifts the *nabedrennik* to the left. Liturgically, the Epigonation symbolizes the spiritual sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God (cf. Ephesians 6:17).

### Historical Origin

Its historical genesis remains subject to scholarly discussion. According to certain authorities, it derived from a ceremonial decorative thigh-shield awarded to high civil and military dignitaries in the Byzantine Empire. According to others, it originated as an ornamental handkerchief designated the *encheirion* (*ἐγχείριον*, or[^609]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Epigonation Concluded – Epiklisis – Epilychnii Psalmi – Epimanikia Part 1) [Demos, p. 1045 / TD-66]

"hand-cloth"), which attained its standardized liturgical form and designation during the twelfth century. It was also utilized historically to carry formal patriarchal credentials and letters of orders certifying the cleric's ordination and status when traveling. Carrying these documents during the liturgical synaxis symbolized his canonical authority to celebrate the Holy Mysteries.

When vesting with the Epigonation, the Priest prays: **"Gird Thy sword upon Thy thigh, O Mighty One, in Thy splendor and in Thy beauty. Go forth, proceed prosperously, and reign, for the sake of truth, meekness, and righteousness; and Thy right hand shall guide Thee marvelously (Psalm 44:4–5 [LXX 44:4–5 / MT 45:3–4]); always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** («Περίζωσαι τὴν ῥομφαίαν Σου ἐπὶ τὸν μηρόν Σου, δυνατέ, τῇ ὡραιότητί Σου καὶ τῷ κάλλει Σου· καὶ ἔντεινε, καὶ κατευοδοῦ, καὶ βασίλευε, ἕνεκεν ἀληθείας, καὶ πραότητος, καὶ δικαιοσύνης· καὶ ὁδηγήσει Σε θαυμαστῶς ἡ δεξιά Σου [Ψαλμ. 44:4–5 / MT 45:3–4]. Πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.»)

**EPIKLISIS** (*᾿Επίκλησις, ἡ*; lit. "invocation" or "calling down from above"): The solemn section of the Anaphora (Eucharistic Prayer) in which the celebrating bishop or priest invokes the Holy Spirit upon the gifts of bread and wine, that He may change them into the precious Body and Blood of Christ. In the Byzantine rite, the Epiklesis follows the Words of Institution and the Anamnesis (*remembrance*) of the Savior’s saving passion, cross, tomb, and resurrection.

**EPILYCHNII PSALMI** (*᾿Επιλύχνιοι Ψαλμοί*): See **LAMPLIGHTING PSALMS**.

**EPIMANIKIA** (*᾿Επιμανίκια, τά*; Slav. *Poruchi* or *Narukavnytsi*): Sacerdotal cuffs laced securely around the wrists and forearms of deacons, presbyters, and hierarchs. The deacon wears them over the sleeves of his inner cassock, beneath the wide sleeves of the *sticharion*; priests and bishops wear them over the cuffs of the *sticharion* / *podryasnik*. Mystically, they signify spiritual strength, patience, and divine power, recalling the cords that bound the sacred hands of our Savior Jesus Christ during His holy Passion.

When vesting with the right cuff, the Priest prays: **"Thy right hand, O Lord, is glorified in power; Thy right hand, O Lord, hath shattered the enemies. And in the multitude of Thy glory Thou hast crushed the adversaries"** (cf. Exodus 15:6–7).[^610]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Epimanikia Concluded – Episkopos – Epitafios – Epitrachilion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1046 / TD-67]

> throw Thy adversaries (Exodus 15:6–7 [LXX 15:6–7]); always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.” («῾Η δεξιά Σου, Κύριε, δεδόξασται ἐν ἰσχύϊ· ἡ δεξιά Σου χείρ, Κύριε, ἔθραυσεν ἐχθρούς· καὶ τῷ πλήθει τῆς δόξης Σου συνέτριψας τοὺς ὑπεναντίους» [᾿Εξόδου 15:6–7]). When vesting with the left cuff, he prays: “Thy hands have made me and fashioned me; give me understanding that I may learn Thy commandments (Psalm 118:73 [LXX 118:73 / MT 119:73]); always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.” («Αἱ χεῖρές Σου ἐποίησάν με καὶ ἔπλασάν με· συνέτισόν με καὶ μαθήσομαι τὰς ἐντολάς Σου» [Ψαλμ. 118:73 / MT 119:73]. Πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.)

**EPISKOPOS** (*᾿Επίσκοπος, ὁ*): See **BISHOP**.

**EPITAFIOS** (*᾿Επιτάφιος, ὁ*; Slav. *Plashchanytsia*): A sacred liturgical cloth icon embroidered or painted with the icon of Christ in the tomb, carried in procession on Great and Holy Friday and deposited upon the Holy Tomb or Tetrapod. See also **KUVUKLION** and **PLASHCHANYTSIA**.

**EPITRACHILION** (*᾿Επιτραχήλιον, τό*; Slav. *Epitrakhil*, *Obvyjnik*): An Eastern liturgical vestment derived from the two Greek words for upon (*epi*) and neck (*trachilos*); functionally equivalent to the Western stole. The principal morphological distinction is that the Epitrachilion is joined together down the front seam, either sewn continuously or fastened by a series of ornamental buttons. It is also broader than the Western stole. It is the indispensable sacerdotal vestment, worn by bishops and priests; a presbyter does not celebrate any divine service or sacred mystery without donning the epitrachilion.

When vesting with the Epitrachilion, the Priest prays: **“Blessed is God Who poureth His grace upon His priests, as ointment upon the head, that runneth down upon the beard, the beard of Aaron, running down to the fringe of his garment (Psalm 132:2 [LXX 132:2 / MT 133:2]); always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** («Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεός, ὁ ἐκχέων τὴν χάριν αὐτοῦ ἐπὶ τοὺς ἱερεῖς αὐτοῦ· ὡς μύρον ἐπὶ κεφαλῆς, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ πώγωνα, τὸν πώγωνα τοῦ ᾿Ααρών, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ τὴν ᾤαν τοῦ ἐνδύματος αὐτοῦ» [Ψαλμ. 132:2 / MT 133:2]. Πάντοτε νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.)[^611]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Epitrachilion Concluded – Equal of the Apostles – Etisis – Evangelion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1047 / TD-68]

The Epitrachilion may be understood historically as the deacon's **Orarion** (*᾿Ωράριον*), looped around the neck and adapted for priestly and episcopal ministry. It is draped around the neck with both ends hanging down equally in front to the lower hem of the sticharion, the two adjoining inner edges being sewn or buttoned together down the midline, leaving an aperture at the upper loop through which the head passes. In established liturgical practice it remains permanently joined. It is customarily fashioned of rich brocade or damask and adorned with seven embroidered crosses: one situated behind the neck, and three descending down each side.

The priest wears the epitrachilion whenever performing any sacerdotal function (as distinguished from merely being present in choir). For lesser offices and treby (e.g., Vespers, Matins, or molebens without full vesting), he dons the epitrachilion over his cassock. When fully vested for the Divine Liturgy, he wears the epitrachilion over the **Sticharion** (*Στιχάριον*) and beneath the **Zone** (*Ζώνη*) and **Felonion** (*Φελόνιον*).

If a priest is present in choir without celebrating, he remains unvested in his cassock, but dons his epitrachilion (and customarily his **Epimanikia** [*᾿Επιμανίκια*]) before approaching to receive the Holy Mysteries.

**EQUAL OF THE APOSTLES** (*᾿Ισαπόστολος, ὁ / ἡ*): A hagiographical title bestowed upon a saint whose missionary labors, martyrdom, or defense of the Orthodox faith elevated them to a stature and dignity comparable to that of the Holy Apostles (e.g., Saint Mary Magdalene, July 22; Saints Constantine and Helen, May 21; Saint Vladimir the Great, July 15).

**ETISIS** (*Αἴτησις, ἡ*): A liturgical petition or request addressed to God by the deacon or priest within an ektenis or litany of supplication.

**EVANGELION** (*Εὐαγγέλιον, τό*; Slav. *Evangeliye*): The liturgical Book of the Holy Gospels containing the inspired accounts written by the Four Evangelists and disciples of Christ: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John. (Consequently, the complete Gospel codex is designated the **Tetrevangelion** [*Τετραευαγγέλιον*].) This sacred volume, decorated with metal plaques or enamels depicting the Resurrection and the Four Evangelists, rests permanently upon the Holy Table, customarily enclosed within a precious gilded metal binding, since ancient tradition discouraged placing bare leather bindings fashioned from animal skins upon the consecrated altar table. The text is arranged for liturgical proclamation by excerpts designated pericopes (lit. "cuttings around", *περικοπαί*), assigned to each day and[^612]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Evangelion Concluded – Evlogitaria – Evlogitos – Evresis Part 1) [Demos, p. 1048 / TD-69]

feast throughout the liturgical calendar.

**EVLOGITARIA** (*Εὐλογητάρια, τά*; Slav. *Neporochny*): See **BENEDICTIONS**.

**EVLOGITOS** (*Εὐλογητός*): An abbreviation designating the initial blessing and sacerdotal exclamation that opens the majority of liturgical services: **“Blessed is our God always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** («Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.») This exclamation commences Vespers, the Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon*) on Sundays, and, in the absence of a concurring feast, the services during the seasons of the Triodion and Pentecostarion when coinciding with a Feast of the Mother of God or a ranked Saint. At Small Vespers following the prayer of the Ninth Hour, no dismissal is made, and Vespers at that point begins without the *Evlogitos*. The exclamation is likewise intoned at Sunday Matins (*Orthros*) and at every other divine service, with three principal statutory exceptions: (a) the Divine Liturgy, (b) the Mystery of Holy Baptism, and (c) the Mystery of Holy Crowning (Matrimony). In these three mysteries, the opening exclamation is: **“Blessed is the kingdom of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** («Εὐλογημένη ἡ βασιλεία τοῦ Πατρὸς καὶ τοῦ Υἱοῦ καὶ τοῦ ῾Αγίου Πνεύματος, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.») Furthermore, at Vespers and Matins during Bright Week (Renewal Week), as well as on the **Apodosis** (lit. "Leave-taking", *᾿Απόδοσις*) of Pascha (Easter), and likewise prior to each daily recitation of the **Six Psalms** (*῾Εξάψαλμος* / Exapsalmos), the opening exclamation is: **“Glory to the holy, consubstantial, life-giving, and undivided Trinity, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** («Δόξα τῇ ῾Αγίᾳ καὶ ῾Ομοουσίῳ καὶ ζωοποιῷ καὶ ἀδιαιρέτῳ Τριάδι· πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.») [See our translation of the *Protheoria*, pp. 4–5, and the *Typikon*, p. 4; also the *Synekdimos*, p. 246.] Note that Morning Prayer or Matins (*Orthros*) commences immediately following the dismissal of the Midnight Office (*Mesonyktikon* / *Μεσονυκτικόν*).

**EVRESIS** (*Εὕρεσις, ἡ*): The finding or recovery, usually of the[^613]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Evresis Concluded – Exapostilarion – Exapsalmos – Exapteryga – Exclamation Part 1) [Demos, p. 1049 / TD-70]

sacred remains or relics of Saints or Martyrs. This may include an initial discovery or several successive findings.

**EXAPOSTILARION, pl. EXAPOSTILARIA** (*᾿Εξαποστειλάριον, τό· ἐξαποστειλάρια, τά*; Slav. lit. *Svetilen*; lit. "Hymn of Light"): In Greek the term signifies "that which is sent forth". These are one or two proper hymns chanted before the **Praises** (*Αἶνοι* / Eni) toward the conclusion of Matins (*Orthros*). On ordinary Sundays, there are eleven Resurrectional Exapostilaria composed by the son of Emperor Leo the Wise, Constantine VII Porphyrogenitus (reigned 912–959). Each of these Exapostilaria corresponds poetically to one of the eleven Resurrection Gospels, the overarching theme of which is the radiant Resurrection of Christ and the events surrounding it.

On major feasts, the Exapostilarion refers directly to the sacred festal event or holy personage commemorated on that day.

In summary, there exist three classes of Exapostilaria: (1) Resurrectional, which follow the elevenfold cycle of Sunday Resurrection Gospels and Gospel stichira; (2) Festal, which pertain to the specific feast being celebrated; and (3) Trinitarian (*Triadika*), which are chanted at certain Lenten services in honor of the Holy Trinity.

**EXAPSALMOS** (*῾Εξάψαλμος, ὁ*; alternate spelling: *Hexapsalmos*; Slav. *Shestopsalmiye*; lit. "Six Psalms" [Psalms 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, and 142; LXX 3, 37, 62, 87, 102, 142 / MT 3, 38, 63, 88, 103, 143]): This fixed sequence of six psalms is read near the beginning of Morning Prayer or Matins (*Orthros*).

**EXAPTERYGA** (*῾Εξαπτέρυγα, τά*): The liturgical processional fans representing the celestial choir of Seraphim, derived from the Greek meaning literally "six-winged". These fans are metallic disks embossed with images of the six-winged Seraphim, mounted on shafts and carried by acolytes during solemn liturgical processions. When not carried in procession, these shafts stand in sockets behind the Holy Table on either side of the central processional Cross. In Greek, the fan is also designated a **Ripidion** (*ῥιπίδιον, τό*), and in Latin, a *flabellum*.

**EXCLAMATION** (*᾿Εκφώνησις, ἡ*; Slav. *Vozglas*): See also **EKPHONISIS**.[^614]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Exclamation Concluded – Exeretos – Exigisis – Exigitis – Exigitikos – Exorason – Fast – Feasts of the Lord Part 1) [Demos, p. 1050 / TD-71]

A liturgical prayer intoned aloud in an elevated, declamatory chant by a priest or bishop at the conclusion of a longer sacerdotal prayer recited in a lower voice (*mystikos*).

**EXERETOS** (*᾿Εξαιρέτως*; lit. "especially" or "above all"): The initial word of the great sacerdotal exclamation commemorating the Mother of God during the Anaphora of the Divine Liturgy: **“Especially our most holy, spotless, most blessed and glorious Lady, the Theotokos and ever-virgin Mary.”** («᾿Εξαιρέτως τῆς παναγίας, ἀχράντου, ὑπερευλογημένης, ἐνδόξου, Δεσποίνης ἡμῶν Θεοτόκου, καὶ ἀειπαρθένου Μαρίας.») In the Divine Liturgy, it is immediately followed by the singing of the hymn designated the **Megalynarion** (*Μεγαλυνάριον* / Slav. *Zadostoinyk*). The customary hymn sung at this point is **“It Is Truly Right”** (*῎Αξιόν ἐστιν ὡς ἀληθῶς...* / *Axion Estin*). This hymn is replaced on Great Feasts by the proper festal Megalynarion (the Heirmos of the Ninth Ode with its refrain) appointed for the feast or Saint of the day, as instructed in the *Protheoria* of the *Typikon*. These festal hymns are also compiled in a collection at the conclusion of the *Handbook of the Church Reader* (*῾Εγκόλπιον τοῦ ᾿Αναγνώστου*) or *Holy Services of Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy* (Thirteenth edition, 1992, Athens, Greece, published by Apostoliki Diakonia of the Church of Greece).

**EXIGISIS** (*᾿Εξήγησις, ἡ*): Commentary, interpretation, explanation; exegesis.

**EXIGITIS** (*᾿Εξηγητής, ὁ*): Interpreter, commentator; exegete.

**EXIGITIKOS, -I, -ON** (*᾿Εξηγητικός, -ή, -όν*): Explanatory, expository; exegetical.

**EXORASON** (*᾿Εξώρασον, τό*): See **VESTMENTS**.

**FAST** (*Νηστεία, ἡ*): Abstaining from certain foods; likewise, the sacred season or period of time during which the faithful abstain from prescribed foods. See also **ABSTINENCE**.

**FEASTS OF THE LORD** (*Δεσποτικαὶ ἑορταί*):

1. The Universal Exaltation of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross (14 September).
2. The Nativity according to the Flesh of our Lord, God, and Savior Jesus Christ (25 December).
3. The Holy Theophany of our Lord and Savior Jesus Christ (6 January).
4. The Meeting of our Lord, God, and Savior Jesus Christ in the Temple (2 February) is regarded as both a Feast of The Lord and of the Theotokos.[^615]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Feasts of the Lord Concluded – Feasts of the Theotokos – Felonion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1051 / TD-72]

regarded as both a Feast of The Lord and of the Theotokos.
5. Palm Sunday (the Sunday of Palms, one week before Pascha).
6. Holy and Great Pascha (*Πάσχα* / Easter / the Radiant Resurrection of Christ).
7. The Ascension of our Lord Jesus Christ (forty days after Pascha).
8. Holy Pentecost (fifty days after Pascha).
9. The Holy Transfiguration of our Lord, God, and Savior Jesus Christ (6 August).

**FEASTS OF THE THEOTOKOS** (*Θεομητορικαὶ ἑορταί*):

1. The Nativity of the Most Holy Theotokos (8 September).
2. The Entry of the Most Holy Theotokos into the Temple (21 November).
3. The Meeting of the Lord in the Temple (2 February), regarded jointly as a Feast of the Theotokos and of the Lord.
4. The Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos (25 March).
5. The Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos (15 August).

**FELONION, pl. FELONIA** (*Φελόνιον, τό· pl. Φελόνια, τά*; Slav. *Phelon*, *Ryza*): See also **VESTMENTS**. In contemporary practice, the Felonion is the principal sacerdotal vestment worn by a priest in the Eastern Church. It is worn over the priest’s other vestments (*sticharion*, *epitrachilion*, and *zone*) and corresponds functionally to the Western chasuble. The Felonion is an ample, conical or circular vestment with an opening in the center for the celebrant’s head. When donned, it reaches to the wearer’s wrists in front, so that when the celebrant holds both arms extended, the vestment forms a semicircle when viewed from front or back. In its developed Byzantine form, the front is deeply cut away (from approximately the waist downward) to facilitate the unimpeded movement of the priest’s hands during liturgical ministrations. Rubrics occasionally direct the priest to “lower the Felonion”, that is, to let it fall over his hands at the Dismissal (*Apolysis*), indicating that the divine service is completed. Slavic *felonia* frequently feature a stiffened collar rising behind the neck, and occasionally a row of small buttons across the chest so that the front of the vestment can be raised or lowered. The Felonion descends historically from the Greco-Roman *paenula*, an outer protective garment worn as a cloak in the early centuries.

Two principal stylistic varieties of Felonion predominate in the Christian East: Greek *felonia*, which are tailored to fit softly over the shoulders without a raised collar; and Slavic *felonia*, which feature a high, stiffened collar that rises behind the back of the head.

There is likewise a shortened variety of Felonion (the small *phelonion*) worn by a[^616]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Felonion Concluded – Festal Precedence – Finding – Forefeast – Fotagogikon – Gerontika Part 1) [Demos, p. 1052 / TD-73]

reader at his solemn tonsuring by a bishop.

When vesting with the Felonion, the Priest prays: **“Thy priests, O Lord, shall be clothed with righteousness, and Thy righteous ones shall rejoice with joy (Psalm 131:9 [LXX 131:9 / MT 132:9]); always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** («Οἱ ἱερεῖς Σου, Κύριε, ἐνδύσονται δικαιοσύνην, καὶ οἱ ὅσιοί Σου ἀγαλλιάσει ἀγαλλιάσονται [Ψαλμ. 131:9 / MT 132:9]. Πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.»)

A bishop who celebrates the Divine Liturgy as a simple priest (that is, without the solemn pontifical rites and prayers of the Hierarchical Divine Liturgy) vests in a Felonion instead of his **Sakkos** (*Σάκκος*), wearing the small **Omophorion** (*᾿Ωμοφόριον*) draped around his neck.

**FESTAL PRECEDENCE**: When two feasts coincide on the same day, one takes precedence over the other by reason of its higher liturgical rank (e.g., a Feast of the Lord takes precedence over a Saint’s Feast). This precedence determines the hierarchical order of hymnody: the hymns of the dominant feast precede the hymns of the lesser feast within each liturgical section (e.g., at the **Praises** [*Αἶνοι*] or among the **Dismissal Troparia** [*᾿Απολυτίκια* / lit. *Apolytikia*] chanted at the Small Entrance of the Divine Liturgy).

**FINDING** (*Εὕρεσις, ἡ*): The discovery or recovery of the sacred relics or remains of a holy personage, either initially or on subsequent occasions (e.g., the First and Second Finding of the Precious Head of Saint John the Baptist and Forerunner, February 24).

**FOREFEAST(S)** (*Προεόρτια, τά*; Slav. *Predprazdnestvo*): See **PROEORTIA** or **FOREFEAST** (lit. *Pre-feast*).

**FOTAGOGIKON** (*Φωταγωγικόν, τό, pl. Φωταγωγικά, τά*; lit. "illuminating hymn"): A hymn or cycle of hymns chanted in place of the Exapostilaria at Matins (*Orthros*) on the fasting weekdays of Great Lent. These hymns are arranged according to the Eight Tones and printed at the conclusion of the *Paraklitiki* (*Octoechos*).

**GERONTIKA** (*Γεροντικά, τά*): A Greek liturgical term designating those[^617]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Gerontika Concluded – Gospels, Book of the – Great Archdeacon – Great Archimandrite – Great Chancellor – Great Dean – Great Ecclesiarch – Great Ektenis – Great Entrance Part 1) [Demos, p. 1053 / TD-74]

parts of the divine office which are traditionally read by the Superior or Elder (*Γέρων*, *Γέροντας* / *Hegumen*). When the Superior is absent, those appointed parts are read by the senior monk present in choir.

A visiting priest or distinguished ecclesiastical guest is frequently invited by the community to read the *Gerontika*. At festal Matins, the *Gerontika* include the **Six Psalms** (*῾Εξάψαλμος*), Psalm 50 (Psalm 50 [LXX 50 / MT 51]), and the concluding Prayer of the First Hour.

**GOSPELS, BOOK OF THE** (*Εὐαγγέλιον, τό*): See **EVANGELION**.

**GREAT ARCHDEACON** (*Μέγας ᾿Αρχιδιάκονος, ὁ*): The title of the ranking senior deacon ministering in the Patriarchal Church of Constantinople.

**GREAT ARCHIMANDRITE** (*Μέγας ᾿Αρχιμανδρίτης, ὁ*): The title of the ranking senior archimandrite serving in the Patriarchal Church.

**GREAT CHANCELLOR** (*Μέγας Πρωτοσύγκελλος, ὁ*): See **MEGAS PROTOSYNGELLOS**.

**GREAT DEAN** (*Μέγας Πριμικήριος, ὁ*): See **MEGAS PRIMIKYRIOS**.

**GREAT ECCLESIARCH** (*Μέγας ᾿Εκκλησιάρχης, ὁ*): The chief sacristan and liturgical master of ceremonies, historically presiding over the cathedral order of the Great Church of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople or a major stauropegial monastery.

**GREAT EKTENIS** (*Μεγάλη ᾿Εκτενής, ἡ*; lit. from *ἐκτείνω*, "to stretch out" or "extend"): A long series of liturgical petitions recited by the deacon or priest at the beginning of the Divine Liturgy and other services. The choir or chanter responds to each petition with **“Lord, have mercy”** («Κύριε, ἐλέησον»).

**GREAT ENTRANCE** (*Μεγάλη Εἴσοδος, ἡ*): The solemn offertory procession in which the clergy, preceded by acolytes and candle-bearers, carry the bread and wine prepared for the Holy Eucharist on the **Paten** or **Diskarion**[^618]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Great Entrance Concluded – Great Ochtoichos – Great Protosyngellos – Great Synapti – Hades – Heirmologic – Hexapsalmos – Hierarch – Holy Altar) [Demos, p. 1054 / TD-75]

(*δισκάριον*, lit. "small tray") and in the chalice—both covered with veils and united under the Great Veil (**Aer**)—from the table of the **Prothesis** (*Πρόθεσις*). They exit the **Sanctuary** through the north door, moving to the center of the **Soleas**, where they pause to recite commemorative prayers. They re-enter the Sanctuary through the central Holy Doors (the Beautiful Gate), and lay the paten and the chalice upon the **Holy Table** (*῾Αγία Τράπεζα*) or **Altar**. This procession takes place during the chanting of the Cherubic Hymn (*Χερουβικόν*) in the Divine Liturgy. When a bishop is officiating, the ritual movements and prayers of the procession expand in length and solemnity.

**GREAT OCHTOICHOS** (*Μεγάλη ᾿Οκτώηχος, ἡ*): See **PARAKLITIKI**.

**GREAT PROTOSYNGELLOS** (*Μέγας Πρωτοσύγκελλος, ὁ*): The Great Chancellor. The Byzantine ecclesiastical dignitary charged with administrative oversight and empowered to preside over liturgical functions in the Patriarch’s absence.

**GREAT SYNAPTI** (*Μεγάλη Συναπτή, ἡ*; lit. from the Greek verb *συνάπτω*, "to gather together" or "to bind"): Essentially identical to the **Great Ektenis**. The Great Synapti, however, may appear within a special service (e.g., the Kneeling Service of Pentecost Vespers) with special petitions adapted to that particular rite and its liturgical function.

**HADES** (*῞Αδης, ὁ*; Slav. *Ad*): The netherworld, the realm of the dead, or hell; historically used in ancient Greece for the abode where souls reside after death, and in Christian theology for the realm despoiled by Christ in His Resurrection.

**HEIRMOLOGIC** (Alternate Greek / Slavonic spelling: *Heirmologic*; lit. *Irmologic*): See **MODE**.

**HEXAPSALMOS** (*῾Εξάψαλμος, ὁ*): See **EXAPSALMOS**.

**HIERARCH** (*῾Ιεράρχης, ὁ*): See **BISHOP**.

**HOLY ALTAR**: See **SANCTUARY**. It is also known by the terms: **AGION VIMA**, **ALTAR**, **HOLY ALTAR**, **IERON VIMA**, **SANCTUARY**, or **VIMA**.[^619]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Holy Chrism – Holy Eucharist – Holy Myrrh – Holy Sanctuary – Holy Table – Horologion – Hours – Hymn) [Demos, p. 1055 / TD-76]

**HOLY CHRISM** (*῞Αγιον Μῦρον, τό*): See **CHRISM**.

**HOLY EUCHARIST** (*Θεία Εὐχαριστία, ἡ*): Instituted by Christ Himself at the Mystical Supper, the Holy Eucharist is the central and most exalted sacramental Mystery in the life of the Church. It is celebrated in the Divine Liturgy as its culminating sacrifice and is synonymous with it. During the Holy Eucharist, the bread and wine are changed by the power and descent of the Holy Spirit into the Body and Blood of Christ. The faithful receive the consecrated Holy Gifts in Holy Communion and thereby become one with Christ. As Mystery, the Holy Eucharist accomplishes the most perfect communion between God and man. At the same time, it unites the communicants with one another as members of Christ’s Body, the Church. The faithful offer thanksgiving (*Eucharistia*) to God for His many blessings and for the salvation that He hath wrought for them, especially through His earthly ministry, His death upon the Cross, and His third-day Resurrection.

**HOLY MYRRH** (*῞Αγιον Μῦρον, τό*): See **CHRISM**.

**HOLY SANCTUARY**: See **SANCTUARY**. It is also known by the terms: **AGION VIMA**, **ALTAR**, **HOLY ALTAR**, **IERON VIMA**, **SANCTUARY**, or **VIMA**.

**HOLY TABLE** (*῾Αγία Τράπεζα, ἡ*; Slav. *Prestol*): See **ALTAR**.

**HOROLOGION** (*῾Ωρολόγιον, τό*): Alternate spelling of **OROLOGION** (*῾Ωρολόγιον τὸ Μέγα* / Slav. *Chasoslov*).

**HOURS** (*῞Ωρα, ἡ, pl. ῏Ωραι, αἱ*; Slav. *Chasy*): Though predominantly celebrated in holy monasteries, the Hours are services regularly celebrated in parish churches in cities and towns. The First, Third, and Sixth Hours are chanted before Matins (*Orthros*), and the Ninth Hour before Vespers. The text of these Hours is found in the liturgical book *The Great Horologion* (*῾Ωρολόγιον τὸ Μέγα* / *Chasoslov*), preceding the Service of Matins (*Orthros*). A distinct solemn cycle called the Great (or Royal) Hours is celebrated on the Eve of the Nativity of Christ, the Eve of Theophany, and on Great and Holy Friday. Their texts are contained in the festal propers of those days.

**HYMN** (*Ὕμνος, ὁ*): See **TROPARION**.[^620]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Hymn of Kassiani – Hymn of the Church – Hypakoi – Ichos – Idiomelon – Ieratikon – Ieromartys) [Demos, p. 1056 / TD-77]

**HYMN OF KASSIANI** (*Τροπάριον τῆς Κασσιανῆς, τό*): Saint Kassiani is also known by the names Kassia, Casia, Ikasia, Cassia, Cassiane, Kassiana, or Eikasia. She was a Byzantine hymnographer (9th c.) born in Constantinople to a noble family, who later matured as an extraordinarily gifted, beautiful, and learned woman—so much so that she was chosen to participate in a "bridal show" (bride-show), at which the Emperor Theophilos was to select an empress. Struck by Kassiani's radiant beauty, the Emperor approached her and remarked: "Through a woman came forth the baser things," referring to Eve's transgression. Kassiani readily replied: "Through a woman came forth the better things," referring to the redemptive Incarnation of Christ through His Most Pure and Virgin Mother. Stung by her sharp and intelligent reply, the Emperor rejected her and instead chose Theodora as his wife. Following this, Kassiani embraced the monastic life and founded a women's monastery in Constantinople, closely affiliated with the renowned Monastery of the Stoudion. Serving as abbess (hegumenia) of the monastery, she composed numerous liturgical hymns, at least twenty of which are preserved in the liturgical books of the Church. Of these compositions, the most famous is the renowned Doxastikon of Holy Wednesday Matins (customarily chanted on the evening of Great and Holy Tuesday), known universally as the **Hymn of Kassiani** (**"Lord, the woman fallen into many sins"** / *Κύριε, ἡ ἐν πολλαῖς ἁμαρτίαις περιπεσοῦσα γυνή*). Her feast is commemorated on September 7.

**HYMN OF THE CHURCH**: See **TU NAU** (*Τοῦ Ναοῦ* / Troparion of the Temple).

**HYPAKOI**: See **YPAKOI** (*῾Υπακοή, ἡ*).

**ICHOS** (*Ἦχος, ὁ*): Mode or Tone; the musical system of eight liturgical melodic modes (Octoechos, Tones 1 through 8) governing Byzantine and Slavic liturgical chant. See **MODE**.

**IDIOMELON, IDIOMELA** (*᾿Ιδιόμελον, τό· pl. ᾿Ιδιόμελα, τά*; Slav. *Samoglasen*): A hymn possessing its own unique proper melody, meter, and stanzaic structure, which does not follow an external melodic model (*podoben* or *prosomion*) and does not serve as a model for other stanzas.

**IERATIKON** (*῾Ιερατικόν, τό*; Slav. *Sluzhebnik*): Also designated the *Ierotelestikon* or *Liturgikon*. The official sacerdotal Sluzhebnik containing the fixed rubrics and prayers chanted and recited by the priest and deacon for Great and Daily Vespers, Matins (*Orthros*), the Divine Liturgies of Saint John Chrysostom, Saint Basil the Great, and the Presanctified Gifts, as well as supplementary prayers and dismissals.

**IEROMARTYS** (*῾Ιερομάρτυς, ὁ*; Slav. *Sviashchennomuchenik*): Hieromartyr; a bishop, priest, or deacon who bore witness to Christ unto shedding his blood and suffering martyrdom for the faith.[^621]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Ieron – Ieron Vima – Igumenos – Ikonomia – Ikonostasion – Ikos – Iliton Part 1) [Demos, p. 1057 / TD-78]

**IERON** (*῾Ιερόν, τό*): Sacred, holy (adjective); substantively used to designate the Sanctuary, holy place, or Altar.

**IERON VIMA** (*῾Ιερὸν Βῆμα, τό*): See **SANCTUARY**. It is also known by the designations: **AGION VIMA**, **ALTAR**, **HOLY ALTAR**, **HOLY SANCTUARY**, **SANCTUARY**, or **VIMA**.

**IGUMENOS** (*῾Ηγούμενος, ὁ*; Slav. *Ihumen* / *Hegumen*): The abbot, archimandrite, or monastic superior governing a monastery.

**IKONOMIA** (*Οἰκονομία, ἡ*): Lit. "stewardship" or "dispensation"; the canonical principle whereby ecclesiastical authority exercises discretionary pastoral leniency, accommodation, or adaptation of the strict letter of church canons and liturgical rubrics (*akribeia*) to meet specific pastoral necessities or spiritual needs without compromising dogma.

**IKONOSTASION** (*Εἰκονοστάσιον, τό*; also spelled *Iconostasion* or *Iconostasis*): The barrier or screen adorned with icons separating the Sanctuary (*Ieron Vima*) from the nave and Soleas. Also historically designated the **Templon** (*Τέμπλον*). In traditional architectural arrangement, it incorporates three doorways:
- The Northern Deacon's Door, customarily depicting the Archangel Michael or the Holy Protomartyr Stephen;
- The Southern Deacon's Door, customarily depicting the Archangel Gabriel or a deacon saint;
- The central two-valved portal, designated the **Holy Doors** or **Beautiful Gate** (*῾Ωραία Πύλη* / *῾Αγίαι Θύραι*), through which only vested sacred ministers pass during the performance of liturgical rites. While in some modern jurisdictions the term "Royal Doors" is applied colloquially to the Beautiful Gate, historically and architecturally that designation applies strictly to the central portal leading from the Narthex into the Nave, through which the Byzantine Emperor and Patriarch entered the temple in solemn procession.

**IKOS, pl. IKOI** (*Οἶκος, ὁ· pl. Οἶκοι, οἱ*): Stanza or "house" (Slav. *Ikos*). Each of the twenty-four poetic stanzas of the Akathist Hymn to the Most Holy Theotokos is designated an Ikos. Furthermore, in the structure of the liturgical Canon at Matins, the Ikos is the poetic stanza chanted immediately following the Kontakion between the Sixth and Seventh Odes. The Ikos amplifies and unpacks the theological mystery articulated in the Kontakion and, as a rule, terminates with the same refrain as the Kontakion.

**ILITON** (*Εἱλητόν, τό*; Slav. *Iliton*): See also **ANTIMINSION**.
The Iliton is a rectangular cloth of red or crimson silk or linen, wrapped around the Antiminsion to preserve and protect it upon the Holy Table.
It is strictly forbidden to celebrate the Divine Liturgy without the Antiminsion. If the consecrated Holy Table is damaged or disturbed by an unforeseen calamity, the Divine Liturgy may nevertheless be lawfully celebrated upon an altar table by deploying the Antim-[^622]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Iliton Concluded – Imerologion – Immovable Feast Part 1) [Demos, p. 1058 / TD-79]

insion. If it becomes necessary to celebrate the Divine Liturgy upon an unconsecrated altar table or in an emergency chapel, the priest is permitted to do so provided he spreads the consecrated Antiminsion upon it.
Only a bishop possesses the authority to consecrate an Antiminsion. This consecration occurs either during the solemn dedication and consecration of a church, or through an independent episcopal rite. In this rite, the bishop anoints a small sewn relic-pocket within the Antiminsion with Holy Chrism (*Agion Myron*), deposits therein the sacred relics of holy martyrs, and seals them securely with fragrant wax-mastic. He subsequently inscribes the Antiminsion with the dedication of the church for which it is consecrated, subscribes his episcopal signature, and imprints his official diocesan seal upon it.
The canonical custody and use of an Antiminsion remains under the statutory jurisdiction of the ruling Bishop. He bestows the Antiminsion and Holy Chrism upon the priest as the tangible sign and mandate of canonical faculty to celebrate the Sacred Mysteries. If the bishop revokes or suspends this pastoral faculty from the priest, he reclaims and removes the Antiminsion.

**IMEROLOGION** (*῾Ημερολόγιον, τό*): Liturgical calendar or ordo book. In current ecclesiastical practice, this is the annual directorium published under the authority of the Church containing the day-to-day order of services and rubrical assignments for each liturgical year. The volume is compiled annually in strict conformity with the directives of the canonical **Typikon**.
The Imerologion is an indispensable rubrical guide utilized by the priest and the chanters to arrange and celebrate the divine services correctly in the parish and monastery.
Note that the official Imerologion issued by the governing ecclesiastical authority regulates all services within its eparchies and parishes, harmonizing the daily intersection of the Octoechos, Menaion, Triodion, and Pentecostarion.

**IMMOVABLE FEAST** (*᾿Ακίνητος ἑορτή, ἡ*): Fixed Feast; a feast whose commemoration is permanently assigned to a fixed solar calendar date in the liturgical year (e.g., the Nativity of Our Lord is celebrated on December 25; the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos[^623]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Immovable Feast Concluded – Incense – Introductory Psalm – Irinika – Heirmos – Isapostolos Part 1) [Demos, p. 1059 / TD-80]

is celebrated on March 25).

**INCENSE** (*Θυμίαμα, τό* or *Λιβάνι, τό*; Slav. *Ladan*): Fragrant tree gum or resin (olibanum) compounded with aromatic essences, placed upon burning charcoal and burned in a liturgical censer (*Thymiaterion* or *Katzion*) as an oblation of prayer and sweet-smelling sacrifice before God.

**INTRODUCTORY PSALM** (*Προοιμιακὸς Ψαλμός, ὁ*; Slav. *Prednachinatelnyi Psalom*): Psalm 103 [LXX 103 / MT 104] (**"Bless the Lord, O my soul"** / *Εὐλόγει, ἡ ψυχή μου, τὸν Κύριον*), read at the beginning of Great and Daily Vespers, chanting the majesty of creation and the providential wisdom of God the Creator.

**IRINIKA** (*Εἰρηνικά, τά*; Slav. *Mirnaya Ekteniya*): The Litany of Peace or Great Litany, whose initial and recurrent petitions commence or conclude with the liturgical supplication: **“In peace, let us beseech the Lord”** (*᾿Εν εἰρήνῃ τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν*).

**HEIRMOS** (*Εἱρμός, ὁ*; Slav. *Irmos*; lit. "link" or "drawing together"): The initial thematic stanza or model hymn opening each Ode of a liturgical Canon. The Heirmos fulfills a twofold structural function: it links the Ode thematically to the scriptural Biblical Canticle upon which it is founded, and, by establishing the melodic pattern, rhythm, meter, and acrostic framework for all subsequent troparia in the Ode, it serves as the primary architectural link in the hymnographic chain.

**ISAPOSTOLOS** (*᾿Ισαπόστολος, ὁ / ἡ*; Slav. *Ravnoapostolnyi*): Equal-to-the-Apostles. An exalted hagiographical title conferred upon canonized saints whose missionary zeal, conversion of nations, heroic defense of Orthodoxy, and apostolic labors equaled the ministry of the Holy Apostles. The title is recognized across Eastern Christian traditions. Below is a representative catalogue of saints honored with the title Equal-to-the-Apostles:
- Saint Mary Magdalene (1st century)
- Saint Photini, the Samaritan Woman (1st century)
- Saint Thekla, Protomartyr among Women (1st century)
- Saint Abercius, Wonderworker of Hierapolis (2nd century)
- Saint Helena, Augusta and Empress of Constantinople (ca. 250 – ca. 330)
- Saint Constantine I, Emperor and Equal-to-the-Apostles (ca. 272 – 337)
- Saint Nino, Enlightener of Georgia (ca. 296 – ca. 338 or 340)
- Saint Patrick, Enlightener of Ireland (5th century)
- Saint Cyril, Teacher of the Slavs (827 – 869)
- Saint Methodios, Archbishop of Great Moravia (826 – 885)[^624]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Isapostolos Concluded – Isodikon – Isodos Part 1) [Demos, p. 1060 / TD-81]`

- Saint Boris I, Tsar and Baptizer of Bulgaria (reposed 907)
- Saint Olga, Princess of Kyiv (ca. 890 – 969)
- Saint Vladimir the Great, Grand Prince and Enlightener of Rus' (ca. 958 – 1015)
- Saint Sava I, First Archbishop of Serbia (1175 – 1235)
- Saint Cosmas of Aetolia, Hieromartyr (1714 – 1779)
- Saint Innocent of Alaska, Metropolitan and Enlightener of the Aleuts (1797 – 1879)
- Saint Nicholas of Japan, Archbishop and Enlightener of Japan (1836 – 1912)

**ISODIKON** (*Εἰσοδικόν, τό*; Slav. *Vkhodnoye*): The Introit or Entrance Hymn chanted by the sacred ministers and chanters at the Small Entrance of the Divine Liturgy after the procession moves from the Sanctuary onto the Soleas before the Holy Doors. The general Sunday and weekday Isodikon is the scriptural verse: **“Come, let us worship and bow down before Christ. O Son of God, Who art risen from the dead, save us who sing to Thee: Alleluia!”** (*Δεῦτε προσκυνήσωμεν καὶ προσπέσωμεν Χριστῷ· σῶσον ἡμᾶς Υἱὲ Θεοῦ, ὁ ἀναστὰς ἐκ νεκρῶν, ψάλλοντάς σοι, ᾿Αλληλούϊα*). On Great Feasts of the Lord, proper festal Eisodika replace the ordinary refrain. Following the intonation of the Isodikon, the clergy enter the Sanctuary through the Beautiful Gate.

**ISODOS** (*Εἴσοδος, ἡ*; Slav. *Vkhod*): Entrance; a solemn liturgical procession in which the celebrating clergy, preceded by taper-bearers holding processional candles (and deacons bearing ripida and censers), process out of the Sanctuary through the northern door of the icon screen onto the Soleas, pause before the Beautiful Gate for the proper entrance prayers and blessings, and re-enter the Sanctuary through the Beautiful Gate. The liturgical tradition prescribes three principal entrances:
- a. At Great Vespers (The Entrance with the Censer): The deacon (or priest, if serving without deacon) carries the censer (with the Gospel book added on days when an appointed Gospel reading occurs, as on the eves of Pascha and major feasts).
- b. The Small Entrance (At the Divine Liturgy): The deacon carries the Book of the Gospels (*Evangelion*), accompanied by candle-bearers and the priest, symbolizing the public manifestation and preaching of Christ in the world.
- c. The Great Entrance (At the Divine Liturgy): The solemn offertory procession transferring the prepared holy gifts of bread and wine from the table of the **Prothesis** (*Πρόθεσις*) out through the north door onto the Soleas and into the Sanctuary before the **Holy Table** (*῾Αγία Τράπεζα*). The deacon, holding the censer upon his finger, carries the paten (**Diskarion** or **Diskos** / *Δισκάριον*), while the priest carries the Holy Chalice (**Agion Potirion** / *῞Αγιον Ποτήριον*), both covered with their[^625]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Isodos Concluded – Ison – Kalymmafchion – Kanion – Kanon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1061 / TD-82]

respective veils and the Great Veil (**Aer**).

**ISON** (*Ἶσον, τό*): The drone, fundamental tonic note, or pitch base of a Byzantine musical Mode or Tone. When a liturgical chant is performed, the drone-holders (*isokrates*) sustain the fundamental pitch sotto voce, either sounding the precise vowel of each text syllable or, in more rapid melismatic melodies, sustaining a continuous vocal resonance on the vowel sound.

**KALYMMAFCHION** (*Καλυμμαύχιον, τό*; Slav. *Kamilavka*): The cylindrical black clerical head covering worn by Eastern Christian clergy. In Greek liturgical use, the kamilavka of the secular (married) clergy features a small raised flat rim or peak at the crown, whereas that of monastics remains flat and smooth. Monks and celibate clergy (hieromonks, archimandrites, and bishops) wear the sacred monastic veil or cowl (Greek *koukoulion* / *ἐπανωκαλύμμαυχον*; Slav. *klobuk* / *namitka*) draped over the kamilavka. In the Slavic tradition, the veil is permanently fastened to the stiff cylindrical hat to form the composite vestment known as the *klobuk*, whereas in Greek tradition the epanokalymmafchion remains detachable.

**KANION** (*Κανίον, τό*): A liturgical sprinkler or flagon of precious metal, utilized for asperging the faithful, the temple, or sacred icons with fragrant rosewater or holy water on feast days.

**KANON, pl. KANONES** (*Κανών, ὁ· pl. Κανόνες, οἱ*; Slav. *Kanon*): See also **CANON**.
A monumental hymnographic poetic form comprising most frequently eight or nine Odes (and more rarely, four, three, or two Odes). The Canon commemorates the sacred mystery, feast, or saint of the day and forms the liturgical center of Matins (*Orthros*). Each Ode consists of an opening model hymn (**Heirmos**) followed by a prescribed series of poetic strophes (**Troparia**). Each of the nine Odes corresponds to and is typologically patterned after one of the nine Biblical Canticles of Holy Scripture, establishing an Old Testament event as the prophetic archetype of a New Testament revelation in Christ. Complete Canons encompass nine Odes; however, the Second Ode (patterned upon the severe song of condemnation in Deuteronomy 32) is sung exclusively during the weekdays of Great Lent. The Great Penitential Canon of Saint Andrew of Crete is chanted in sections during the first week of Great Lent and in its entirety on Wednesday evening of the Fifth Week.
When a Canon contains fewer than nine Odes, it is designated a **tetraodion** (four odes), **triodion** (three odes), or **diodion** (two odes), according to the number of constituent odes.[^626]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kanonarchis – Katanyktikon – Katasarkion – Katavasia – Kathagiasis – Kathisma Part 1) [Demos, p. 1062 / TD-83]

**KANONARCHIS** (*Κανονάρχης, ὁ*; Slav. *Kanonarkh*): The canonarch; the official cantor, precentor, or prompter whose liturgical office is to intone and dictate the verses, stichera, and troparia to the left and right choirs phrase by phrase during the performance of the divine services. Lit. "one who begins or leads the canon."

**KATANYKTIKON, pl. KATANYKTIKA** (*Κατανυκτικόν, τό· pl. Κατανυκτικά, τά*; Slav. *Pokayanni*): Penitential troparia or hymns permeated with spiritual compunction, contrition, sorrow for sin, and repentance, chanted especially on Lenten days and during the weekdays of the Octoechos.

**KATASARKION** (*Κατασάρκιον, τό*; Slav. *Srachitsa*): Lit. "upon the flesh"; the white linen tunic or seamless undercloth fitted tightly over the bare stone surface of the Holy Table as a protective inner shroud during the rite of Consecration of a church. It represents the winding sheet of Christ in the tomb and is bound permanently to the four columns of the altar table with a consecrated cord (*spargana*).

**KATAVASIA, pl. KATAVASIE** (*Καταβασία, ἡ· pl. Καταβασίαι, αἱ*; Slav. *Katavasiya*): A liturgical classification of festive model hymns chanted at the conclusion of each Ode of the Canon at Matins (*Orthros*). In Greek ecclesiastical usage, the term derives from the verb *katabaino* (*καταβαίνω*, "to descend"), designating the ancient monastic practice whereby the chanters descended from their raised choir stalls (*stacidia*) into the center of the nave to unite in chanting these climactic heirmoi together. On Sundays and major festal celebrations, a Katavasia is chanted after every individual Ode. The term "Katavasiai" likewise denotes the complete set of festal heirmoi assigned across the nine Odes of Matins. Katavasiai are seasonal and anticipate upcoming Great Feasts: thus the Katavasiai of the Nativity of Christ (**"Christ is born, glorify Him!"** / *Χριστὸς γεννᾶται· δοξάσατε*) are chanted from November 21 through December 25. The most frequently chanted ordinary Katavasiai are those of the Canon to the Most Holy Theotokos in Tone 4, **“I shall open my mouth”** (*᾿Ανοίξω τὸ στόμα μου*). On ordinary non-festal days, the Katavasia is chanted only after the Third, Sixth, Eighth, and Ninth Odes.

**KATHAGIASIS or KATHAGIASMOS** (*Καθαγίασις, ἡ* or *Καθαγιασμός, ὁ*): Sacerdotal consecration or sanctification; specifically designating the consecration of the Holy Gifts in the Eucharistic Anaphora of the Divine Liturgy.

**KATHISMA, pl. KATHISMATA** (*Κάθισμα, τό· pl. Καθίσματα, τά*; Slav. *Kafizma*, pl. *Kafizmy*; also *Sedalen*): The term possesses three distinct liturgical meanings:
- First, a Kathisma is one of the twenty major divisions or sections into which the Septuagint (LXX) Old Testament Psalter is partitioned for liturgical recitation throughout the weekly cycle in the Byzantine and Slavic traditions. Each Kathisma is subdivided into three states or antiphons (*Staseis* / *Στάσεις*), each comprising a variable group of psalms (e.g., Kathisma 1 = Psalms 1–8; Kathisma 2 = Psalms 9–17, etc.), concluded by the Doxology ("Glory... both now... Alleluia").[^627]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kathisma Concluded – Kathisma Hymn – Katzion – Ke Nyn – Keros) [Demos, p. 1063 / TD-84]

- Second, Kathismata denote a class of liturgical troparia (**Sessional Hymns**, Slav. *Sedalni*). These short poetic hymns are chanted following the reading of each Kathisma of the Psalter, after the Polyeleos, and after the Third Ode of the Canon at Matins. The designation derives from the Greek verb *kathezomai* (*καθέζομαι*, "to sit"), indicating the rubrical dispensation allowing the clergy and faithful to sit during their recitation, in contrast to the standing posture required during solemn psalms and prayers.
- Third, in monastic administration, a Kathisma denotes a small solitary monastic dwelling or hermitage. On the Holy Mountain of Athos, each subordinate monastic settlement (*skete*, *cell*, *kalyve*, *hesychasterion*, or *kathisma*) exists under the jurisdiction of one of the twenty sovereign ruling monasteries. The Kathisma constitutes one of the smallest establishments, designed as a simple habitation for a single solitary hesychast monk.

**KATHISMA HYMN**: Also designated **SESSIONAL HYMN** (lit. 'sitting hymn'; Slav. *sedalen*): A hymn chanted as an invitation to seated reflection and rest following lengthy periods of standing, such as the chanting of the Psalter, the singing of the Polyeleos, or the completion of the Third Ode of the Canon at Matins.

**KATZION** (*Κάτζιον, τό*): A small hand-held censer fitted with a short handle and small jingling bells, utilized in monastic rubrics and at specific rites (such as the Lenten Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts or monastic commemorations) for swift censing of icons and brotherhood.

**KE NYN** (*Καὶ νῦν*): Lit. "And now"; the standard rubrical abbreviation for the concluding phrase of the liturgical doxology: **“Both now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen.”** (*Καὶ νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν*). This cue is employed ubiquitously in the **Typikon** and liturgical books, routinely following the abbreviation **Doxa** (*Δόξα* / "Glory...").

**KEROS** (*Καιρός, ὁ*; Slav. *Vremya*): Lit. "appointed time", "due season", or "opportunity". In liturgical practice, "taking keros" (*Kairon*) designates the preliminary sacerdotal rite of preparation performed by the priest and deacon before vesting for the Divine Liturgy. The sacred ministers stand before the Holy Doors of the icon screen, make three reverences, venerate the holy icons of Christ, the Theotokos, the Forerunner, and the patron saint of the temple, recite the appointed penitential and vesting prayers, turn and bow to the people to ask forgiveness, and enter the Sanctuary to vest.[^628]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kimisis – Kinonikon – Kiropigion – Kliros Part 1) [Demos, p. 1064 / TD-85]

**KIMISIS** (*Κοίμησις, ἡ*; Slav. *Uspeniye*): The Falling Asleep or Dormition of the Most Holy Mother of God (celebrated on August 15). See **DORMITION**.

**KINONIKON** (*Κοινωνικόν, τό*; Slav. *Prychasten*): The Communion Hymn; a liturgical verse, customarily excerpted from the Septuagint Psalter or appointed festal scriptures, chanted melismatically and ornately by the choir while the sacred ministers receive Holy Communion within the Sanctuary, immediately preceding the distribution of Holy Communion to the faithful. The ordinary Sunday Kinonikon is Psalm 148:1 [LXX 148:1 / MT 148:1]: **“Praise the Lord from the heavens; praise Him in the highest. Alleluia!”** (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον ἐκ τῶν οὐρανῶν· αἰνεῖτε αὐτὸν ἐν τοῖς ὑψίστοις. ᾿Αλληλούϊα*).

**KIROPIGION** (*Κηροπήγιον, τό*): A liturgical candlestick, taper stand, or candelabrum used to hold wax candles in the temple.

**KLIROS, pl. KLIRI** (*Κλῆρος, ὁ· pl. κλῆροι, οἱ*; Slav. *Kliros*): An ecclesiastical term possessing two primary meanings:
- First, the general order of the clergy (ordained sacred ministers and minor clerics).
- Second, the designated area or choir stalls on either side of the Soleas in an Eastern Christian church reserved for the cantors and chanters during divine worship. By extension, the term designates the choir itself or the liturgical music stand upon which liturgical books and chant manuscripts are placed.
The designation derives historically from the Greek word *kleros* (*κλῆρος*, "lot" or "inheritance"), recalling that the ministers of the altar were originally chosen by lot and set apart for the service of the Lord.
In traditional cathedral and monastic architecture, two choirs (*kliri*) are situated on the north (left) and south (right) flanks of the Soleas before the icon screen, enabling the antiphonary execution of hymns between the right and left choirs. This antiphonal choir arrangement was perfected in the Great Imperial Church of Hagia Sophia in Constantinople, establishing the benchmark for all monuments within the Byzantine liturgical sphere.
This classical structure presupposes two complete choirs led by two master chanters (*protopsaltes* and *lampadarios*). In contemporary parish practice, where resources are limited, a single kliros is often maintained on the right side of the Soleas, or singing is supported from a choir loft situated at the western end of the nave above the narthex.[^629]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kliros Concluded – Klitir Ravduchos – Kollyva – Kontakion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1065 / TD-86]

A typical parish church today will frequently incorporate a choir loft above the narthex at the western end of the nave, in addition to the traditional kliros situated before the icon screen on the southern side of the Soleas.
In Greek and some Slavic traditions, singers chanting at the kliros wear a black clerical cassock (*riasson* / *ράσον*). In the Armenian tradition, both male and female members of the choir at the kliros vest in the liturgical *sticharion*.

**KLITIR RAVDUCHOS** (*Κλητὴρ ῥαβδοῦχος, ὁ*): Staff-bearing usher or mace-bearer; an ecclesiastical officer who clears the way and maintains order during episcopal processions and solemn patriarchal ceremonies.

**KOLLYVA** (*Κόλλυβα, τά*; Ukrainian / Slav. *Kolyvo*): Boiled wheat sweetened with honey, sugar, raisins, and pomegranate seeds, blessed in memorial services (Panakhyda / Parastasis) in honor of the departed, symbolizing the bodily resurrection of the dead according to the words of Christ: "Unless a grain of wheat falls into the earth and dies, it remains alone; but if it dies, it bears much fruit" (John 12:24).

**KONTAKION, pl. KONTAKIA** (*Κοντάκιον, τό· pl. Κοντάκια, τά*; Slav. *Kondak*): The Kontakion was the preeminent poetical and hymnographic genre of early Byzantine hymnology, achieving its classic zenith in the inspired verse of Saint Romanos the Melodist (6th century), many of whose masterpieces remain central to the liturgical life of the Church. Thematically, the classical Kontakion presented an expansive narrative and dramatic homily upon the great feast of the day, notably the Nativity of Christ. Structurally, an ancient Kontakion consisted of 18 to 30 or more stanzas, each designated an **Ikos** (*Οἶκος*), composed in identical meter and rhythm and sung to the same melody. The initial letters of these stanzas formed an acrostic, spelling out the title of the feast, the poet’s name, or the Greek alphabet. These stanzas were preceded by a short independent introductory stanza called the **proimion** (*προοίμιον*) or **kukulion** (*κουκούλιον*), which established the musical Mode and the concluding refrain (**efymnion** / *ἐφύμνιον*) repeated at the end of every subsequent Ikos.
In contemporary Byzantine and Slavic practice, the ancient extended Kontakion has been largely replaced in the daily office by the Canon; today, only the introductory prelude (now bearing the name Kontakion) and a single Ikos are chanted at Matins between the Sixth and Seventh Odes of the Canon. Furthermore, this short Kontakion is chanted at the Divine Liturgy immediately preceding the Trisagion Hymn. The ordinary daily Kontakion chanted at the Divine Liturgy is the beloved Theotokion: **“The Protection of Christians”** (*Προστασία τῶν Χριστιανῶν*), honoring the Mother of God as an unashamed intercessor before the Creator.[^630]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kontakion Continued Part 2) [Demos, p. 1066 / TD-87]

cessor and protectress of all Christians.

The term *kontakion* originally derived from the Greek diminutive *kontakion* (*κοντάκιον*, from *κόνταξ*, a short rod or wand), referring to the wooden stick or cylinder around which the hymnographic parchment roll or scroll was wrapped on account of its length.

Originally, the Kontakion was a poetic "verse sermon" consisting of an introductory preamble or Proimion (*Προοίμιον*) followed by a series of longer stanzas, the Ikoi (*Οἶκοι*), all terminating with an identical recurring phrase or refrain (*ephymnion*). Kontakia were gradually supplanted in the liturgical offices by a subsequent poetic hymnographic cycle, the **Canon** (*Κανών*). In the present ecclesiastical office, only the Proimion (now itself customarily designated simply as the Kontakion) and the first Ikos survive in liturgical recitation. In contemporary Byzantine practice, they are regularly read at Matins (*Orthros*), except on great feasts, and the final phrase is repeated by the choir—a living survival of the ancient practice. The Kontakion is likewise read at the Little Hours and chanted at the Divine Liturgy, where in standard usage the proper kontakia are seasonal, coordinating with the prescribed Katavasiai.

No direct documentary evidence survives to establish the exact date at which the Kontakion was formally incorporated into the Byzantine liturgical system. No reference to this poetic genre is to be found in imperial Byzantine documents or writings during the initial era of its flourishing. Even the technical name *Kontakion* appears for the first time only in ninth-century sources; the monastic hymnographers who originally composed works of this genre designated such a composition variously as a Hymn (*ὕμνος*), Psalm (*ψαλμός*), Poem (*ποίημα* or *ἔπος*), Canticle or Song (*ᾠδή* or *ᾆσμα*), Praise or Laud (*αἶνος*), Prayer (*προσευχή*), or Supplication (*δέησις*).

A complete historical Kontakion comprised from eighteen to thirty, or even more, structurally and metrically identical stanzas. A single stanza is designated a Troparion (*Τροπάριον*), varying in length from three to thirteen poetic lines. All subsequent Troparia in the work are composed upon the exact metric and rhythmic model of an initial stanza, the **Heirmos** (*Εἱρμός*). A Kontakion is either constructed upon the pattern of an Heirmos composed expressly for that poem, or adopts the meter of a pre-existing Heirmos previously composed for another Kontakion. At the commencement of the Kontakion stands a short Troparion that is metrically and melodically independent of the subsequent strophes: this is the Proimion (*Προοίμιον*) or Koukoulion (*Κουκούλιον*), which at a later[^631]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kontakion Continued Part 3) [Demos, p. 1067 / TD-88]

stage often consists of two or three stanzas. The Proimion and the subsequent stanzas are linked together by the refrain, the Ephymnion (*᾿Εφύμνιον*), with which all stanzas conclude, and by the shared musical Tone (*Ἦχος*). The placement of this common refrain at the conclusion of each stanza demonstrates that the ancient Kontakia were chanted by a solo cantor, while the assembled choir and congregation joined in singing the refrain. The successive stanzas of the Kontakion are bound together either alphabetically or through an acrostic: the initial letters of each stanza sequentially form either the complete Greek alphabet in regular order, or an inscribed sentence containing the name of the hymnographer and the title of the composition. This acrostic is formally announced in the rubrical title of the Kontakion. From this liturgical heading we ascertain: (1) the day and feast upon which the Kontakion is chanted; (2) the sacred subject or person commemorated; (3) the exact acrostic schema; and (4) the musical Tone of the melody. For example, in the renowned Kontakion **"Joseph the All-Chaste"** composed by Saint Romanos the Melodist, chanted on Great and Holy Monday of Passion Week, the Greek heading reads as follows:

*«Τῇ ἁγίᾳ καὶ μεγάλῃ δευτέρᾳ. Κοντάκιον εἰς τὸν Ἰωσὴφ φέρον ἀκροστιχίδα τήνδε· ᾿Αλφάβητον ῾Ρωμανοῦ, Πλάγιος δ΄. Πρὸς τὸ ῾Ο υἱός σου παρθένε πανάμωμε.»*

From this title we learn that the Kontakion chanted on Great and Holy Monday was composed in honor of the Patriarch Joseph; that each successive stanza begins with a letter of the Greek alphabet, followed by an acrostic spelling out the words *᾿Αλφάβητον ῾Ρωμανοῦ* ("Alphabet of Romanos"); that the melody is set in Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal); and that it is modeled upon the pre-existing melodic model stanza beginning with the incipit **"Thy Son, O all-blameless Virgin"** (*῾Ο υἱός σου, παρθένε πανάμωμε*). This complete Kontakion therefore comprises forty stanzas, each beginning with one of the following letters:

α β γ δ ε ζ η θ ι κ λ μ ν ξ ο π ρ σ τ υ φ χ ψ ω α λ φ α β η τ ο ν ρ ω μ α ν ο υ.

The acrostic most frequently encountered indicates that the humble Romanos is the author of the poem:

*«φέρον ἀκροστιχίδα τήνδε· τοῦ ταπεινοῦ ῥωμανοῦ»* (bearing this acrostic: "Of the humble Romanos"), or *«τοῦ ταπεινοῦ ῥωμανοῦ τοῦτο τὸ ποίημα»* ("This is the poem of the humble Romanos"), or *«τοῦ ταπεινοῦ ῥωμανοῦ ὁ ψαλμός»* ("The psalm of the humble Romanos"). When the hymnographer wished to compose more stanzas than the available letters of the acrostic permitted, he repeated selected letters of the acrostic for two consecutive stro-[^632]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kontakion Continued Part 4) [Demos, p. 1068 / TD-89]

phes, as in the doubled-letter acrostic: *ττοῦ ταπεινοῦ γαβριὴλ ὁ ὕμνος οὗτος ἀμήν ἀμμήν* ("This hymn is of the humble Gabriel. Amen, Amen").

In another instance, in an anonymous hymnographer's poem composed in memory of Saint John Chrysostom, this principle of letter doubling is expanded even further. The prescription in the title: *«φέρον ἀκροστιχίδα τήνδε· εἰς τὸν χρυσόστομον»* ("bearing this acrostic: Unto Chrysostom") is executed through extensive gemination: *εις τον χχρρυυσσοοοσσττομον*. At the period when the classic Kontakia were composed, the Greek language had already surrendered the classical distinction between long and short vowels, which constituted the foundation of the quantitative meter of ancient classical poetry. The vowels and diphthongs *η*, *ει*, and *οι* had become phonetically equivalent to *ι* (iota), and *αι* equivalent to *ε* (epsilon). Consequently, a new tonic-rhythmic system of versification arose, founded upon the strict rule that all stanzas must possess the exact same number of syllables as the model **Heirmos** (*Εἱρμός*) upon which they were modeled, and that the word stress accents must occupy identical positions across all stanzas as in the corresponding verses of the Heirmos. The composition of a Kontakion—or, in later centuries, of a **Canon** (*Κανών*)—was a sophisticated undertaking that could be accomplished only by a poet who was simultaneously a master composer and musician. Melody and verse formed a single entity. In adapting the Troparia to the melody of the Heirmos, it was not merely necessary to equate the syllable count of each line with the model strophe; the dynamic melodic stress accents had to coincide precisely with the accented syllables of the hymnographic text.

The Kontakion appeared in Byzantine worship with striking suddenness, without direct literary antecedents in classical Greek forms. Byzantine hagiographical tradition attributes its introduction into Eastern Christian worship to Saint Romanos the Melodist, celebrated by Saint Germanos, who composed a hymn in his honor providing an account of his miraculous divine inspiration. The historical association of Saint Romanos with the rise of the Kontakion allows us to date its formal entry into the Constantinopolitan liturgical tradition to the early decades of the sixth century. Saint Romanos was of Jewish lineage, born in Emesa upon the Orontes River (modern Homs, Syria). He was ordained deacon at Berytus (Beirut) in Phoenicia, and subsequently journeyed to Constantinople during the reign of Emperor Anastasios I (491–518), joining the clergy of the Church of the Most Holy Theotokos in the Kyros district. There, according to sacred tradition, the Mother of God appeared to him on the Feast of the Nativity of Christ, bestowing upon him the miraculous charism of hymnography, whereupon he composed more than one thousand Kontakia. In August 535, a catastrophic earthquake devastated a substantial portion of Constantinople, which was subsequently commemorated each[^633]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kontakion Concluded Part 5) [Demos, p. 1069 / TD-90]

year on the anniversary of the catastrophe with solemn hymns and supplications. Saint Romanos, who frequently incorporated references to contemporary historical events into his compositions, commemorated this earthquake in a hymn (styled a "Psalm") chanted on Wednesday of the third week of the Great Fast. Because this Kontakion was composed under the vivid and immediate impression of the disaster, and because no historical event occurring after 535 is mentioned anywhere in the corpus of Saint Romanos, it is reasonably concluded that he reposed shortly after that date. Notable antecedents to the literary form of the Kontakion existed in Syriac Christian poetry, with which Saint Romanos, being of Syrian provenance, was intimately familiar. His supreme achievement lay in translating and adapting these Semitic poetic structures into the spirit, elegance, and melody of Byzantine hymnography, thereby establishing an entirely new genre in the liturgical life of Constantinople. Scholars identify three primary genres of classical Syriac poetry: the *Memra*, the *Madrasha*, and the *Sogitha*. The *Memra*, or metrical homily, served as the principal structural prototype for the Kontakion: like the *Memra*, the Kontakion occupied a fixed station in the Morning Office (Matins) immediately following the reading of the Holy Gospel, providing an extensive poetic amplification and paraphrase of the sacred scriptural narrative. Yet an essential distinction remains: while the Syriac *Memra* remained strictly an homiletic discourse in verse, the Kontakion in the masterworks of Saint Romanos transcends mere moral exhortation and, by absorbing the lyricism of the *Madrasha* and the dramatic dialogue of the *Sogitha*, blossoms into a sublime theological contemplation and celebration of the sacred mystery of the feast. (Adapted from *A History of Byzantine Music and Hymnography* by Egon Wellesz).

Originally, the Kontakion was an extended liturgical poem comprising eighteen to twenty-four stanzas, whose substantial length necessitated the use of a continuous parchment scroll. Saint Romanos the Melodist (+c. 556) stands as the pre-eminent master of these monumental compositions. Over time, these extended hymnographic cycles fell out of active liturgical performance in the Byzantine Rite. Today, what survives in the printed liturgical books under the designation of **Kontakion** represents only the brief introductory strophe (the ancient Proimion) accompanied by the initial **Ikos** (*Οἶκος*). In the current liturgical ordo, the Kontakion is chanted following Ode 6 of the Canon at Matins (jointly with the Ikos), at the Little Hours, and following the Dismissal Troparia at the Divine Liturgy. The Kontakion is never chanted at Vespers.[^634]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Kuvuklion – Lamb – Lambadarios – Lambaduchos – Lamplighting Psalms – Lance – Apodosis – Lenten Triodion) [Demos, p. 1070 / TD-91]

**KUVUKLION** (*Κουβούκλιον, τό*): A ceremonial canopy structure, typically carved of wood, representing the Holy Sepulchre and Tomb of Christ. During the solemn Vespers and Matins services of Great and Holy Friday and Holy Saturday, it is erected upon the Soleas (or before the Tetrapod) to enshrine the sacred cloth icon of the entombed Savior, the **Plashchanytsia** (*Epitaphios* / *᾿Επιτάφιος*).

**LAMB** (*᾿Αμνός, ὁ*; Slav. *Agnets* / *Агнецъ*): The square Eucharistic host stamped and extracted from the principal liturgical loaf (**Prosphora** / *Προσφορά*), bearing the sacred cruciform inscription **IC XC NIKA** (Ἰησοῦς Χριστὸς Νικᾷ, "Jesus Christ Conquers"). This consecrated portion is excised with the holy Lance during the Office of Preparation (**Proskomide** / *Προσκομιδή*) for subsequent Eucharistic consecration at the Divine Liturgy. The Holy Scriptures designate Christ as the "Lamb of God" (John 1:29, 1:36), fulfilling the prophetic vision of Isaiah concerning the suffering Servant: "He was led as a sheep to the slaughter, and as a lamb dumb before its shearer, so He opens not His mouth" (Isaiah 53:7 [LXX 53:7]).

**LAMBADARIOS** (*Λαμπαδάριος, ὁ*): The leader and master chanter directing the left choir at the Analogion upon the northern side of the Kliros, before the Iconostasis. Historically, the title signifies the sacred dignitary privileged to receive the Paschal Flame directly from the hand of the Patriarch on Holy Pascha to distribute the light to the clergy and faithful.

**LAMBADUCHOS** (*Λαμπαδοῦχος, ὁ*): A ceremonial taper-bearer or candle-bearer.

**LAMPLIGHTING PSALMS** (*᾿Επιλύχνιοι Ψαλμοί, οἱ*): The fixed collection of four evening psalms chanted at Great and Daily Vespers during the lighting of the lamps and the incensation of the holy temple, accompanying the insertion of the evening stichera. They comprise:
- Psalm 140 [LXX 140 / MT 141]: **"Lord, I have cried"** (*Κύριε, ἐκέκραξα...*);
- Psalm 141 [LXX 141 / MT 142]: **"With my voice unto the Lord"** (*Φωνῇ μου πρὸς Κύριον...*);
- Psalm 129 [LXX 129 / MT 130]: **"Out of the depths I have cried unto Thee, O Lord"** (*᾿Εκ βαθέων ἐκέκραξά Σοι, Κύριε...*);
- Psalm 116 [LXX 116 / MT 117]: **"Praise the Lord, all nations"** (*Αἰνεῖτε τὸν Κύριον, πάντα τὰ ἔθνη...*).

**LANCE OR SPEAR** (*Λόγχη, ἡ*; Slav. *Kopíye*): See **LONCHI**.

**LEAVE-TAKING** (lit. 'Leave-taking' / *᾿Απόδοσις, ἡ*): See the canonical liturgical term **APODOSIS**.

**LENTEN TRIODION** (Slav. *Postna Triod* / *Постная Тріодь*): See **TRIODION**.[^635]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Lipsana – Lipsanon – Litany – Liti – Liturgical Colors Part 1) [Demos, p. 1071 / TD-92]

**LIPSANA** (*Λείψανα, τά*): Sacred relics once belonging to a holy saint or martyr; the term specifically denotes the bodily remains of martyrs and saints which are deposited within the Holy Table during the solemn consecration of a church.

**LIPSANON** (*Λείψανον, τό*): The physical relic or sacred remains of a deceased holy person.

**LITANY** (*Λιτανεία, ἡ*): See **EKTENIS** or **SYNAPTI**.
A prescribed sequence of petitions offered unto God by the deacon or priest, to which the choir and chanters respond with either **"Lord, have mercy"** (*Κύριε, ἐλέησον*) or **"Grant this, O Lord"** (*Παράσχου, Κύριε*).

**LITI** (*Λιτή, ἡ*; Slav. *Litiya* / *Литія*): (1) Liturgically, an earnest, prolonged entreaty and supplication.
