It denotes the solemn liturgical procession of the sacred ministers and choir into the Narthex of the church during Great Vespers on the eve of great feasts, accompanied by the chanting of festive idiomela stichera, lengthy petitions for ecclesiastical and civil well-being, and the solemn blessing of five loaves, wheat, wine, and oil (Artoklasia). During this procession, proper **Stichera at the Liti** are chanted according to the feast. (2) Also designates a brief memorial service for the departed, consisting of the Trisagion Prayers, the funeral Dismissal Troparia, and a memorial litany.

**LITURGICAL COLORS**: Across the Byzantine liturgical tradition, shared by Eastern Orthodox and Byzantine Catholic Churches, no rigid, uniform color canon exists comparable to the post-Tridentine Western rubrical system. In the authoritative Greek Typikon of George Violakis, rubrical directions regarding vesture focus primarily upon specifying "bright" (*λευκά* / light) or "dark" (*σκοτεινά*) vestments. In modern Greek practice, deep red, maroon, or burgundy vestments are customary for solemn feast days, while a broad spectrum of colors is employed on other occasions, with gold and white prevailing as standard festive tones.

In the early Church, the universal festive vesture was unadorned white linen or silk, as witnessed in patristic writings. Over centuries of liturgical development, a symbolic variety of vestment colors emerged. In the Slavic tradition, established liturgical customs prescribe specific colors for particular cycles: white vestments, for[^636]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Liturgical Colors Concluded Part 2) [Demos, p. 1072 / TD-93]

example, are associated with the Paschal season (although in certain local traditions red vestments are donned at Pascha), symbolizing the radiant joy of Christ's resurrection and the destruction of death, echoing the apocalyptic vision: "He that overcomes, the same shall be clothed in white raiment" (Rev. 3:5). Green, signifying life, spiritual rebirth, and renewal, is appointed for Pentecost and the feast of the Holy Trinity, symbolizing the descent of the Holy Spirit, as well as for the feasts of venerable monastic saints who dwelt in the wilderness. Red vestments are worn at the Nativity of Christ, the feast of the Holy Apostles Peter and Paul, and on the commemorations of holy martyrs who shed their blood for Christ. Blue is traditionally appointed for feasts of the Most Holy Theotokos, honoring Her as the Queen of Heaven. Deep purple or dark crimson is worn during the Great Fast as a solemn remembrance of the passion of Christ and the purple robe with which the Roman soldiers mockingly clothed Him before His Crucifixion. Outside of specific festal seasons, gold vestments serve as the universal standard, evoking the heavenly Jerusalem: "and the city was pure gold, like unto clear glass" (Rev. 21:18). Liturgical vesture not only elevates the visual beauty of divine worship, but transforms the ministers—from the presiding hierarch to the youngest acolyte—into living icons of the heavenly court.

Historically, six principal liturgical colors developed in Byzantine practice: white, green, purple, red, blue, and gold. In later centuries, black vestments were introduced, particularly for weekday services in Great Lent. In various regional customs, amber, saffron, or rust-colored vestments are also utilized. Liturgical commentators have attributed rich theological symbolism to these colors: white represents the uncreated light of the Divine Energies; green represents the life-creating power of the Holy Spirit and the life-giving Cross; purple signifies the suffering and sovereignty of Christ; deep red recalls the Precious Blood shed upon the Cross and the witness of the martyrs; blue honors the virginity and heavenly glory of the Mother of God; and gold represents the splendor of the Kingdom of God and the grace of the Holy Spirit. While in ancient Eastern tradition white was the mourning color of Christian burial—signifying the hope of resurrection—black became widespread in the West and eventually entered Slavic funeral usage. In traditional Ruthenian and Slavic lands, red (*krasny*) is the color of festal majesty, spiritual beauty, and joy. None of these specific color systems is mandated in the statutory text of the Typikon, and diverse local traditions preserve distinctive usages.[^637]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Liturgical Colors Appendix – Liturgical Year – Liturgy Part 1) [Demos, p. 1073 / TD-94]

For an extensive historical and rubrical documentation of liturgical color traditions across Eastern and Oriental Christian traditions, see scholarly and comparative studies on ecclesiastical vesture.

**LITURGICAL YEAR** (*Λειτουργικὸν ἔτος, τό*): The annual cycle of ecclesiastical seasons, festal periods, fasts, and commemorations in the Eastern Church governing the celebration of feasts, memorials, daily scriptural lections, and proper hymnography. In the Byzantine Rite, the liturgical year commences on September 1, designated the Feast of the Indiction and the beginning of the Ecclesiastical New Year, and concludes on August 31. Distinct liturgical colors and propers correspond to each liturgical cycle throughout the church year.

The liturgical calendars of the Christian East and West share a common historical foundation and structural logic, though divergent calendar reckonings (Julian and Gregorian) and computational methods for calculating the date of Pascha create variations in festal dates. Feasts that fall upon a fixed calendar date every year (such as the Nativity of Christ on December 25 or the Dormition on August 15) are designated **Immovable Feasts** (*᾿Ακίνητοι ἑορταί*), whereas feasts whose annual celebration depends upon the moveable date of Holy Pascha (such as the Ascension, Pentecost, and all services of the Triodion and Pentecostarion) are designated **Moveable Feasts** (*Κινηταὶ ἑορταί*).

**LITURGY** (*Λειτουργία, ἡ*): In general Christian usage, liturgy signifies the public, official prayer and sacramental worship of the Church. The word derives from the classical Greek composite *leitourgia* (*λειτουργία*, from *laos* / *leos*, "people", and *ergon*, "work"), originally denoting a public civic duty or service rendered by an individual citizen for the welfare of the commonwealth. In Christian theology and Byzantine liturgical terminology, the term is consecrated pre-eminently to designate the celebration of the Holy Eucharist—the Divine Liturgy. The Byzantine Rite celebrates several distinct Eucharistic liturgies:

1) **The Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom** (Archbishop of Constantinople, c. 398): The standard and most frequently celebrated liturgy of the Byzantine Rite, celebrated on the majority of days throughout the liturgical year, and appointed as a Vesperal Liturgy on the[^638]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Liturgy Continued Part 2) [Demos, p. 1074 / TD-95]

Feast of the Annunciation of the Most Holy Theotokos when it falls on certain weekdays.

2) **The Divine Liturgy of our Father among the Saints, Basil the Great** (Archbishop of Caesarea in Cappadocia, 370–379): Ancient patristic tradition affirms that the expansive anaphoral prayers and sacerdotal texts of this Liturgy represent the authentic composition of Saint Basil the Great, developed and enriched over centuries of liturgical transmission. This venerable liturgy is celebrated ten times during the ecclesiastical year, namely:
- On the Feast of Saint Basil the Great (January 1);
- On the first five Sundays of the Great Fast (Lent);
- On Great and Holy Thursday (celebrated jointly with Great Vespers);
- On Great and Holy Saturday (celebrated jointly with Great Vespers);
- On the Paramony (Eve) of the Nativity of Christ (celebrated with Vespers);
- On the Paramony (Eve) of the Holy Theophany (celebrated with Vespers).

When the Great Feasts of the Nativity of Christ or Holy Theophany fall on a Sunday or Monday, the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great is celebrated on the day of the feast itself, while the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is celebrated on the Eve (Paramony).

3) **The Divine Liturgy of Saint Mark**: The ancient Alexandrian Liturgy, historically utilized in Egypt.

4) **The Divine Liturgy of Saint James the Brother of the Lord**: The venerable Jerusalem Liturgy, historically attributed to Saint James the Just, the first Bishop of Jerusalem (not to be confused with Saint James the Apostle, brother of Saint John the Theologian). Today, this ancient hierarchical liturgy is celebrated on the Feast of Saint James (October 23) in Jerusalem and, by episcopal blessing, in various cathedrals and theological seminaries.

5) **The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts**: See below.

Among these liturgical rites, the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom represents an anaphoral abbreviation and condensation of the earlier liturgical tradition exemplified in the Liturgy of Saint Basil, both of which share ancestral roots with the ancient Liturgy of Saint James of Jerusalem.[^639]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Liturgy Concluded Part 3 – Lonchi – Makarismos, Makarismoi – Mandyas – Mandylion – Master Icons) [Demos, p. 1075 / TD-96]

**The Liturgy of the Presanctified Gifts**: Celebrated on Wednesdays and Fridays of the Great Fast, on Thursday of the Great Canon, and on the first three days of Great and Holy Week (Monday, Tuesday, and Wednesday). This rite is structurally an Office of Solemn Vespers conjoined to a communion rite, wherein the faithful receive the Holy Mysteries previously consecrated at the Divine Liturgy on the preceding Sunday and reserved upon the Holy Table. Sacerdotal tradition attributes the compilation and ordering of this service to Saint Gregory the Dialogist (Pope of Rome).

The Great Fast is fundamentally an "aliturgical" season on weekdays, meaning that the full celebration of the Eucharistic sacrifice with its festal, resurrectal character is precluded on ordinary Lenten days and reserved for Saturdays and Sundays. The celebration of the Presanctified Liturgy was instituted to strengthen the faithful through frequent reception of Holy Communion during their ascetic struggle without interrupting the penitential character of the fast.

**LONCHI** (*Λόγχη, ἡ*; Slav. *Kopíye* / *Копіе* / Lance or Spear): A sacred liturgical implement consisting of a small, double-edged steel blade mounted upon a wooden or metal handle terminating in a cross. The priest employs the holy Lance during the Office of Preparation (**Proskomide**) to excise the **Lamb** (*᾿Αμνός*) and particles from the loaves of **Prosphora** (*Προσφορά*). The piercing action of the Lance mystically commemorates the Roman soldier piercing the side of Christ hanging upon the Cross, from which flowed blood and water (John 19:34).

**MAKARISMOS, MAKARISMOI** (*Μακαρισμός, ὁ· pl. Μακαρισμοί, οἱ*; Slav. *Blazhenni* / *Блаженни*): The Beatitudes; the eight evangelical blessings pronounced by Christ in His Sermon on the Mount (Matt. 5:3–12). In the Byzantine Rite, the Beatitudes constitute the standard Third Antiphon at the Divine Liturgy, interspersed with Troparia from the Oktoechos or the feast. The term also designates the formal patriarchal address "Your Beatitude" (*Μακαριώτατε*). Special hymnographic Beatitudes are likewise appointed for specific solemn offices, such as the Twelve Passion Gospels on the evening of Great and Holy Thursday. See **BEATITUDES**.

**MANDYAS** (*Μανδύας, ὁ*; Slav. *Mantiya* / *Мантія*): The monastic mantle or episcopal cloak, worn by bishops as their solemn non-eucharistic and processional vesture. Crafted of rich purple or crimson fabric, the episcopal mandyas features long flowing trains, red and white horizontal stripes representing the streams of divine teaching (*potamoi* / *струи*), and four embroidered square tablets or medallions at the neck and hem depicting the Four Holy Evangelists (or seraphim). It fastens at the neck and lower front. (See also under **VESTMENTS: NON-LITURGICAL ATTIRE OF THE CLERGY**).

**MANDYLION** (*Μανδήλιον, τό*): See **AGION MANDYLION**.

**MASTER ICONS** (*Δεσποτικαὶ εἰκόνες, αἱ*): The principal tier of four foundational icons enshrined upon the lower tier of the Iconostasis (**Ikonostasion**), flanking the Holy Doors: (1) Christ the Teacher; (2) the Most Holy Theotokos; (3) Saint John the Forerunner and Baptist (or the patronal icon); and (4) the Icon of the Patron Saint or Mystery of the Temple.[^640]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Matins Part 1) [Demos, p. 1076 / TD-97]

**MATINS** (*ὄρθρος, ὁ*; Slav. *Utrenya* / *Утреня*): Matins is a Western term which, in Greek tradition, is designated by the Greek term **Orthros** (*Ὄρθρος*), signifying "early dawn" or "daybreak." As an integral part of the daily cycle of services, it constitutes the final office among the four nocturnal offices, which encompass Vespers, Compline, the Midnight Office, and Matins. In traditional monasteries it is celebrated daily in the early morning so as to conclude at sunrise.

Matins is the longest and most complex service of the daily cycle. It is normally celebrated in the early morning, on occasion preceded by the Midnight Office, and regularly followed immediately by the First Hour. On Great Feasts it is celebrated as the principal component of an All-Night Vigil on the preceding evening, conjoined with Great Vespers and the First Hour. In the Greek parish tradition, Matins is normally celebrated in the morning immediately preceding the Divine Liturgy. In the Slavic tradition, an All-Night Vigil is celebrated on every Saturday evening preceding Sunday.

The fixed psalmody of the service comprises primarily the Six Psalms, fixed morning psalms, and litanies. The variable hymnography of Matins is drawn principally from the canons and hymns of the **Paraklitiki** (**Oktoechos** / *᾿Οκτώηχος*), the liturgical volume containing the hymnographic cycle for each of the eight Tones across each day of the week, cycling over eight weeks, and from the **Menaion** (*Μηναῖον*, pl. **Menaia** / *Μηναῖα*), the twelve monthly volumes comprising the propers for each calendar day of the ecclesiastical year. During the Great Fast (Lent), portions of the Oktoechos and Menaion are superseded by the proper hymnography of the **Triodion** (*Τριῴδιον*), and during the Paschal Season by the joyful hymnography of the **Pentecostarion** (*Πεντηκοστάριον*). On Sundays, Matins also features Gospel lections and proper hymns from the eleven-part cycle of Resurrectional Gospels (*῾Εωθινὰ Εὐαγγέλια*).

For an exhaustive and authoritative presentation of the rubrical order of Matins across all occasions of the Ecclesiastical Year, see the following liturgical sources:
1. The present Typikon of George Violakis:
    a. The *Protheoria* (*Προθεωρία*) for the general principles governing the order of divine services;[^641]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Matins Concluded Part 2 – Mega Kiropigion – Mega Synodikon – Megali Oktoichos – Megali Synapti – Megalynarion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1077 / TD-98]

    b. The specific festal rubrics set forth for each immovable and moveable feast throughout the liturgical year.
2. The foundational liturgical books: the **Paraklitiki**, the twelve volumes of the **Menaia**, the **Triodion**, and the **Pentecostarion**.
3. The **Ieratikon** (Slav. **Sluzhebnik** / *Служебникъ*).
4. The **Engolpion tu Anagnostu** (*᾿Εγκόλπιον τοῦ ᾿Αναγνώστου*, Reader's Handbook).
5. The **Megas ke Ieros Synekdimos** (*Μέγας καὶ ῾Ιερὸς Συνέκδημος*, Great and Sacred Vade Mecum).

**MEGA KIROPIGION** (*Μέγα κηροπήγιον, τό*): A large floor candelabrum or candle stand, typically positioned upon the Soleas before the Iconostasis.

**MEGA SYNODIKON** (*Μέγα Συνοδικόν, τό*): A Synodikon is defined as a formal conciliar decree and synodal proclamation promulgated in churches summarizing the dogmatic definitions and canonical decisions of a council of bishops. With the qualifying epithet *Mega* ("Great"), it designates pre-eminently the solemn liturgical text proclaimed in the Eastern Church on the Sunday of Orthodoxy (the First Sunday of the Great Fast), originally compiled in 843 under Patriarch Methodios I upon the definitive triumph over Iconoclasm.

**MEGALI OKTOICHOS** (*Μεγάλη ᾿Οκτώηχος*): The Great Oktoechos; synonymous with the **PARAKLITIKI**.

**MEGALI SYNAPTI** (*Μεγάλη Συναπτή, ἡ*): The Great Synapte; the Great Litany or Litany of Peace, consisting of an expansive series of petitions chanted by the deacon and concluded with the priestly doxology.

**MEGALYNARION** (*Μεγαλυνάριον, τό*; Slav. *Velichanie* / *Величаніе*): A hymn of praise and magnification in honor of the Most Holy Theotokos or a sacred feast, chanted following the epiklesis and the hymn **"We praise Thee, we bless Thee"** (*Σὲ ὑμνοῦμεν*) during the Anaphora of the Divine Liturgy. The Megalynarion is frequently the model strophe or **Heirmos** (*Εἱρμός*) of the Ninth Ode of the Canon appointed at Matins for the feast, such as **"Shine, shine, O New Jerusalem"** (*Φωτίζου, φωτίζου*) from the Ninth Ode of the Paschal Canon. On other occasions, the Megalynarion is an independent festive composition, such as **"All of creation rejoices in thee"** (*«᾿Επὶ σοὶ χαίρει»*) chanted at the Divine Liturgy of Saint Basil the Great. The Megalynarion standardly chanted at the Divine Liturgy of Saint John Chrysostom is **"It is truly meet"** (*«῎Αξιόν ἐστιν»*).[^642]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Megalynarion Concluded Part 2 – Megalynarion at the Diptycha – Megas Archidiakonos – Megas Archimandritis – Megas Ekklisiarchis – Megas Primikirios – Megas Protosyngkellos) [Demos, p. 1078 / TD-99]

Furthermore, the Megalynarion customarily incorporates either at its beginning or near its conclusion the verb *megalynomen* (*μεγαλύνομεν*, "we magnify").

**MEGALYNARION AT THE DIPTYCHA** (*Μεγαλυνάριον εἰς τὰ Δίπτυχα*): A proper Megalynarion dedicated to a specific saint or sacred commemoration, chanted in addition to the customary Megalynarion of the Divine Liturgy. It is sung by the choir immediately preceding its liturgical response **"And each and all"** (*«Καὶ πάντων καὶ πασῶν»*). For the proper rubrical order, see the *Ieratikon* (ed. Apostoliki Diakonia, Athens, 1977 and 1987, p. 182). The *Engolpion tu Anagnostu* (*Manual of the Church Reader*, ed. Apostoliki Diakonia, Athens, c. 1970, p. 83) records a modern variant custom wherein the Megalynarion at the Diptycha is sung immediately preceding the priest's intercessory petition: **"Remember also, Lord, those whom each of us calls to mind and all Your people"** (*«Καὶ ὧν ἕκαστος κατὰ διάνοιαν ἔχει καὶ πάντων καὶ πασῶν»*). The Typikon of George Violakis references only two such Diptych Megalynaria: for Saint Basil the Great on January 1 (pp. 137–138, § 3), and for the Life-Giving Spring (**Zoodochos Pege** / *Ζωοδόχος Πηγή*) on Bright Friday following Pascha (pp. 374–375, § 8). The *Engolpion tu Anagnostu*, by contrast, provides several additional compositions, composed after the publication of the 1888 Typikon.

**MEGAS ARCHIDIAKONOS** (*Μέγας ᾿Αρχιδιάκονος, ὁ*): The Great Archdeacon; the premier ranking deacon of the Patriarchal court or cathedral.

**MEGAS ARCHIMANDRITIS** (*Μέγας ᾿Αρχιμανδρίτης, ὁ*): The Great Archimandrite; an exalted patriarchal dignitary assisting in the administration of the Great Church.

**MEGAS EKKLISIARCHIS** (*Μέγας ᾿Εκκλησιάρχης, ὁ*): The Great Ecclesiarches; the chief liturgical master of ceremonies and sacristan charged with directing the typikon and liturgical order of monastic and cathedral offices.

**MEGAS PRIMIKIRIOS** (*Μέγας Πριμικήριος, ὁ*): The Great Primikirios; the chief ceremonial dean and director of tapers in the patriarchal court.

**MEGAS PROTOSYNGKELLOS** (*Μέγας Πρωτοσύγκελλος, ὁ*): The Great Protosyngellos; the Grand Chancellor and Vicar General of the Patriarchate, empowered to administer ecclesiastical affairs and officiate liturgical services in the absence of the Patriarch.[^643]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Megas Sakellarios – Megas Synaxaristis – Mesonyktikon – Mesopentikosti – Metania – Metastasis – Metheorta Part 1) [Demos, p. 1079 / TD-100]

**MEGAS SAKELLARIOS** (*Μέγας Σακελλάριος, ὁ*): The Great Sakellarios; the high Byzantine ecclesiastical dignitary supervising the imperial or patriarchal treasury, financial administration, and the records and properties of stavropegial and imperial monasteries.

**MEGAS SYNAXARISTIS** (*Μέγας Συναξαριστής, ὁ*): The Great Synaxaristes; a monumental twelve-volume compilation of the lives of the holy saints, arranged sequentially according to their calendar commemorations across each month of the ecclesiastical year. A concise abridgment of these hagiographical biographies is contained in the liturgical book entitled the **Synaxarion** (*Συναξάριον*), and corresponding summaries are likewise printed within the twelve volumes of the **Menaia**.

**MESONYKTIKON** (*Μεσονυκτικόν, τό*; Slav. *Polunoshchnitsa* / *Полунощница*): The Midnight Office; a foundational liturgical office of the Daily Cycle appointed to be recited at midnight or in the early morning vigil in Orthodox and Eastern Catholic tradition.

**MESOPENTIKOSTI** (*Μεσοπεντηκοστή, ἡ*; Slav. *Prepolovenie Piatidesiatnitsy* / *Преполовеніе Пятидесятницы*): Mid-Pentecost; the feast marking the midpoint of the fifty-day joyful season between Holy Pascha and Pentecost Sunday. The Feast of Mid-Pentecost is celebrated on Wednesday following the Sunday of the Paralytic (the Fourth Week of Pascha).

**METANIA** (*μετάνοια, ἡ*; Slav. *poklon* / *поклонъ*): An external bodily act of penitence and reverence, signifying spiritual repentance. The term designates either a waist bow (lesser metania) wherein the fingers of the right hand touch the ground, or a full prostration (great metania) cast upon the pavement in humble adoration and penitence before the Holy Mysteries or holy icons.

**METASTASIS** (*μετάστασις, ἡ*): See **TRANSLATION** (*μετάστασις, ἡ*; repose or translation unto Heaven).

**METHEORTA** (*μεθέορτα, τά*), **METHEORTIA** (*μεθεόρτια, τά*; Slav. *Poprazdnstvo* / *Попраздνство*): The Greek neuter plural denoting the festal period of the **Afterfeast** (lit. 'post-feast'): This is a liturgical season appended to a Great Feast celebrated in the Byzantine Rite, extending from one to eight days following the principal feast day, during which the mystery of the feast continues to be commemorated in the daily hymnography and prayers. This festal cycle corresponds functionally to the Western liturgical octave. The final concluding day of this festal period is[^644]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Metheorta Concluded Part 2 – Metheorton – Midnight Service – Mild Chromatic Scale – Mineon, Minea – Minologion – Miracle-Worker – Mitra) [Demos, p. 1080 / TD-101]

designated the **Apodosis** (*᾿Απόδοσις, ἡ*; lit. 'leave-taking'), signifying the liturgical completion and return of the feast.

**METHEORTON** (*μεθέορτον*): The grammatical adjective designating a hymn as belonging to the festal period of a feast's Afterfeast (*Metheorta* or *Metheortia*), as in a Metheorton Dismissal Troparion (lit. 'Metheorton Apolytikion'), Metheorton Kontakion, or Metheorton Canon. See also **AFTERFEAST** (lit. 'post-feast').

**MIDNIGHT SERVICE**: See **MESONYKTIKON**.

**MILD CHROMATIC SCALE** (*μαλακὴ χρωματικὴ κλῖμαξ*): The Soft Chromatic Scale in Byzantine musical theory. See the introductory treatise on liturgical chant.

**MINEON, pl. MINEA** (*Μηναῖον, τό· Μηναῖα, τά*; Slav. *Mineya* / *Минея*): The **Menaion**, derived from the Greek noun for "month" (*μήν*). The Menaia comprise the liturgical volumes containing the variable offices for each calendar day of the twelve months of the liturgical year. For smaller parishes lacking the complete twelve-volume set of the Menaia, there exists in both Greek and Slavonic tradition a single-volume compendium containing common offices for each class of saint, designated the **General Menaion** (*᾿Ανθολόγιον* / Slav. *Obshtaya Mineya* / *Общая Минея*). The Greek and Slavonic General Menaia diverge in content: the Slavonic edition provides expanded propers, including complete All-Night Vigil texts for each order of saint, as well as common propers for feasts of the Lord and the Mother of God.

**MINOLOGION** (*Μηνολόγιον, τό*): The **Menologion**; an ecclesiastical calendar documenting the daily cycle of Immovable Feasts across the twelve months of the liturgical year.

**MIRACLE-WORKER** (*θαυματουργός, ὁ*; Slav. *Chudotvorets* / *Чудотворецъ*): Wonderworker; an honorary epithet bestowed upon a holy saint renowned for performing miracles of divine grace during earthly life or through holy relics after repose, such as Saint Charalambos the Hieromartyr (commemorated February 10).

**MITRA** (*μίτρα, ἡ*; Slav. *Venets* / *Вѣнецъ* or *Mitra* / *Митра*): The Byzantine Miter or Crown; an ornate, domed liturgical head covering worn by bishops (and by archimandrites or mitred archpriests by special award). It is richly embellished with embroidered or enameled icons of Christ and the Four Evangelists and decorated with precious jewels and pearls, surmounted by the Holy Cross. It symbolizes the Crown of Thorns, the imperial majesty of the King of Kings, and the episcopal authority conferred upon the hierarch at his consecration.[^645]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Mode / Tone Part 1: Irmologic, Sticheraric, Papadic Styles; Octoichos Origins) [Demos, p. 1081 / TD-102]

**MODE** (*ἦχος, ὁ*; Slav. *Glas*, pl. *glasy* / *Гласъ*): The musical and liturgical **Tone**. A musical Mode in the Byzantine system is defined by: (a) a specific scale of melodic intervals, and (b) a characteristic corpus of melodic formulae and cadential figures (Slav. *popevki*). A mode is likewise governed by its distinct melodic range (*ἔκτασις*), opening intonation formula (*ἀπήχημα*), foundational base tone or ison (*βάσις, ἶσον*), dominant tones (*δεσπόζοντες φθόγγοι*), modal and cadential signature marks (*μαρτυρίαι*), and modal modulation signs (*φθοραί*). The liturgical melodies within each mode are composed in three distinct melodic genres or styles:

1. **Heirmologic** (*εἱρμολογικόν* / lit. 'irmologic'): The melodies are strictly syllabic in character, wherein each syllable of the sacred text corresponds to a single musical interval (or at most two beats). They are chanted in a brisk, rapid tempo (*allegretto* to *vivace*). A prime exemplar of the Heirmologic style is the musical setting appointed for the eight Resurrection Dismissal Troparia (lit. 'Apolytikia') chanted at the Small Entrance of the Divine Liturgy.

2. **Sticheraric** (*στιχηραρικόν*): The melodies are moderately ornate and neumatic compared to the Heirmologic genre, with each syllable sustained across two to three beats. The tempo ranges generally from *allegretto* to *andante*. The Sticheraric style is employed for the Lucernarium stichera (**"Lord, I have cried"** / *Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*), the Praises (*Ainoi* / *Pasapnoaria*) at Matins, festal stichera, and select festal Megalynaria at the Divine Liturgy.

3. **Papadic** (*παπαδικόν*): The melodies are highly melismatic, expansive, and elaborate. The tempo is stately and slow, though certain ornate passages are executed more rapidly. The Cherubic Hymn (*Χερουβικόν*) and the Communion Hymns (*Κοινωνικά*) serve as classic models of the Papadic genre. The particular melodic style chosen alters and expands the modal characteristics outlined above.

This eightfold modal classification system in Byzantine chant and hymnology gave rise to the weekly cycle of the Eight Tones. As early as the sixth century, these liturgical hymns were compiled into the hymnographic collection known as the **Oktoechos** (*᾿Οκτώηχος*), which was systematically edited and perfected in the eighth century by our venerable Father, Saint John of Damascus.[^646]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (The Cycle of Modes Part 2: Weekly Oktoechos Cycle, Paschal Sequence) [Demos, p. 1082 / TD-103]

### The Cycle of the Eight Tones (Modes)

In Byzantine liturgical music, eight canonical Tones are recognized: Tone 1 (Mode I), Tone 2 (Mode II), Tone 3 (Mode III), Tone 4 (Mode IV), Tone 5 (Mode I Plagal), Tone 6 (Mode II Plagal), Tone 7 (Varys / Grave Tone), and Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal). All hymnography of the Byzantine liturgical tradition is set within one of these eight Tones. The Eight Tones constitute the foundational architectural framework around which the Church organizes the daily offices and their hymnographic cycles throughout the ecclesiastical year. This structural logic is codified within the principal liturgical book of the daily cycle, the **Paraklitiki** (**Megali Oktoichos** / *Μεγάλη ᾿Οκτώηχος*).

The Paraklitiki contains the appointed services and propers for every day of the week across the year. It is divided into eight modal sections or tone periods (*περίοδοι τῶν ἤχων*), each comprising the complete services of a full week from Sunday through Saturday. Each of the eight modal sections is sung in one of the eight Tones, advancing sequentially from Tone 1 through Tone 8. When the entire eight-week sequence has concluded, the cycle recommences with Tone 1, repeating continuously throughout the church year.

This annual cycle of Tones is solemnly inaugurated on Holy Pascha with Tone 1, as evidenced by the predominant use of Tone 1 in the Praises (**Ainoi** / *Αἶνοι*) of the Paschal Matins preceding the Great Doxology. During Bright Week, the daily services draw upon the Paraklitiki in a unique daily progression:
- Bright Monday: Tone 2 (Mode II);
- Bright Tuesday: Tone 3 (Mode III);
- Bright Wednesday: Tone 4 (Mode IV);
- Bright Thursday: Tone 5 (Mode I Plagal);
- Bright Friday: Tone 6 (Mode II Plagal);
- Tone 7 (Varys / Grave Tone) is omitted during Bright Week;
- Bright Saturday: Tone 8 (Mode IV Plagal).

On the Second Sunday of Pascha (Sunday of Saint Thomas), the weekly cycle returns to Tone 1. From that day forward, the modal pattern shifts weekly, changing at Great Vespers on Saturday evening before each Sunday. Thus, the Third Sunday of Pascha (Sunday of the Myrrh-Bearing Women) is appointed in Tone 2, and so forth in unbroken progression through the subsequent Sundays until Tone 8 is completed. Upon reaching Tone 1 again, a new modal cycle begins, repeating cyclically until the Fifth Sunday of the Great Fast of the following year. Palm Sunday,[^647]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (The Cycle of Modes Concluded Part 3 – Movable Feast – Myrofori – Myrovlitis – Myrrh – Myrrh Bearers – Myrrh-Exuding – Mysteries Part 1) [Demos, p. 1083 / TD-104]

which has no single Tone assigned to its festal services, interrupts the regular Oktoechos cycle. The ensuing Feast of Holy Pascha initiates an entirely new annual cycle beginning once more with Tone 1.

**MOVEABLE FEAST** (*κινητὴ ἑορτή, ἡ*): A liturgical feast whose celebration is not tied to a fixed calendar date of the civil or ecclesiastical year. Moveable feasts are governed by the liturgical cycles of the **Triodion** and **Pentecostarion**, which together establish the liturgical framework of the Paschal cycle. This framework commences on the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee and concludes on the Sunday of All Saints (the First Sunday after Pentecost). Every moveable feast stands in a fixed temporal relationship to the Sunday of Holy Pascha, which may occur between March 22 and April 25 on the Julian Reckoning (corresponding to April 4 and May 8 on the revised calendar). The precise calendar date for any moveable feast depends directly upon the date of Pascha in that particular year. (For the earliest and latest possible calendar limits of all moveable feasts, see scholarly liturgical tables and typikon guides).

**MYROFORI** (*μυροφόροι, αἱ*, pl.): The Holy **Myrrh-Bearers** (Myrrh-bearing women).

**MYROVLITIS** (*μυροβλήτης, ὁ*): Myrrh-exuding or Myrrh-streaming; an honorary title bestowed upon a holy saint whose relics or tomb miraculously stream sweet-smelling, healing myrrh (*myron*), such as Saint Demetrios the Great Martyr of Thessaloniki (commemorated October 26).

**MYRRH** (*μῦρον, τό*): See **CHRISM** (Holy Myron / *῞Αγιον Μῦρον*).

**MYRRH-BEARERS** (*μυροφόροι, αἱ*): The holy women who brought aromatic spices, fragrant ointments, and perfumes to the tomb of Christ early on the first day of the week to anoint His sacred Body. Foremost among them were Saint Mary Magdalene, Mary the wife of Cleopas (mother of James), Salome the mother of the Sons of Zebedee, Joanna, and the sisters Martha and Mary of Bethany.

**MYRRH-EXUDING**: See **MYROVLITIS**.

**MYSTERIES** (*Μυστήρια, τά*; *῞Αγια Μυστήρια*): The sacred Mysteries or Holy Sacraments of the Church. The term encompasses the foundational dogmatic mysteries of divine revelation as well as the holy sacra-[^648]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Mysteries Concluded Part 2 – Naos – Narthex Part 1) [Demos, p. 1084 / TD-105]

mental rites through which the grace of the Holy Spirit is imparted unto the faithful. These transcendent mysteries exceed the comprehension of human intellect and are embraced through living faith. Central among Christian mysteries is the mystery of the Holy Incarnation, wherein the eternal Word of God assumed human nature from the Virgin Mary, uniting the divine and human natures in the one Divine Hypostasis of Jesus Christ. Another supreme mystery is the Glorious Resurrection: God, the Author of life, transcending non-existence, conquered death by death and bestowed resurrection upon mankind.

A third profound mystery is the divine action of the Holy Mysteries in uniting man unto God. In the Holy Eucharist, humble earthly gifts of bread and wine are transformed by the Holy Spirit into the true Body and the true Blood of Christ. Pronouncing over the bread and the chalice at the Mystical Supper: **"This is My Body... This is My Blood"**, Christ Himself instituted the Sacrament of Holy Communion for all generations. By receiving the Holy Mysteries, the Christian is united unto Christ, becoming a partaker of the Divine Nature through grace. The Church recognizes seven principal Holy Mysteries: Baptism, Chrismation, the Eucharist (Holy Communion), Holy Orders (Priesthood), Repentance (Confession), Holy Unction (Anointing of the Sick), and Holy Matrimony (Crowning). Through these Holy Mysteries, man is consecrated in faith and united unto God from birth until the conclusion of his earthly life.

**NAOS** (*ναός, ὁ*): The holy temple or church building. In architectural terminology, it specifically designates the central space of the temple, the **Nave**, reserved for the assembly of the faithful. See also **EKKLISIA**.

**NARTHEX** (*νάρθηξ, ὁ*; Slav. *Pritvor* / *Притворъ*): The entrance vestibule of the church building. The traditional Byzantine temple comprises three distinct liturgical areas: the Sanctuary (**Bema**), the Nave (**Naos**), and the Narthex (**Pritvor**). The Narthex serves as the entrance foyer through which the faithful enter the holy temple. In Byzantine ecclesiastical architecture, the narthex is frequently divided into two parts: an outer porch (**exonarthex**) forming the exterior facade of the building, and an inner vestibule (**esonarthex**) opening directly into the nave through the royal doors. In the narthex, the faithful offer prayers for the living and[^649]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Narthex Concluded Part 2 – Nave – Nekrosimon – Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed – Now and Ever) [Demos, p. 1085 / TD-106]

the departed, light tapers, and reverence holy icons. In early Christian centuries, the Narthex fulfilled essential liturgical functions: on Sundays and feast days it was occupied by catechumens preparing for Holy Baptism and by penitents performing public penance, who followed the sacred services from this outer station. The poor and infirm gathered in the narthex to receive alms from the faithful. Ecclesiastical history records instances where holy hierarchs and pastors stood in the narthex to bar the entrance of heretics and unrepentant sinners from entering the church proper.

During centuries of Ottoman subjection in Greece and under atheist Communist regimes throughout Eastern Europe, the Narthex served an additional sacred purpose: faithful children gathered there at night to receive secret instruction in the Christian faith and the native language from faithful clergy. In contemporary church life, while catechumens and public penitents no longer occupy separate stations in the narthex, it remains a space for prayerful preparation, purchasing candles, acquiring sacred books, prayer ropes, prosphora seals, and making charitable offerings for church benevolence.

**NAVE** (*ναός, ὁ*): The central hall of the church building situated between the Narthex (vestibule) and the Sanctuary. In Eastern Christian theology, the tripartite structure of the church building (Sanctuary, Nave, and Narthex) mirrors the tripartite structure of the ancient Tabernacle and Solomon's Temple (Holy of Holies, Holy Place, and Outer Court). While the Holy of Holies was entered exclusively by the High Priest once a year on the Day of Atonement, the Christian Sanctuary enshrines the Holy Table where the Eucharistic Sacrifice is continually offered, while the faithful assemble within the Nave to participate in the sacred mysteries.

**NEKROSIMON** (*Νεκρώσιμον, τό*, pl. *Νεκρώσιμα, τά*): A funeral or memorial hymn, or an entire liturgical office chanted in commemoration of the reposed faithful.

**NICENE-CONSTANTINOPOLITAN CREED** (*Σύμβολον τῆς Πίστεως*): The Symbol of Faith. See **CREED**.

**NOW AND EVER** (*Καὶ νῦν*): The opening incipit of the final clause of the minor doxology: **"Both now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*Καὶ νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.*). See **KE NYN**.[^650]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Oblation – Ode – Oktoechos – Olonyktia – Omophorion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1086 / TD-107]

**OBLATION** (from Latin *oblatum*, "offering"): The sacred act of offering and preparing the Eucharistic gifts of bread and wine during the office of the **Proskomide** (**Proskomidi** / *Προσκομιδή*), which is celebrated upon the Table of **Prothesis** (*Πρόθεσις*) or Oblation.

**ODE** (*ᾠδή, ἡ*): See under **CANTICLE**.

**OKTOECHOS** (*᾿Οκτώηχος, ἡ*) or **GREAT OKTOECHOS** (**OCHTOICHOS I MEGALI** / *᾿Οκτώηχος ἡ Μεγάλη*; Slav. *Oktoikh* / *Октоихъ*, *Osmoglasnik* / *Осмогласникъ*): See **PARAKLITIKI**.

**OLONYKTIA** (*ὁλονυκτία, ἡ*): All-night prayer and vigil without sleep. See **VIGIL**.

**OMOPHORION** (**OMOFORION** / *ὠμοφόριον, τό*; Slav. *Omofor* / *Омофоръ*, *Omoforij* / *Омофорій*, *Nadramenik* / *Надраменникъ*): See also **VESTMENTS**.

The distinguishing liturgical vestment of a bishop, worn over the shoulders, and the supreme emblem of his spiritual and pastoral authority. Originally fashioned from unbleached white sheep's wool, in modern usage it is tailored from rich liturgical brocade embroidered with crosses, worn encircling the neck and draped across the shoulders. Symbolizing the lost sheep that the Good Shepherd sought out and carried home upon His sacred shoulders, it signifies the bishop's pastoral vocation as a living icon of Christ.

Clergy and ecclesiastical institutions subject to a bishop's canonical governance and episcopal protection are formally designated as being "under his Omophorion."

While every Eastern bishop vests in the Omophorion, a corresponding vestment in Western Christianity is the archiepiscopal *pallium*, whose liturgical employment is governed by distinct canonical rubrics and limitations.

The Omophorion exists in two distinct forms: the ancient **Great Omophorion** (*μέγα ὠμοφόριον*), which passes around the neck, folds across the breast, and descends past the knees in both front and back; and the **Small Omophorion** (*μικρὸν ὠμοφόριον*), which is simpler in form, passing around the neck and descending in front similar to an **Epitrachilion** (stole), yet broader and shorter, reaching just past the waist. Owing to the intricate arrangement of the Great Omophorion and the liturgical dignity of the episcopal rank, whenever the bishop vests in this Omophorion or removes it, he is assisted by two subdeacons.[^651]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Omophorion Concluded Part 2) [Demos, p. 1087 / TD-108]

Whenever he presides at any divine service, the bishop vests in the Omophorion. If he celebrates the Divine Liturgy, he wears both the Great and the Small Omophorion at different appointed times over his sacred vestments. At any divine office other than the Divine Liturgy, he customarily wears the Small Omophorion.

During the Divine Liturgy, the rubrics prescribe that the bishop vest in and remove the Omophorion repeatedly. When first vested before the Liturgy, the subdeacons place the Great Omophorion upon him; subsequently, when the rubric calls for him to resume the Omophorion, it is replaced, for practical convenience, with the Small Omophorion. In local traditions where multiple hierarchs concelebrate, customary usage assigns the Great Omophorion exclusively to the principal presiding celebrant at appointed rubrical moments, while the other concelebrating bishops wear the Small Omophorion throughout the celebration.

**Historical Development:**
In the early Church, the Omophorion consisted of a broad band of white wool embroidered with crosses and draped loosely around the neck, over the shoulders, and across the breast. The modern Roman pallium developed from this primitive Omophorion; however, in the West it evolved over the centuries into a narrow circular woolen band resting upon the shoulders, with short, weighted pendants hanging before and behind. In contemporary times, the papal pallium adopted by Pope Benedict XVI returned closer to the original form of the ancient Omophorion.

In the Christian East, the only structural alteration in the Omophorion has been an increase in its width and the adoption of embroidered silk and gold brocade in place of wool. Distinct historical testimony confirming the Omophorion as the liturgical vestment of the bishop is preserved in the epistles of Saint Isidore of Pelusium (c. 400). At that early date, it was already woven of wool and viewed as an explicit symbol of the pastoral duty of bishops shepherding their spiritual flocks. In the miniature illuminations of an Alexandrian World Chronicle, written probably during the fifth century, pictorial representations of the Omophorion already appear. In later centuries, the same depiction is found upon the celebrated ivory relief of Trier, depicting the solemn translation of holy relics. Among the sacred images dating from the seventh and eighth centuries in which the Omophorion appears are the frescoes uncovered in the church of Santa Maria Antiqua in the Roman Forum. The representation in these frescoes is essentially identical to its modern form.[^652]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Omophorion Development Concluded Part 3 – Omologitis – Opisthamvonos Efchi Part 1) [Demos, p. 1088 / TD-109]

The Omophorion probably evolved from the civil omophorion, a shoulder mantle or dignitary scarf in common civic use in late antiquity. Either the bishops introduced it directly by positive canonical enactment as a liturgical pontifical badge—a humeral cloth resembling the secular omophorion and bearing its name—or the civil omophorion was initially adopted by hierarchs as a simple dignitary ornament without special mystical signification, and in the course of time developed into an exclusively episcopal vestment, ultimately acquiring the canonical character of an episcopal badge of office.

**OMOLOGITIS** (*῾Ομολογητής, ὁ*; Slav. *Ispovednik* / *Исповѣдникъ*): Confessor (a holy saint who publicly bore witness to the faith and suffered imprisonment, torture, or exile without dying in martyrdom).

**OPISTHAMVONOS EFCHI** (*᾿Οπισθάμβωνος Εὐχή, ἡ*; Slav. *Zaamvonnaya Molitva* / *Заамвонная молитва*): The **Prayer behind the Ambo**; the solemn prayer recited by the priest near the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy. It is so named because the priest stands "behind the Ambo" (in the central nave facing eastward) and faces the Master Icon of Christ (*Δεσποτικὴ εἰκὼν τοῦ Χριστοῦ*) immediately to the right of the Holy Doors (the Beautiful Gate or Royal Doors). The complete text of this prayer in English and Greek is set forth below:

"Lord, bless those who praise Thee and sanctify those who trust in Thee. Save Thy people and bless Thine inheritance. Preserve the fullness of Thy Church; sanctify those who love the beauty of Thy house. Glorify them in return by Thy divine power, and forsake us not who place our hope in Thee. Grant peace unto Thy world, unto Thy churches, unto the priests, unto our civil authorities, unto the armed forces, and unto all Thy people. For every good gift and every perfect gift is from above, coming down from Thee, the Father of lights. And unto Thee we send up glory, thanksgiving, and worship, to the Father and to the Son and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."

«῾Ο εὐλογῶν τοὺς εὐλογοῦντάς σε, Κύριε, καὶ ἁγιάζων τοὺς ἐπὶ σοὶ πεποιθότας, σῶσον τὸν λαόν σου, καὶ εὐλόγησον τὴν κληρονομίαν σου. Τὸ πλήρωμα τῆς ᾿Εκκλησίας σου φύλαξον· ἁγίασον τοὺς ἀγαπῶντας τὴν εὐπρέπειαν τοῦ Οἴκου σου. Σὺ αὐτοὺς ἀντιδόξασον τῇ θεϊκῇ σου δυνάμει, καὶ μὴ ἐγκαταλίπῃ[^653]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Opisthamvonos Efchi Concluded Part 2 – Orarion – Order of Service – Orea Pili – Orlets – Orologion to Mega – Orthros Part 1) [Demos, p. 1089 / TD-110]

ς ἡμᾶς τοὺς ἐλπίζοντας ἐπὶ σέ. Εἰρήνην τῷ κόσμῳ σου δώρησαι, ταῖς ᾿Εκκλησίαις σου, τοῖς ῾Ιερεῦσι, τοῖς Βασιλεῦσιν ἡμῶν, τῷ Στρατῷ καὶ παντὶ τῷ Λαῷ σου, ὅτι πᾶσα δόσις ἀγαθή, καὶ πᾶν δώρημα τέλειον, ἄνωθέν ἐστι, καταβαῖνον ἐκ σοῦ τοῦ Πατρὸς τῶν φώτων· καὶ σοὶ τὴν δόξαν, καὶ εὐχαριστίαν, καὶ προσκύνησιν ἀναπέμπομεν, τῷ Πατρί, καὶ τῷ Υἱῷ, καὶ τῷ ῾Αγίῳ Πνεύματι, νῦν, καὶ ἀεί, καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν.»

**ORARION** (*᾿Ωράριον, τό*; Slav. *Orar* / *Орарь*): The deacon's stole. See **VESTMENTS**.

**ORDER OF SERVICE** (*τάξις, ἡ*; Slav. *Chinosledovanie* / *Чинослѣдованіе*): See **TYPIKON**.
The canonical sequence of hymns, prayers, and ceremonial actions prescribed for each divine office of the Church, as codified by the Holy Synod of the Patriarchate of Constantinople and set forth in the *Typikon* by George Violakis, published in 1888. Orders of service are likewise contained within the foundational liturgical books of the Church, including the **Menaion** (**Menaia**), the **Triodion**, the **Pentecostarion**, the **Paraklitiki**, the **Euchologion** (**Efchologion** / Slav. **Trebnik**), and the **Ieratikon** (Slav. **Sluzhebnik**). Since its publication, the Typikon has been modified through patriarchal decrees—a living and continuous pastoral practice of the Church.

**OREA PILI** (**OREA PYLI** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη, ἡ*; Slav. *Krasnyya Vrata* / *Красныя Врата*): The **BEAUTIFUL GATE** or **Royal Doors**. This is the central entrance connecting the **Nave** (**Naos**) with the **Sanctuary** (**Bema**), situated in the center of the Iconostasis.

**ORLETS** (*᾿Αετός, ὁ*; Slav. *Orlets* / *Орлецъ*, "eagle rug"): The small circular episcopal carpet depicting an eagle soaring over a city. See **AETOS**.

**OROLOGION TO MEGA** (*῾Ωρολόγιον τὸ Μέγα*; Slav. *Chasoslov* / *Часословъ* or *Velikiy Chasoslov* / *Великій Часословъ*): The **Great Book of Hours** (**Horologion**), containing the fixed texts for the offices of the Daily Cycle, as well as the **Dismissal Troparia** (lit. 'Apolytikia') and **Kontakia** for all immovable and moveable feasts of the ecclesiastical year. It is one of the principal liturgical books of the Eastern Church. Alternate spelling: **Horologion to Mega**. As with the *Euchologion to Mega*, an abbreviated edition is likewise published for reader and parish use.

**ORTHROS** (*Ὄρθρος, ὁ*; Slav. *Utrenya* / *Утреня*): See also **MATINS**. In Greek[^654]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Orthros Concluded Part 2 – Osios, Osia – Osiomartys – Pantokrator – Paraklitiki Part 1) [Demos, p. 1090 / TD-111]

the word signifies "early Morning Prayer" or "daybreak." Orthros precedes the Divine Liturgy and constitutes the longest and most complex office of the daily cycle of services. In monasteries, Orthros is celebrated in the early morning and is regularly followed immediately by the First Hour. On Great Feasts, it is celebrated as an integral part of an All-Night Vigil commencing on the preceding evening, conjoined with Great Vespers and the First Hour. In Eastern parish tradition on Sunday mornings, Orthros is standardly served immediately preceding the Divine Liturgy. The office concludes with the Great Doxology and the festal **Dismissal Troparion** (lit. 'Apolytikion') of the day.

**OSIOS**, **OSIA** (Masc. *Ὅσιος, ὁ*; Fem. *ὁσία, ἡ*; Slav. *Prepodobnyy* / *Преподобный*, *Prepodobnaya* / *Преподобная*): A Venerable or Righteous Monastic Saint. A hagiographical title designating a holy saint who led an ascetical, monastic life, dying of natural causes. The conjoined title **Righteous Martyr** or **Venerable Martyr** (*ὁσιομάρτυς*; Slav. *prepodobnomuchenik*) refers to a martyred monk or nun.

**OSIOMARTYS** (*ὁσιομάρτυς, ὁ*; Slav. *Prepodobnomuchenik* / *Преподобномученикъ*): A Righteous Martyr or Venerable Martyr.

**PANTOKRATOR** (*Παντοκράτωρ, ὁ*; Slav. *Vsederzhitel* / *Вседержитель*; lit. 'He Who holds and sustains all'): The iconographic representation of Christ the Almighty depicted in the apex of the central dome of an Eastern church.

**PARAKLITIKI** or **OKTOICHOS I MEGALI** (*Παρακλητικὴ* ἢ *᾿Οκτώηχος ἡ Μεγάλη*; Slav. *Oktoikh* / *Октоихъ*, *Paraklitik* / *Параклитикъ*): Both Greek titles designate the **GREAT OKTOECHOS**, one of the most foundational and ubiquitous liturgical volumes of the Church. It contains the variable hymnographic parts of the services for all days of the year arranged across the Eight Tones (Modes): 1) Small and Great Vespers, 2) the **Mesonyktikon** (Midnight Office) for Sundays only, 3) **Orthros** (Matins), and 4) the Divine Liturgy. For the chronological and modal structure as well as the liturgical application of the Paraklitiki, see the *Cycle of Modes* (under **MODE** / **TONE**).

It should be noted that the services taken from the *Paraklitiki* for each day are regularly combined with the services of the same calendar day appointed in the **Menaia** (for an Immovable Feast) or with the services of that day appointed in the **Triodion** or **Pentecostarion** (for a Moveable Feast). This[^655]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Paraklitiki Concluded Part 2 – Paramoni – Parartima – Parathrone – Pascha – Paten – Pateritsa – Patriarch – Patriarchiki Pimantoriki Ravdos Part 1) [Demos, p. 1091 / TD-112]

entire process of liturgical combination is regulated by the **Typikon** and its canonical rubrics governing all services of the Church.

**PARAMONI** (*παραμονή, ἡ*; Slav. *Navecherie* / *Навечеріе*, *Sochelnik* / *Сочельникъ*): The **Eve** or Vigil-Eve of a major feast, occurring on the day immediately preceding the feast. The liturgical service of a feast's *paramoni* (eve) acts as a preamble to the feast itself, its hymns, readings, and prayers foretelling and anticipating the impending sacred event.

**PARARTIMA** (*παράρτημα, τό*): Appendix or Supplement.

**PARATHRONE** or **PARATHRONO** (*Παράθρονο, τό*): A secondary throne situated between the Patriarchal Throne and the Right Choir outside the Iconostasis on the south side of the church. This throne is reserved for the Grand Chancellor (**Megas Protosyngkellos**) who officiates in the absence of the Patriarch.

**PASCHA** (*Πάσχα, τό*; Slav. *Paskha* / *Пасха*): The Feast of Feasts, the Glorious Resurrection of Christ, historically known in Western vernacular usage as Easter.

**PATEN** (*Δισκάριον, τό*; Slav. *Diskos* / *Дискосъ*): The Diskos; a sacred shallow plate set upon a permanent base or foot, which holds the bread prepared at the Table of **Prothesis** during the rite of the **Proskomide** (Office of Oblation). It represents the cave of Bethlehem in which Christ was born and the tomb wherein He was laid. In its circular shape, it likewise symbolizes the heavens that encompass God.

**PATERITSA** (*Πατερίτσα, ἡ*; Slav. *Posokh* / *Посохъ*): A pastoral staff, walking stick, or cane of wood, surmounted by a minimally ornamented knob.

**PATRIARCH** (*Πατριάρχης, ὁ*): As employed throughout this **Typikon**, the Ecumenical Patriarch of Constantinople.

**PATRIARCHIKI PIMANTORIKI RAVDOS** (*Πατριαρχικὴ ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος*): The Patriarchal Pastoral Staff (Crozier). This staff is surmounted by a decorative metallic motif depicting two serpents with a Cross in the center. This crest is an emblem of episcopal authority: the serpents represent the visible and invisible adversaries of the Church, and the Cross symbolizes the power that Christ has granted unto the Church and entrusted unto the bishop to vanquish these "serpents" of[^656]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Patriarchiki Pimantoriki Ravdos Concluded Part 2 – Pentikostarion – Phrase – Pimantoriki Ravdos – Placing – Plirotika – Polychronion – Polyeleos Part 1) [Demos, p. 1092 / TD-113]

evil. The staff also recalls the rod of Moses with which he guided the Israelites unto the Promised Land.

**PENTIKOSTARION** (*Πεντηκοστάριον, τό*; Slav. *Tsvetnaya Triod* / *Цвѣтная Тріодь*, *Pentikostaria*):
1. The title of the liturgical book containing the proper offices for all Moveable Feasts beginning on the Sunday of Holy Pascha (Easter) and ending on the Sunday of All Saints.
2. The term is likewise applied to the liturgical season spanning the period from the Sunday of Pascha through the Sunday of All Saints.

**PHRASE** (*φράσις, ἡ*): In Greek liturgical and grammatical terminology, the word *phrasis* designates a complete grammatical sentence as well as a distinct liturgical clause or fragment.

**PIMANTORIKI RAVDOS** (*ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος, ἡ* or *πατερίτσα, ἡ*; Slav. *Posokh* / *Посохъ*, *Zhezl* / *Жезлъ*): The Pastoral Staff (Crozier) of a bishop. See also **PATRIARCHIKI PIMANTORIKI RAVDOS**.

**PLACING** (*κατάθεσις, ἡ*; Slav. *Polozhenie* / *Положеніе*): Specifically, the liturgical feast commemorating the deposition or placing of a sacred relic or vestment into a shrine or place of worship; e.g., the Placing of the Precious Robe of the Theotokos at the Church of Blachernae in Constantinople, an event commemorated on July 2.

**PLIROTIKA** (*Πληρωτικά, τά*; Slav. *Prositelnaya Ekteniya* / *Просительная ектенія*): The Litany of Supplication (Litany of Completion); the series of petitions chanted by the deacon following the Cherubic Hymn and the Great Entrance in the Divine Liturgy, commencing with the words **"Let us complete our prayer unto the Lord"** (*Πληρώσωμεν τὴν δέησιν ἡμῶν τῷ Κυρίῳ*).

**POLYCHRONION** (*Πολυχρόνιον, τό*; Slav. *Mnogoletie* / *Многолѣтіе*): A solemn hymn of liturgical acclamation chanted in honor of a hierarch or eminent civic leader.

**POLYELEOS** (*Πολυέλεος, ὁ*; Slav. *Polyeley* / *Полиелей*): The title of a festal hymn comprising Psalms 134–135 (LXX), chanted as the third Kathisma of the Psalter at Matins on major feasts. These Psalms are collectively called the Polyeleos ("Many Mercies") because of the recurring refrain **"For His mercy endures forever"** (*Ὅτι εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα τὸ ἔλεος αὐτοῦ*) repeated in every verse of Psalm 135 (LXX). The third section consists of selected psalm verses (*eklogaria*) for each major feast. In Slavic practice these are usually[^657]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Polyeleos Concluded Part 2 – Afterfeast (lit. 'post-feast') – Postscript – Potirion – Praise, Praises – Forefeast (lit. 'pre-feast') – Priest – Primikirios – Proeortia Part 1) [Demos, p. 1093 / TD-114]

reduced to one or two verses, accompanied by a **Megalynarion** (*Velichanie*).
The name Polyeleos is likewise given to the great chandelier suspended directly beneath the central dome of an Eastern church. It symbolizes Christ, the All-Merciful Light. When this chandelier was furnished with oil lamps, it was so designated because it employed "many oil lamps" (*poly-elaion* / *πολυέλαιον*). On the Holy Mountain of Athos, during solemn all-night vigils, the Polyeleos is illuminated and swung in a wide arc to spectacular effect at Vespers during the singing of **"Lord, I have cried"** (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*) and during the chanting of the Polyeleos at Matins. It is also illuminated at the Great Entrance on major feasts and at funeral services. Encircling the Polyeleos there often hangs a great brass or metal corona decorated with icons, normally depicting the Twelve Apostles, and surmounted by tapers. This circular chandelier is known as a **Choros** (*χορός*).

**POST-FEAST** (*μεθέορτα, τά*): See **METHEORTA**. The canonical liturgical designation is the **AFTERFEAST** (lit. 'post-feast').

**POSTSCRIPT**: See **YPOMNIMA**.

**POTIRION**: The Holy Chalice. See **AGION POTIRION**.

**PRAISE, PRAISES** (*αἶνος, ὁ*; pl. *αἶνοι, οἱ*; Slav. *Khvalitny* / *Хвалиτны*): See also **ENI**. These festive psalms (Psalms 148–150 LXX) are chanted near the end of **Orthros** (Matins), immediately following the **Exapostilarion**. In Western churches, the Praises are known as Lauds.

**PRE-FEAST** (*προεόρτια, τά*): The canonical liturgical designation is the **FOREFEAST** (lit. 'pre-feast'). See **PROEORTIA**.

**PRIEST** (*῾Ιερεύς, ὁ*; Slav. *Ierey* / *Іерей*, *Sviashchennik* / *Священникъ*): The sacred minister ordained to celebrate the Holy Mysteries and other sacred rites. In the ecclesiastical hierarchy, he ranks below a bishop but above a deacon.

**PRIMIKIRIOS** (*Πριμικήριος, ὁ*): The Director of Ceremonies or Dean of tapers in the Patriarchal Church.

**PROEORTIA** (*Προεόρτια, τά*; Slav. *Predprazdnstvo* / *Предпразднство*): See also **FOREFEAST** (lit. 'pre-feast'). The liturgical preparatory period immediately before a feast—lasting from one to several days—preparing for and foreshadowing the coming feast. This[^658]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Proeortia Concluded Part 2 – Proeortion – Proestos – Profitologion – Proimiakos Psalmos – Proistamenos – Prokimenon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1094 / TD-115]

festal preparation is expressed in the hymns and prayers of this period.

**PROEORTION** (*Προεόρτιον*; Slav. *Predprazdnstvo* / *Предпразднство*): See **FOREFEAST** (lit. 'pre-feast'). An adjective characterizing a proper hymn as belonging to the period of a feast's **Forefeast** (*Proeortia*): e.g., Forefeast Dismissal Troparion (lit. 'Proeortion Apolytikion', p. 149 in Bogdanos' *Byzantine Liturgy: Hymnology and Order*), Forefeast Kontakion (*ibid.*, p. 151), etc.

**PROESTOS** (*Προεστώς, ὁ*; Slav. *Nastoyatel* / *Настоятель*): The rector or presiding priest of a church, or the Abbot and Superior of a monastery.

**PROFITOLOGION** (**PROPHETOLOGION** / *Προφητολόγιον, τό*; Slav. *Paremiynik* / *Паремійникъ*): The Old Testament Lectionary containing the scriptural readings (**Paroimiai** / *Παροιμίαι*, *Paremias*) assigned to Vespers, the Royal Hours, and other divine services throughout the ecclesiastical year. When a separate edition of this lectionary is unavailable, the appointed Old Testament readings are extracted from the **Menaia**, the **Triodion**, or the **Pentecostarion**. In English, classic collections containing these appointed lections include the *Divine Prayers and Services of the Catholic Orthodox Church of Christ* by Father Seraphim Nassar.

**PROIMIAKOS PSALMOS** (*Προοιμιακὸς Ψαλμός, ὁ*; Slav. *Prednachinatelnyy Psalom* / *Предначинательный псаломъ*): The **Introductory Psalm**; Psalm 103 (LXX), chanted at the opening of Vespers. See **INTRODUCTORY PSALM**.

**PROISTAMENOS** (*Προϊστάμενος, ὁ*): In collegiate churches and cathedrals where numerous clergy serve, the *proistamenos*, by seniority of ordination, is the ranking senior priest, known as the Dean of the cathedral.

**PROKIMENON** (**PROKEIMENON** / *Προκείμενον, τό*; Slav. *Prokimen* / *Прокименъ*): The Greek word signifies "that which is set before" or "introduces." The Prokeimenon was originally an entire Psalm that served to introduce the reading of Sacred Scripture that followed it. One verse from the Psalm was selected as the responsorial refrain to the chanting of all the others. In current liturgical use, the Prokeimenon is reduced to the refrain and[^659]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Prokimenon Concluded Part 2 – Prosfora – Proskynisis – Pros Kyrion, Ta – Proskomidi – Prosomion) [Demos, p. 1095 / TD-116]

one to four verses of the Psalm being employed.

Another liturgical definition for the Prokeimenon: A verse from the Psalter sung immediately before Scripture lessons, primarily at the Divine Liturgy, Vespers, and Matins. (Except on major feasts and during the Great Fast, Scripture lessons have been discontinued at daily Vespers). The responsorial verse sung immediately before the Gospel lesson is the **Alleluia**.

**PROSFORA** (**PROSPHORA** / *Προσφορά, ἡ*; Slav. *Prosvira* / *Просвира*, *Proskura* / *Проскура*; lit. 'offering' or 'oblation'): A round loaf of leavened Eucharistic bread of pure wheat used in the celebration of the Divine Liturgy. Upon its upper surface it bears a round relief seal, in the center of which is a square stamp inscribed with the sign of the Cross and the Greek letters **IC XC NIKA** (**ΙΣΧΣ ΝΙΚΑ**) at the four corners, representing *Jesus Christ Conquers* (**IC** being the abbreviation for Jesus, **XC** for Christ, and **NIKA** for Victor).

**PROSKYNISIS** (**PROSKYNESIS** / *προσκύνησις, ἡ*; Slav. *Poklonenie* / *Поклоненіе*): A reverent bowing, prostration, or veneration offered before holy icons, relics, or the Cross, distinguished from *latreia* (the worship and adoration offered unto God alone).

**PROS KYRION, TA** (*Τὰ Πρὸς Κύριον*): Lit. "Unto the Lord." A standard abbreviation commonly used in the **Typikon** for the entire Eighteenth Kathisma of the Septuagint (LXX) Psalter. This Kathisma encompasses a total of fifteen Psalms, from Psalm 119 to Psalm 133 inclusive (the Songs of Ascents). See also **PSALM 119**.

**PROSKOMIDI** (**PROSKOMIDE** / *Προσκομιδή, ἡ*; Slav. *Proskomidiya* / *Проскомидія*): Often rendered *Proskomide* or *Office of Oblation* (lit. 'the offering or bringing of gifts to God'). This is the Service of Preparation performed before the Divine Liturgy. In this service, the priest prepares the bread and the wine for the Holy Eucharist at the Table of **Prothesis** (**Table of Oblation**). This table or alcove is situated on the north side of the Sanctuary. The service standardly takes place while **Orthros** (Matins) is being chanted.

**PROSOMION** (*προσόμοιον, τό*; pl. **PROSOMIA** / *προσόμοια, τά*; Slav. *Podoben* / *Подобенъ*): A proper liturgical hymn whose poetic meter, rhythm, and melody are modeled upon and copied from an **Automelon** (**Aftomelon** / *αὐτόμελον*), an original model hymn.[^660]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Prostration – Protheoria – Prothesis Part 1) [Demos, p. 1096 / TD-117]

**PROSTRATION** (*κατάπτωσις, ἡ*; Slav. *poklon* / *поклонъ*, *zemnoy poklon* / *земный поклонъ*): A term indicating that the worshipping individual falls down to a completely prone position, touching the forehead to the ground. See also **METANIA** and **PROSKYNESIS**.

**PROTHEORIA** (*προθεωρία, ἡ*; lit. "pre-theory", introductory principles): In his *Typikon*, George Violakis devotes the introductory treatise—which he designates the *Protheoria* (*Προθεωρία*)—to defining the general principles that govern the ordering of the divine services of the Church. In the main body of his *Typikon*, he applies these principles systematically to the rubrical order of service for each specific feast day throughout the ecclesiastical year, encompassing both immovable and movable cycles.

**PROTHESIS** (*Πρόθεσις, ἡ*; Slav. *Zhertvennik* / *Жертвенникъ*): The small alcove or the table upon which the office of the **Proskomide** (**Proskomidi** / *Προσκομιδή*) is celebrated. This sacred area is situated within the **Sanctuary** (*Ἱερὸν Βῆμα*) on its northern side.

If one were to gaze through the central Holy Doors (the **Beautiful Gate** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη*) of the icon screen (**Iconostasis** / *Εἰκονοστάσιον*), one would observe small alcoves, marble shelves, or tables on either side of the eastern sanctuary wall. The alcove upon the left (north) is designated the **Prothesis**, while the corresponding area upon the right (south) is known as the **Diakonikon** (*Διακονικόν*). The distinct liturgical function of each within the Sanctuary is realized by the sacred vessels and implements that rest upon them and the sacred icons mounted upon the wall immediately behind them.

Situated in the Sanctuary, the Prothesis is the appointed place where the Eucharistic Gifts of bread and wine are prepared during the morning office of **Orthros** (Matins), prior to the celebration of the Divine Liturgy. In addition, the Prothesis serves as the repository where the sacred Eucharistic vessels and implements—namely, the Holy Chalice (*Ποτήριον*), the Holy Paten (*Δισκάριον*), the Asterisk (*᾿Αστερίσκος*), the liturgical spear (*Λόγχη*), the communion cloths, the communion spoon (*Λαβίς*), and the veils—are preserved. Upon the wall behind the Table of Prothesis is traditionally depicted the icon of the Nativity of Christ or the Extreme Humility (*Ἄκρα Ταπείνωσις*).

In the early history of the Church, this alcove or table was not situated within the sanctuary itself. Instead, the Eucharistic Gifts were prepared and the liturgical treasures of the Church were preserved in an external structure or adjacent chamber, the **Skevophylakion** (*Σκευοφυλάκιον*, the sacristy), and from this place the Book of the Gospels and the Holy Offerings were brought into the church at the appointed moments of the sacred synaxis by the sacred ministers (our modern liturgical processions with the Holy Gospel during[^661]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Prothesis Concluded Part 2 – Protomartyr – Protonotarios – Protopsaltis – Protothrone – Psalm Part 1) [Demos, p. 1097 / TD-118]

the Small Entrance and with the Holy Gifts during the Great Entrance are direct liturgical witnesses to this ancient practice). The Skevophylakion, or "treasury for guarding the sacred vessels," subsequently ceased to function as a separate chamber and was architecturally integrated into the Sanctuary as the Prothesis and Diakonikon.

**PROTOMARTYR** (*πρωτομάρτυς, ὁ*; Slav. *Pervomuchenik* / *Первомученикъ*; lit. "the first martyr"): The title assigned to the first witness who shed his blood for Christ. See also under **DEACON** for Saint Stephen the Archdeacon and Protomartyr.

**PROTONOTARIOS** (*πρωτονοτάριος, ὁ*): The chief notary and chancellor among the imperial or patriarchal scribes and secretaries.

**PROTOPSALTIS** (*πρωτοψάλτης, ὁ*; Slav. *Pervopenets* / *Первопѣвецъ*): The First or Head Chanter who directs the right (southern) choir upon the **Soleas** (*Σολέα*). Conversely, the chanter who directs the left (northern) choir upon the soleas is designated the **Lambadarios** (*Λαμπαδάριος*).

**PROTOTHRONE** or **PROTOTHRONOS** (*πρωτόθρονος, ὁ*; lit. "First-Throne"): In Orthodox ecclesiology, the title of primacy accorded to the historic episcopal cathedra occupied by the Archbishop and Patriarch of Constantinople and New Rome at the Ecumenical Patriarchate, formerly in the imperial capital of the Byzantine Empire and currently located at the Patriarchal Church of Saint George in the Phanar (Fener) district of Istanbul.

**PSALM** (*ψαλμός, ὁ*; Slav. *Psalom* / *Псаломъ*; lit. "a psalm, song of praise"): According to ancient monastic discipline, monks recited all 150 Psalms of the Psalter on a continual, recurring cycle. In early Christian antiquity, desert hermits recited the entire Psalter daily. With the historical emergence and spread of cenobitic (communal) monasticism, the practice was instituted of chanting the Canonical Hours in common, and the Psalter thus became the foundational bedrock of the Daily Office, augmented by liturgical hymnography, prayers, and scriptural lessons. The universal monastic custom of chanting through all 150 Psalms every week during the course of the divine offices was established in due course.

To facilitate this systematic psalmody, the 150 Psalms were divided into twenty major sections designated **Kathismata** (*καθίσματα, τά*; sing. **Kathisma** / *κάθισμα*; Slav. *Kafismy* / *Каѳисмы*), signifying literally "sittings." The name is de-[^662]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Psalm Continued Part 2 – Chapter Divisions: Septuagint vs. Masoretic Text Part 1) [Demos, p. 1098 / TD-119]

rived from the fact that, in the monastic offices as they developed in the cenobitic centers of Jerusalem (the Lavra of Saint Sabbas) and Constantinople (the Monastery of Studios), the Psalms were chanted or read by a designated brother while the remaining brethren remained seated and listened attentively.

Each Kathisma is further subdivided into three sections designated **Stases** (*στάσεις, αἱ*; sing. **Stasis** / *στάσις*; Slav. *Statyi* / *Статіи*), signifying literally "standings," because at the conclusion of each Stasis the reader chants: "Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen" (*«Δόξα Πατρὶ καὶ Υἱῷ καὶ Ἁγίῳ Πνεύματι· καὶ νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*), at which moment all present stand erect in reverent worship of the Holy Trinity.

The Church maintains as its authentic, canonical witness of the Old Testament the ancient Septuagint (Greek / LXX) version, rather than the later Masoretic (Hebrew / MT) recension. For this reason, the canonical numbering and division of the Psalms in liturgical practice follows the Greek Septuagint numbering rather than the Masoretic numbering found in the King James Version and Western vernacular Bibles.

### Chapter Divisions: Septuagint vs. Masoretic Text

The Septuagint Greek version (LXX) is the authentic Old Testament text received and utilized by the Church. The LXX Psalter diverges in several respects from the Masoretic Text (MT), which forms the basis for the King James Version and most modern Western translations.

In addition to substantive textual and theological variants, the LXX and MT recensions of the Psalter diverge most visibly in their numerical chapter divisions. This discrepancy frequently causes confusion for readers unfamiliar with biblical textual history. The chapter divisions of the LXX and MT recensions correspond as set forth in the table below:

| LXX Septuagint (Greek) | MT Masoretic (Hebrew) |
| :--- | :--- |
| 1–8 | 1–8 |
| 9 | 9–10 |

[^663]

The twenty Kathismata are distributed between Vespers and Orthros (Matins), ensuring that the entire Psalter of 150 Psalms is read in its entirety each week. In standard non-festal order, one Kathisma is appointed at Vespers and two or three at Orthros, depending upon the day of the week, the liturgical season, and the specific prescriptions of the Typikon.

On Sunday evenings and on evenings following an All-Night Vigil, no Kathisma is appointed at Vespers. During Great Lent, Kathismata are additionally appointed at the Canonical Hours (First, Third, Sixth, and Ninth Hours), so that the entire Psalter is chanted twice through within each week.

In addition to the 150 Psalms, the liturgical Psalter contains the Nine Biblical Canticles (*ᾠδαί*), which are chanted at Orthros during Great Lent.

The Seventeenth Kathisma, which comprises solely Psalm 118 ("The Psalm of the Law"), constitutes a central element of the midnight and morning psalmody at Orthros.

**PSALM 50** (*Ψαλμὸς Ν΄, ὁ*; Slav. *Psalom 50* / *Псаломъ 50*): This psalm is frequently abbreviated as *«ὁ Ν΄»* in the rubrics of the *Typikon*. It is the preeminent penitential psalm of King David, opening with the incipit: **"Have mercy on me, O God, according to Thy great mercy, and according to the multitude of Thy compassions blot out my transgression"** (*«Ἐλέησόν με, ὁ Θεός, κατὰ τὸ μέγα ἔλεός σου, καὶ κατὰ τὸ πλῆθος τῶν οἰκτιρμῶν σου ἐξάλειψον τὸ ἀνόμημά μου...»*). Its complete text is contained in the liturgical Psalter (*Ψαλτήριον*).

**PSALM 103**: See **INTRODUCTORY PSALM** (*Προοιμιακὸς Ψαλμός*).

**PSALM 118** (*Ψαλμὸς ΡΙΗ΄*): This psalm is known liturgically as the **Amomos** (*Ἄμωμος*; Slav. *Neporochny* / *Непорочны*, "The Blameless"), an appellation derived from the opening word of the psalm in[^664]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Psalm 118 Concluded Part 2 – Psalm 119 – Psalter Part 1) [Demos, p. 1100 / TD-121]

Greek in its initial verse: *"Blessed are the undefiled [blameless] in the way"* (*Μακάριοι οἱ ἄμωμοι ἐν ὁδῷ*). This psalm is the longest in the entire Psalter (containing 176 verses). Although subdivided into three distinct Stases or sections, it constitutes the opening and sole psalm comprising the **Seventeenth Kathisma** (*Δέκατον Ἕβδομον Κάθισμα, τό*) of the Septuagint Greek Psalter.

**PSALM 119** (*Ψαλμὸς ΡΙΘ΄*): This psalm, which begins the **Eighteenth Kathisma** (*Δέκατον Ὄγδοον Κάθισμα, τό*) of the Septuagint Psalter, opens with the Greek incipit *Pros Kyrion* (*Πρὸς Κύριον*, "Unto the Lord"). The title **Ta Pros Kyrion** (*Τὰ Πρὸς Κύριον*) is the standard rubric abbreviation in the Violakis *Typikon* designating the entire Eighteenth Kathisma, which encompasses Psalms 119 through 133 inclusive (the fifteen Songs of Ascents / Gradual Psalms).

**PSALTER** (*Ψαλτήριον, τό*; Slav. *Psaltyr* / *Псалтирь*): A canonical book of the Old Testament containing all 150 Psalms of David according to the Septuagint. In liturgical usage, these Psalms are systematically partitioned into twenty major sections designated **Kathismata** (*Καθίσματα, τά*), each containing several Psalms as set forth in the table below (wherein Greek numeral designations are accompanied by parenthetical Arabic numerals). Each Kathisma is further subdivided into three divisions termed **Stases** (*στάσεις*):

The deuterocanonical corpus of the Septuagint contains an additional psalm ascribed to David (*"I was small among my brethren"*), designated Psalm 151. This psalm is not numbered among the standard 150 Psalms and is not included within the liturgical cycle of the twenty Kathismata.

**PSALTIRION** (*Ψαλτήριον, τό*): See **PSALTER**.

**PULPIT** or **AMBO** (*Ἄμβων, ὁ*; Slav. *Amvon* / *Амвонъ*; lit. "an elevated place, podium"): See also **AMVON**. The pulpit is an elevated platform or elaborate architectural podium situated forward of the icon screen (**Iconostasis**) upon the north (left) side of the **Soleas** (*Σολέα*). Embellished with iconographic representations of the Four Evangelists, it is the designated station from which the deacon or priest proclaims the Holy Gospel or delivers the homily.

**RAVDOS** (*ῥάβδος, ἡ*; Slav. *Zhezl* / *Жезлъ*, *Posokh* / *Посохъ*): Staff or pastoral crozier. See also **PATRIARCHAL PASTORAL STAFF** and **PIMANTORIKI RAVDOS**.

**READER** (*᾿Αναγνώστης, ὁ*; Slav. *Anagnost* / *Анагностъ*, *Prichetnik* / *Причетникъ*, *Chtets* / *Чтецъ*): The minor cleric ordained to read, chant, and give liturgical responses during divine services. Such an individual is formally consecrated to these sacred duties by the bishop through the imposition of hands and appointed prayers in the rite of clerical tonsure.

**RECOVERY** (*εὕρεσις, ἡ*; Slav. *Obretenie* / *Обрѣтеніе*; of the holy relics of a saint): See **FINDING**.

**REFECTORY** (*Τράπεζα, ἡ*; Slav. *Trapeza* / *Трапеза*): The communal dining hall of a monastery. In Byzantine and Eastern monasticism, the Refectory is revered as a sacred liturgical extension of the church; in many monastic establishments, it is architecturally fashioned with an altar and icon screen. An icon of Christ or the Mother of God, before which a vigil lamp (*lambada*) burns perpetually, is always present. Specific liturgical offices are appointed to be celebrated in the Trapeza, such as the Rite of Mutual Forgiveness at the opening of Great Compline in monastic communities.[^666]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Relics – Renewal Week – Repose – Righteous Saint – Ripidion – Rite – Royal Door – Royal Gate – Royal Office Part 1) [Demos, p. 1102 / TD-123]

**RELICS** (*λείψανα, τά*; Slav. *Moshchi* / *Мощи*): The physical bodily remains of a canonized Saint, or sacred personal possessions such as garments or vestments hallowed by a saint's life. Holy relics are venerated with religious devotion throughout the Church. When a new church is consecrated, the consecrating bishop permanently encloses and seals holy relics within the Holy Altar Table (**Holy Table** / *Ἁγία Τράπεζα*), perpetuating the primitive Christian tradition wherein the Holy Eucharist was celebrated upon the tombs of the holy Martyrs.

**RENEWAL WEEK** (*Διακαινήσιμος Ἑβδομάς, ἡ*; Slav. *Svetlaya Sedmitsa* / *Свѣтлая седмица*): The week of Paschal renewal. See **BRIGHT WEEK**.

**REPOSE** (*κοίμησις, ἡ*; Slav. *Uspenie* / *Успеніе*): The falling asleep of a Christian in the Lord. See **DORMITION**.

**RIGHTEOUS SAINT**: In Eastern Christian hagiology, **Righteous** (*Δίκαιος, ὁ*; fem. *Δικαία, ἡ*; Slav. *Pravednyy* / *Праведный*) designates a lay or married saint of the Old or New Covenant who lived in holy obedience to God in the world (such as Righteous Joseph, or the Holy and Righteous Ancestors of God Joachim and Anna).
