This title is distinct from that of a monastic ascetic, who is designated **Osios** (*Ὅσιος, ὁ*; Slav. *Prepodobnyy* / *Преподобный*, Venerable Monastic). See **OSIOS, OSIA**.

**RIPIDION** (*ῥιπίδιον, τό*; pl. **RIPIDIA** / *ῥιπίδια, τά*; Slav. *Ripida* / *Рипида*; lit. "a fan"): Derived from the Greek verb *ripizo* (*ῥιπίζω*, "to fan"). In liturgical practice, this motion of fanning is witnessed in the Eucharistic Anaphora when the priest fans or gently waves the **Aer** (*ἀήρ*) over the Holy Gifts prior to the consecratory epiclesis, reciting the prayer: *"So that they may be to those who partake of them for vigilance of soul, forgiveness of sins, communion of Thy Holy Spirit, fulfillment of the kingdom of heaven, confidence before Thee, and not in judgment or condemnation..."* (*«Ὥστε γενέσθαι τοῖς μεταλαμβάνουσιν εἰς νῆψιν ψυχῆς, εἰς ἄφεσιν ἁμαρτιῶν, εἰς κοινωνίαν τοῦ Ἁγίου σου Πνεύματος, εἰς βασιλείας οὐρανῶν πλήρωμα, εἰς παῤῥησίαν τὴν πρὸς σέ, μὴ εἰς κρῖμα ἢ εἰς κατάκριμα...»*). See also **AER** and **EXAPTERYGA**.

**RITE** (*τελετή, ἡ*; *ἀκολουθία, ἡ*): See **CEREMONY**.

**ROYAL DOOR** (*Βασιλικὴ Θύρα, ἡ*; Slav. *Tsarskie Vrata* / *Царскія врата*): While occasionally confused with the central Holy Doors of the icon screen (**Beautiful Gate** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη*), this term properly denotes the central portal leading from the narthex into the western end of the nave, through which Byzantine emperors solemnly entered the cathedral of Hagia Sophia.

**ROYAL GATE**: See **ROYAL DOOR**.

**ROYAL OFFICE**: The brief preparatory service that precedes the chanting of the Six Psalms at Orthros (Matins). This office originated in monastic communities established by royal[^667]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Royal Office Concluded Part 2 – Rubrics – Sacrament – Sacristan – Sacristy – Saint – Sakkos – Sanctuary Part 1) [Demos, p. 1103 / TD-124]

foundations as an intercessory service on behalf of the Imperial Family and sovereign rulers (comprising Psalms 19 and 20 LXX, Troparia of the Cross, and commemorative litanies).

**RUBRICS** (*τυπικόν, τό*; *διάταξις, ἡ*; *ἐρυθρογραφία, ἡ*): Liturgical directions or instructions inscribed in red ink in liturgical books to guide celebrants and singers. In a broader sense, the heading of an ecclesiastical office or liturgical section rubricated in color to distinguish ceremonial directives from chanted text.

**SACRAMENT**: See **MYSTERIES** (*Μυστήρια*).

**SACRISTAN** (*ἱεροφύλαξ, ὁ*; Slav. *Riznichiy* / *Ризничій*): The church officer in charge of the sacristy (**Skevophylakion**) where sacred vessels, vestments, and liturgical treasures are preserved. In Eastern liturgical tradition, this duty is overseen by the **Ecclesiarch** (*᾿Εκκλησιάρχης*; Slav. *Ustavshchik* / *Ekklesiarkh*).

**SACRISTY**: See **SKEVOPHYLAKION** (*Σκευοφυλάκιον*).

**SAINT** (*Ἅγιος, ὁ*; fem. *Ἁγία, ἡ*; Slav. *Svyatoy* / *Святый*): All holy men and women who, through virtuous living upon earth or through martyrdom and public confession of faith, have been canonized and enrolled in the Church's calendar. The saints and the faithful departed who dwell in heavenly glory with God constitute the Church Triumphant.

**SAKKOS** (*σάκκος, ὁ*; pl. *σάκκοι, οἱ*; Slav. *Sakkos* / *Саккосъ*; from Hebrew *saq*, sackcloth): The episcopal vestment (corresponding in form to the Western dalmatic) originally signifying a garment of humble sackcloth or tunic of mockery worn by Christ during His Passion. In modern liturgical usage, the Sakkos is a magnificent tunic tailored from rich brocade, modeled upon the imperial tunic bestowed by Byzantine emperors upon the Ecumenical Patriarch. Formerly reserved as an exclusive Patriarchal vestment, it is now worn by all bishops as the primary hierarchical vestment replacing the priestly **Phelonion** (**Felonion** / *φαιλόνιον*).

**SANCTUARY** (*Ἱερὸν Βῆμα, τό*; *Ἱερόν, τό*; Slav. *Altar* / *Олтарь*, *Svyatilishche* / *Святилище*): Also designated the Holy Bema (**Agion Vima** / *῞Αγιον Βῆμα*), the Holy Altar, or the Holy of Holies. By ancient ecclesiastical tradition, the Sanctuary is built facing the eastern end of the church building, as Christians pray facing towards[^668]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Sanctuary Concluded Part 2 – Saturday of the Akathist – Savanon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1104 / TD-125]

the rising sun, which is revered as a cosmic icon of the spiritual Sun of Righteousness, Jesus Christ the Son of God, Who will dawn from the East and illumine the cosmos. Where architectural constraints preclude exact eastward alignment, the altar represents the symbolic liturgical East. In the center of the Sanctuary stands the **Holy Table** (**Altar Table** / *῾Αγία Τράπεζα*), upon which the Eucharistic Sacrifice of the Divine Liturgy is celebrated. The table of the **Prothesis** is situated upon the northern side of the Sanctuary, while the **Diakonikon** is situated upon the southern side.

The Sanctuary of the Church is the Holy of Holies, the earthly reflection of the heavenly court wherein the sacred ministers, by the grace of God, unite with the angelic hosts in offering thanksgiving, adoration, and ceaseless praise unto our Heavenly Father. While possessing an exalted mystical dignity, it serves as the functional presbytery designed to facilitate the ceremonial actions of the clergy who officiate at the divine offices.

No person may enter the sacred precincts of the Sanctuary unworthily or without the explicit blessing of the presiding priest. Clergy alone are appointed to minister within the altar, entering only after devout spiritual preparation to serve among the angels in the presence of God. Within the ecclesial body, clergy, laity, catechumens, and penitents occupy their designated stations, participating in the divine praises according to their ordained charisms within the royal priesthood of all believers. In these sacred spaces we fulfill our stewardship unto Christ, and "glorify God in every place of His dominion" (Ps. 102:22 LXX).

**SATURDAY OF THE AKATHIST** (*Σάββατον τοῦ Ἀκαθίστου*; Slav. *Subbota Akafista* / *Суббота Акаѳиста*): The Fifth Saturday of Great Lent, dedicated to the solemn chanting of the Akathist Hymn to the Most Holy Theotokos. See **AKATHIST HYMN**.

**SAVANON** (*σάβανον, τό*; Slav. *Savan* / *Саванъ*, *Srachitsa* / *Срачица*; lit. "winding-sheet, shroud"): A white linen sheet or alb worn by the Bishop during the consecration of a church, during which the Holy Table is washed, wiped, and anointed with Holy Myron. It is this[^669]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Savanon Concluded Part 2 – Scale) [Demos, p. 1105 / TD-126]

linen garment that is reverently cut into small pieces at the conclusion of the consecration rite and distributed as a blessing to all participants, both clergy and faithful.

**SCALE** (*κλῖμαξ, ἡ*; *μουσικὴ κλῖμαξ, ἡ*): In music theory and acoustics, a scale is a structured sequence of musical tones arranged in ascending or descending pitch order. Scales are determined by the fundamental frequencies of their constituent pitches, providing a calibrated measure of musical intervals. The interval between two successive pitches is designated a scale step.

In acoustics, musical pitches are measured by vibrational frequency, expressed in cycles per second (cps) or Hertz (Hz). In liturgical chant, musical tone is distinguished from noise by regular, periodic acoustic vibrations producing a distinct pitch, accompanied by dynamic amplitude (volume) and timbral timbre (vocal or instrumental color).

Diverse modal scales have developed across sacred musical cultures. In the sixth century B.C., Pythagoras formulated the mathematical ratios of the fundamental intervals: the octave (2:1), fourth (4:3), and fifth (3:2). In Christian antiquity, the ancient Greek tonal system was adapted into the eight ecclesiastical modes—the Byzantine **Oktoechos** (*᾿Οκτώηχος*, Tones 1 through 8) and Western church modes.

In Western ecclesiastical practice, the modal framework was gradually transformed by polyphony into the diatonic major and minor scales. The diatonic major scale comprises five whole tones (t) and two semitones (s) in the pattern: t–t–s–t–t–t–s (exemplified by the white keys of the keyboard from C to C′), which served as the foundation of classical Western musical architecture.[^670]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Scale Continued Part 2) [Demos, p. 1106 / TD-127]

cal tonality; tonality, in music, is the quality by which all tones of a composition are heard in relation to a central tone designated the keynote or tonic. The diatonic minor scale is based upon the interval sequence: tone–semitone–tone–tone–semitone–tone–tone (t–s–t–t–s–t–t). This arrangement produces the lower third, sixth, and seventh degrees characteristic of the minor mode; the higher seventh degree, situated a semitone rather than a whole tone below the primary note or "tonic," is frequently borrowed from the major mode for use at cadential resolutions. Until the close of the nineteenth century, the use and influence of the diatonic scale in Western music remained predominant.

Related to musical scales is the fundamental concept of key. In musical theory, the term "key" is employed to designate the scale from which the tonal material of a given composition is derived. To state, for example, that a composition is set in the key of C major signifies that it utilizes as its primary tonal material the intervals proper to that scale.

The conception of key developed wherein a home tone, or tonic, serves as the principal focus of a musical composition, while the remaining tones assume structural importance according to their harmonic relationship to the tonic. The increasing complexity of musical instruments necessitated more refined tuning systems for assigning specific pitches to the twelve Western pitch classes that constitute the octave. In standard usage, this procedure designates the method employed in the tuning of keyboard instruments.

By the era of J. S. Bach, equal temperament—the calibrated adjustment of specific intervals from their mathematically pure acoustical ratios in order to establish a tuning system wherein music may modulate freely from key to key without unacceptable dissonance—had become firmly established. The resulting chromatic scale comprises twelve semitone intervals within the octave (represented by the white and black keys of the keyboard). Although the diatonic scale is fundamentally heptatonic (seven-toned), compositions set in standard major or minor tonality employ the remaining five tones of the chromatic scale as auxiliary or ornamental pitches. Music that employs them freely is characterized as highly chromatic, whereas music that employs them sparingly is designated diatonic.

The twelve scales, established upon each note as a home tone, together with the twelve[^671]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Scale Concluded Part 3 – Byzantine Modal Scales Part 1) [Demos, p. 1107 / TD-128]

concomitant minor scales, remained the primary organizing framework of Western music until the tonal system was challenged by dodecaphonic (twelve-tone) composers, notably Arnold Schoenberg (1874–1951), an Austrian composer born in Vienna. He revolutionized modern music by abandoning traditional functional tonality and formulating a twelve-tone "serial" technique of composition. Schoenberg composed actively into the mid-twentieth century. In his compositions, he established the principle of atonality, defined as the systematic avoidance of harmonic or melodic reference to tonal centers. The term designates a compositional method in which the composer deliberately rejects functional tonality.

The whole-tone scale, which divides the octave into six equal whole tones (represented by C, D, E, F-sharp, G-sharp, and A-sharp on the keyboard), imparts an ambient quality that rendered it adaptable to Impressionism; its harmonic possibilities were explored extensively in the works of Claude Debussy. The pentatonic scale (exemplified by the five black keys of the keyboard) has traditionally been associated with Eastern musical cultures owing to its prevalence in Chinese, Japanese, and Javanese traditions. The most intricate scales known belong to Arabian music, cultivated in the classical musical traditions of the Islamic peoples of Arabia, the Fertile Crescent, and North Africa.

*Byzantine Modal Scales* (*βυζαντιναὶ μουσικαὶ κλίμακες*) (See also **MODE**) (T. Bogdanos):

The musical scales of Byzantine sacred hymnography (Byzantine Chant) are fundamentally modal in character. They comprise eight ecclesiastical modes: Tone 1, Tone 2, Tone 3, Tone 4, Tone 5 (Tone 1 Plagal), Tone 6 (Tone 2 Plagal), Tone 7 (Grave Mode / Tone 3 Plagal / *Varys*), and Tone 8 (Tone 4 Plagal).

Although derived historically from the Ancient Greek modal system, both Byzantine and Western church modes in the Middle Ages diverged from their classical predecessors in nomenclature as well as in intervallic structure. Having exerted a formative influence upon Western plainchant (Gregorian Chant), Byzantine modal scales were structurally analogous to those of the medieval West and, like them, essentially diatonic. Under the influence of prolonged exposure to Near Eastern musical traditions during the four centuries of Ottoman rule following the fall of the Byzantine[^672]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Byzantine Modal Scales Continued Part 2) [Demos, p. 1108 / TD-129]

Empire (1453–1821), the scales of Byzantine ecclesiastical chant became increasingly chromatic. Thus, whereas the fourteenth-century scale of Tone 6 (Tone 2 Plagal) was purely diatonic—E, F, G, A, B, C, D, E′—by the close of the eighteenth century this mode had become fully chromatic—E, F, G-sharp, A, B, C, D (D-sharp), E′—with an altered melodic range (*ekstasis* / *ἔκτασις*) and distinctive dominant reciting tones (*despozontes phthongoi* / *δεσπόζοντες φθόγγοι*). See **MODE**. By reason of these structural developments, the era extending from the sixteenth century to the present is designated the *Post-Byzantine* period.

With the introduction of chromaticism, the scales of Post-Byzantine chant became increasingly nuanced, utilizing intervals narrower than the semitone and broader than the whole tone of the Western tempered system. This was accomplished through the addition or subtraction of microtones (*moria*) to or from standard intervals. These microtonally calibrated scales cannot be executed upon fixed-pitch tempered instruments such as the piano.

While modern Western music relies primarily upon two scale types—major and minor—Post-Byzantine sacred music employs four comprehensive tonal classifications designated genera (*γένη*, sing. *γένος*): the Diatonic Genus, the Enharmonic Genus, the Hard Chromatic Genus, and the Soft Chromatic Genus.

(1) The **Diatonic Genus** (*Διατονικὸν γένος*), in its central octave, spans from C to C′, with the pitches E and B pitched microtonally flatter than the corresponding E and B of the Western tempered scale. To this genus belong the scales of Tone 1, Tone 5 (Tone 1 Plagal), Tone 8 (Tone 4 Plagal, with an occasional minor seventh: C, D, E, F, G, A, B-flat, C′), and Tone 4 (the papadic melodies of Tone 4 employ a minor seventh and occasionally a minor third: G, A, B-flat, C, D, E, F-natural, G′).

An ecclesiastical mode consists not merely of an abstract scale, but of a defined intervallic scale united to traditionally established melodic formulae and cadential figures (*theseis*) characteristic of that tone (see **MODE**).

(2) The **Enharmonic Genus** (*᾿Εναρμόνιον γένος*) is tuned identically to the Western[^673]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Byzantine Modal Scales Continued Part 3) [Demos, p. 1109 / TD-130]

major scale. It is the sole genus that can be rendered accurately upon a tempered keyboard instrument or fretted stringed instruments (such as the mandolin or guitar). To the Enharmonic Genus belong the scales of Tone 3 and Tone 7 (Grave Mode / *Varys*), with the exception of the papadic (melismatic) diatonic melodies of Tone 7.

(3) The **Hard Chromatic Genus** (*Σκληρὸν Χρωματικὸν γένος*) is exemplified by a scale composed of two disjunct (separated), identical tetrachords: d–e–f–g and a–b–c–d. Their second degrees (E and B) are flatter than the E-flat and B-flat of Western music, while their third degrees (F and C) are sharper than Western F-sharp and C-sharp. To the Hard Chromatic Genus belong the scales of papadic and sticheraric Tone 6 (Tone 2 Plagal) and the heirmologic melodies of Tone 2.

(4) The **Soft Chromatic Genus** (*Μαλακὸν Χρωματικὸν γένος*) is characterized by a scale of two disjunct, identical tetrachords: the first comprising C–D–E–F and the second G–A–B–C. Their second degrees (D and A) are intervalically broader than D-flat and A-flat, yet narrower than D-natural and A-natural by approximately one-third of a tone (a microtone). In Byzantine musical notation, they are designated by crossed-flat accidentals (D-crossed flat and A-crossed flat).

To the Soft Chromatic Genus belong the scales of papadic and sticheraric Tone 2, heirmologic Tone 6 (Tone 2 Plagal), and occasionally the heirmologic melodies of Tone 4.

Unless proficient in reading Post-Byzantine neumatic notation, contemporary church musicians typically learn ecclesiastical chant transcribed into Western staff notation with the assistance of a keyboard instrument. Consequently, scales conceived originally as microtonal are executed in tempered approximations. In liturgical practice, the intervalic variance between the authentic microtonal scale and its tempered rendering does not substantially alter the sacred *ethos* or spiritual character of the chant.

The Soft Chromatic Genus, representing the most intricate aspect of Post-[^674]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Byzantine Modal Scales Concluded Part 4 – Schima – Scholium – Sessional Hymn – Sexton) [Demos, p. 1110 / TD-131]

Byzantine chant to transcribe into Western tempered notation, requires deliberate adjustment. The D-crossed flat and A-crossed flat, although lowered, are intervalically nearer to D-natural and A-natural in the tempered scale, while remaining more distant from D-flat and A-flat. Accordingly, it is more faithful to sing these pitches as D-natural and A-natural in tempered execution. The practical performance is determined by melodic contour: if the altered pitch is followed by a descending figure, it is sung as D-flat or A-flat; if followed by an ascending figure, it is sung as D-natural or A-natural.

The foregoing summarizes the genera and scales governing the eight modes of Post-Byzantine chant utilized in contemporary liturgical practice.

For comprehensive musicological treatises, see the studies on Byzantine Chant by Chrysanthos of Madytos, F. Desby, H. Powers, and P. Jeffery cited in the Bibliography.

**SCHIMA** (*σχῆμα, τό*; Slav. *skhima* / *схима*; lit. "form" or "fashion"): A reverent bow. In monastic tradition, the Holy Schema denotes the monastic habit and represents the distinct degrees of ascetic life, specifically the Little Schema (*mikroschemos*) and the Great and Angelic Schema (*megaloschemos* / *схима*).

**SCHOLIUM**, pl. **SCHOLIA** (*σχόλιον, τό*; pl. *σχόλια, τά*): In ancient and Byzantine manuscript traditions, marginal annotations or exegetical commentaries inscribed by scribes alongside the sacred text. Frequently incorporated into subsequent manuscript copies, these glosses became integral components of textual transmission prior to the introduction of printing.

**SESSIONAL HYMN** (*κάθισμα, τό*; Slav. (*Sedalen*) / *сѣдаленъ*): See **KATHISMA HYMN**.

**SEXTON** (*νεωκόρος, ὁ*; Slav. *Prichetnik* / *причетникъ*, *Ponomar* / *пономарь*): An officer or caretaker charged with the physical maintenance of the church edifice, sacred vessels, holy relics, and liturgical vestments. The sexton assists the officiating clergy and performs auxiliary duties, including the sounding of bells, the lighting and extinguishing of candles, and the preparation of the censer.[^675]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Skevophylakion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1111 / TD-132]

**SKEVOPHYLAKION** (*Σκευοφυλάκιον, τό*; Slav. *Sosudokhranilishche* / *Сосудохранилище*; lit. "vessel-treasury"): The sacred treasury and sacristy. In contemporary ecclesiastical architecture, this area functions as the **Diakonikon** (*Διακονικόν*) or vestry, situated on the southern side of the Sanctuary, where liturgical vestments, sacred vessels, holy relics, and crosses are preserved and where the clergy vest prior to divine services. In early Christian antiquity, the Skevophylakion was erected as a distinct, freestanding structure near the eastern facade of the church basilica. From this repository, the Book of the Gospels and the Eucharistic Offerings were solemnly carried into the church in procession, a liturgical movement commemorated in the Small and Great Entrances of the Divine Liturgy.

The modern Patriarchal Treasury of the Ecumenical Patriarchate contains a select collection of embroidered vestments, dating primarily from the seventeenth to nineteenth centuries, worn by Patriarchs, synodal hierarchs, priests, and deacons. No vestments have survived in situ from the Byzantine era, owing to catastrophic fires, urban conflagrations, and the presentation of diplomatic gifts to foreign Orthodox sovereigns and prelates—particularly in Russia—both prior to and following the Fall of Constantinople (1453).

Extant Byzantine vestments are preserved in international museums and monastic treasuries. The historic **Sakkos** attributed to Emperor Nikephoros II Phokas (963–969) is preserved at the Great Lavra on Mount Athos, while that of Emperor John I Tzimiskes (969–976) is conserved at Iviron Monastery. The Synodical Sacristy of the Patriarchate of Moscow preserves two gold-embroidered *sakkoi* bearing imperial portraits presented to Saint Photios, Metropolitan of Moscow (1408–1431), by Emperor John VIII Palaiologos (1425–1448). The *sakkos* of Ecumenical Patriarch Parthenios II (1644–1646, 1648–1650)—together with five additional *sakkoi*, omophoria (*ὠμοφόρια*), epigonatia (*ἐπιγονάτια*), and a gold pectoral cross containing a relic of the True Cross—was sold in 1654 by Greek merchants to the Tsar of Russia, who presented them to Patriarch Nikon of Moscow. The *sakkos* and pectoral medallion (**Engolpion** / *ἐγκόλπιον*) of Patriarch Cyril I Loukaris, purchased by Tsar Alexis I in 1655, was likewise bestowed upon Patriarch Joasaph of Moscow. Numerous hierarchs bequeathed their sacred vestments and pectorals to the Ecumenical Patriarchate, monastic houses, or kin. Surviving eighteenth-century inventories record a remarkable abundance of[^676]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Skevophylakion Concluded Part 2 – Patriarchal Sakkoi Part 1) [Demos, p. 1112 / TD-133]

sacred artifacts: hierarchical vestments, pectoral crosses, engolpia, pastoral staffs, episcopal, priestly, and diaconal stoles, liturgical girdles, and vessels of gold and silver. The first of these catalogues, confirmed by the Ecumenical Patriarch in 1714, enumerates the offerings presented over centuries to the Patriarchate. A second inventory records twenty-five precious objects, alongside a jeweled mitre adorned with diamonds and pearls and a diamond-encrusted pectoral medallion formerly belonging to Metropolitan Leontios of Heliopolis, presented in his memory by his brother Hadji-Joasaph to the Monastery of the Great Lavra on Mount Athos in 1803.

The liturgical embroidery ateliers of Constantinople, and subsequently of Trebizond and Smyrna, attained renown for their exquisite gold and silver metallic needlework executed upon precious brocades and silks from Bursa (Brusa). These workshops produced gold-embroidered hierarchical and ministerial vestments of extraordinary artistic merit. Among the celebrated embroideresses whose signatures survive upon extant works are Despoineta, Mariora, Euphrosyne Agatha, Sophia, Theodosia, and Daphnitsa. Following the eighteenth century, comparable embroidery ateliers were established in Russia and Vienna.

*Patriarchal Sakkoi*: The **Sakkos** (*σάκκος, ὁ*), a tunic featuring wide half-sleeves, originated as an imperial garment bestowed exceptionally by Byzantine emperors upon the Ecumenical Patriarch and selected autocephalous hierarchs. Following the Fall of Constantinople, the *sakkos* was adopted universally by all bishops, replacing the ancient *polystavrion phelonion* (*πολυσταύριον φαιλόνιον*, a Phelonion adorned with multiple crosses).

Within the Patriarchal Treasury are preserved renowned historical *sakkoi*, designated respectively as the *Sakkos of Tiberias*, the *Sakkos of the Tree of Jesse*, the *Sakkos of the Enamels*, the two *Sakkoi of the Eagle*, and the *Sakkos of the Evangelist Matthew*, which are utilized by the Ecumenical Patriarch on solemn feasts.

The *Sakkos of Tiberias*: This vestment is embroidered with two narrative scenes depicting the Appearance of Christ after His Resurrection unto the Disciples fishing upon the Sea of Tiberias, as recounted in the Holy Gospel according to Saint John (John 21:1–14) and commemorated in the Tenth Resurrectional Exaposteilarion and Eothinon Sticheron (**"Sea of Tiberias..."** / *Τιβεριάδος θάλασσα...*). The scene embroidered upon the back of the *sakkos*, executed in gold and silver thread upon white silk, portrays Christ standing upon[^677]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Patriarchal Sakkoi Continued Part 2) [Demos, p. 1113 / TD-134]

the shore of the lake, directing the weary Disciples—who had labored through the night and caught nothing—to cast their nets once more. This nocturnal scene is framed by two tall palm trees beneath a canopy of stars, a crescent moon, and clouds. An embroidered inscription records: "It was the property of the late Dionysios of Ephesus. 4 March 1810."

The miraculous draught of fishes is depicted upon the front of the *sakkos*. The Apostles are shown hauling the laden nets unto the shore under the command of Christ. Before the standing figure of the Lord appear a loaf of bread and glowing embers upon which fish is prepared. The background features a radiant sun rising between leafy trees. An inscription reads: "Repaired in the patriarchate of Dionysios of Adrianople. 1888."

The *Sakkos of Tiberias*, renowned for the uniqueness of its iconographic program, represents a masterpiece of Constantinopolitan liturgical needlework. Metropolitan Gennadios of Heliopolis records that this vestment "was embroidered by Daphnitsa of the Psaroudes family of Therapia, renowned for her embroidery of hierarchical vestments" (*Orthodoxia*, 24 [1949], p. 81). The vestment was the property of Metropolitan Dionysios Kalliades of Ephesus (d. 1821).

The *Sakkos of the Tree of Jesse*: Embroidered upon rich crimson silk, this vestment portrays the *Tree of Jesse* upon the back and the *Glorious Second Coming of Christ* upon the front. The genealogical tree and the heavenly background are worked in gold thread, while the figures are highlighted in colorful silks, bordered by gold-embroidered lilies. The genealogical scene follows traditional Byzantine iconography, depicting the righteous Jesse as the ancestral root of the House of David and of Christ.

Ten medallions suspended from the branches contain half-length figures of Old Testament Prophets holding prophetic scrolls, with Christ enthroned at the summit in the act of blessing. The obverse depicts the Second Coming: against a radiant gold background, Christ, flanked in holy deesis by the Most Holy Theotokos and Saint[^678]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Patriarchal Sakkoi Concluded Part 3) [Demos, p. 1114 / TD-135]

John the Baptist, is portrayed raising His right hand in benediction. Beneath the clouds appears the assembly of the righteous, which led earlier scholars to designate this vestment the *Sakkos of All Saints*. In addition to the primary scenes, the vestment is adorned with six medallions depicting the Passion of Christ: the Raising of Lazarus, the Entry into Jerusalem, the Mystical Supper, Ecce Homo, the Crucifixion, and the Lamentation at the Tomb. These secondary scenes reflect Western stylistic influence. The vestment was presented by Patriarch Kallinikos of Alexandria (1858–1861).

*Archaeological and Textile Analysis*: This *sakkos* is tailored according to traditional Byzantine patterns, featuring broad sleeves composed of straight fabric panels joined to the shoulder and fastened along the lateral seams with silver-gilt buttons (cf. Johnstone 1967, figs. 1, 7, 11). The fabric consists of double damask woven in gold and silver metallic thread, displaying medallions framing busts of *Christ the Great High Priest* (*᾿Ιησοῦς Χριστὸς ὁ Μέγας ᾿Αρχιερεύς*) alternating horizontally with cherubim and seraphim in circlets, separated by crosses fleury.

Christ is depicted vested in an imperial *sakkos* adorned with cross medallions and an **Omophorion** (*᾿Ωμοφόριον*) marked with large crosses over a cinctured undergarment. He bears a cruciform nimbus bearing the retrograde Greek inscription: *«᾿Ιησοῦς Χριστὸς ὁ Μέγας ᾿Αρχιερεύς»* ("Jesus Christ, the Great High Priest"). The christological monograms flanking the cross (*«᾿Ιησοῦς Χριστὸς Νικᾷ»* / "Jesus Christ Conquers") are likewise woven in reverse.

This fabric belongs to a select corpus of Christian figured textiles produced to order by master weavers in Bursa and Constantinople (cf. *Pyles Mysteriou*, 1994, no. 129). While these imperial looms executed commissions for the Ottoman court, their textiles were marketed commercially and exported to Ukraine, Russia, Poland, and Venice (Inalcik, *EI*²; Patrinelis 1988–1989; Sahillioglu 1985).

Parallels featuring identical medallions of Christ the Great High Priest with properly oriented inscriptions are preserved in the Benaki Museum in Athens (inv. no. 3871) and at the Monasteries of Solca and Putna in Romania (Barnea, Iliescu, and Nicolescu 1971, p. 128; Tafrali 1925, pl. LIX). The Putna exemplar bears the embroidered[^679]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Patriarchal Sakkoi Postscript – Skufos – Small Entrance) [Demos, p. 1115 / TD-136]

date 1614, establishing a firm *terminus ante quem* when the fabric was remounted as a priestly Phelonion (*φαιλόνιον*). On stylistic grounds regarding the development of Ottoman textile design, such fabrics date from the mid-sixteenth century onward (Mackie 1980, p. 352). The absence of characteristic Ottoman floral motifs suggests an origin in the first half of the sixteenth century. A commercial archival register of 1548 records that Ottoman merchants in Leontopolis (Lviv) offered for sale textiles decorated with Christian crosses (Rogers, Tezjan, and Delibas 1986, p. 29), demonstrating the established circulation of such fabrics in Ukrainian and Central European trade. This early dating is corroborated by the medallion layout, an iconographic scheme regarded as archaic by the fifteenth century (Geijer 1979, pp. 143–148).

Athonite tradition attributes the Dionysiou vestment to Saint Niphon II, an Athonite hesychast who served as Ecumenical Patriarch on three occasions (1486–1489, 1497–1498, and 1502). Following a pastoral mission in Wallachia, he retired to Dionysiou Monastery, where he reposed in 1508 (*Actes de Dionysiou*, 1968, p. 18; Nasturel 1986, pp. 142–144). Textile analysis substantiates this dating, establishing the Dionysiou *sakkos* as the earliest dated figured textile from the Bursa looms bearing Christian epigraphy.

**SKUFOS** or **SKOUFOS** (*σκοῦφος, ὁ*; Slav. *skufiya* / *скуфія*): A soft pleated cap of black cloth, worn by monastics and clergy as part of daily ecclesiastical attire.

**SMALL ENTRANCE** (*Μικρὰ Εἴσοδος, ἡ*; Slav. *Malyi Vkhod* / *Малый входъ*): A solemn liturgical procession during the Divine Liturgy wherein the celebrants, preceded by candle-bearers and acolytes, take the Book of the Holy Gospels from the Holy Table, exit the Sanctuary through the northern diaconal door, and advance to the center of the Soleas facing east toward the Altar. The deacon (or priest) elevates the Holy Gospel and proclaims aloud: **"Wisdom. Let us stand!"** (*«Σοφία. Ὀρθοί»*). The clergy chant the Entrance Hymn (**Isodikon** / *Εἰσοδικόν* / Slav. *Vkhodnoye*) before re-entering the Sanctuary through the Holy Doors (**Beautiful Gate** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη*). When a bishop presides at the Divine Liturgy, the Small Entrance is celebrated with distinct pontifical rubrics.[^680]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Soft Chromatic Scale – Soleas – Spear – Spoon – Staff – Staff-Bearing Usher) [Demos, p. 1116 / TD-137]

**SOFT CHROMATIC SCALE** (*μαλακὴ χρωματικὴ κλίμαξ, ἡ*): The microtonal scale characterized by crossed-flat accidentals; identical with the Mild Chromatic Scale (see **SCALE** and **MODE**).

**SOLEAS** (*σωλέας, ὁ*; Slav. *soleya* / *солея*): In contemporary liturgical architecture, this term designates the entire raised area immediately fronting the **Iconostasis** (*εἰκονοστάσιον, τό*). The soleas is elevated one or more steps above the floor level of the **Nave** (*ναός, ὁ*). In early Byzantine churches, the outer perimeter of the soleas was demarcated by a low chancel screen or balustrade known as the **Templon** (*τέμπλον, τό*).

The **Sanctuary** (*βῆμα, τό* / *ἱερατεῖον, τό*) is elevated one to three steps above the floor of the Nave. The Iconostasis is not positioned flush at the edge of the Sanctuary platform, but is recessed several feet behind the uppermost step. This arrangement creates a continuous walkway in front of the screen reserved for the liturgical movements of the clergy, designated the soleas. In the exact center of the soleas, immediately before the **Beautiful Gate** (**Holy Doors** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη* / *Ἅγιαι Θύραι*) and upon the same level as the Sanctuary floor, extends a semicircular projection designated the **Ambon** (*ἄμβων, ὁ*), upon which the priest or deacon stands to proclaim the Holy Gospel, recite litanies, and deliver homilies during the divine services. In larger cathedral churches, the soleas is expanded to accommodate episcopal ceremonies, solemn entrances, and liturgical processions.

**SPEAR** (*λόγχη, ἡ*; Slav. *kopio* / *копіе*): The liturgical lance utilized by the priest at the Proskomide (Table of Preparation) to cut and pierce the Lamb from the Prosphora, recalling the piercing of Christ's side upon the Cross (John 19:34). See **LONCHI**.

**SPOON** (*λαβίς, ἡ*; Slav. *lzhitsa* / *лжица*; lit. "tongs," recalling Isaiah 6:6): The long-handled liturgical spoon with which the priest or bishop administers the Holy Mysteries of Christ's Body and Blood to the faithful communicants toward the conclusion of the Divine Liturgy.

**STAFF** (*ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος, ἡ*; Slav. *posokh* / *посохъ* or *zhezl* / *жезлъ*): The Pastoral Staff (Western crozier) of an Eastern hierarch. In the Byzantine tradition, the summit of the staff is surmounted by two serpents facing one another and flanking a small cross or globe, recalling Christ's injunction to the Apostles: "Be wise as serpents and innocent as doves" (Matthew 10:16). As a serpent annually sloughs off its skin to renew its strength, so also the bishop must guide the flock along the ascetical path that leads to spiritual renewal. The pastoral staff signifies that the hierarch is the chief shepherd of Christ's flock.

**STAFF-BEARING USHER** (*κλητὴρ ῥαβδοῦχος, ὁ*; Slav. *posokhonosets* / *посохоносецъ*): An acolyte, subdeacon, or junior cleric charged with carrying the Pastoral Staff of the officiating hierarch and presenting it to him during episcopal ceremonies.[^681]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Stanza – Star – Stasidia – Stasis – Stavrosimon – Stavrotheotokion – Stichari – Sticharion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1117 / TD-138]

**STANZA** (*στροφή, ἡ*; Greek *strofe*; lit. "a turn, bend, or twist"): In Byzantine hymnography, as in general poetic theory, a stanza (*strophe*) constitutes a discrete metric unit within a larger poetic composition, sharing the metrical and rhythmic structure of the melodic model (**Heirmos** / *εἱρμός* / **Automelon** / *αὐτόμελον*). Exemplified by the successive strophes (troparia or oikoi) of a **Kontakion** (*κοντάκιον*) or the odes of a **Canon** (*κανών*). See **STROPHE**.

**STAR** (*ἀστερίσκος, ὁ*; Slav. *zvezditsa* / *звѣздица*): The liturgical metal star composed of two crossed arcs surmounted by a small cross, placed over the Diskos during the Proskomide to prevent the veil from disturbing the arranged holy particles, symbolizing the Star of Bethlehem (Matthew 2:9). See **ASTERISK**.

**STASIDIA** (*στασίδια, τά*, sing. *στασίδιον, τό*; Slav. *stasidii* / *стасидії*): Monastic choir stalls affixed to the perimeter walls of church naves and chancels. Characterized by high panelled backs, folding hinged seats, and elevated armrests designed to support chanters and monastics during prolonged standing offices. The undersurface of the folding seat is fitted with a small supportive ledge (misericord) that affords physical support while maintaining an upright posture. See **KATHISMATA**.

**STASIS** (*στάσις, ἡ*; Slav. *stattya* / *статія*; lit. "standing"): In liturgical usage, a stationary structural section of a service; specifically, the three subdivisions comprising each **Kathisma** of the Psalter, each stasis concluding with the doxological triad: "Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen. Alleluia, alleluia, alleluia, glory to Thee, O God."

**STAVROSIMON**, pl. **STAVROSIMA** (*σταυρώσιμον, τό*, pl. *σταυρώσιμα, τά*; Slav. *krestovosledovatelen* / *крестовоскресный* or *kresten* / *хресний*): A hymn or cycle of hymns dedicated to the veneration of the Precious and Life-Giving Cross of the Lord.

**STAVROTHEOTOKION** (*σταυροθεοτοκίον, τό*; Slav. *krestobohorodichnyj* / *крестобогородиченъ*): A hymn addressed to the Most Holy Theotokos contemplating her standing beside the Cross of her Son and God during His Holy Passion. These hymns are chanted in the weekly cycles of Wednesday and Friday within the **Octoechos** (**Parakletike** / *Παρακλητική*).

**STICHARI** (*στιχάρι, τό*): Vernacular or colloquial form; see **STICHOS** and **VERSE**.

**STICHARION** (*στιχάριον, τό*; Slav. *stikhar* / *стихарь*; Latin *alba*): The universal liturgical vestment, traditionally white, worn by all ranks of the sacred clergy. In slightly differentiated forms, it is worn by altar servers, readers, subdeacons, and deacons as an outer vestment, and by priests and bishops as the foundational undergarment (**podriznik** / *ὑπορράσον*). For the sacred orders, it constitutes a lightweight, long-sleeved, ankle-length tunic, deriving historically from the white garment bestowed at Holy Baptism. The Latin designation *alb* is an abbreviation of *tunica alba* (white tunic). In the first century, the[^682]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Sticharion Continued Part 2 – History of the Greco-Roman Tunic and Himation) [Demos, p. 1118 / TD-139]

tunic constituted the foundational article of attire donned at the beginning of the day. Working-class laborers wore knee-length tunics, whereas venerable elders and citizens of sedentary rank wore ankle-length tunics. The classical Greco-Roman tunic was originally sleeveless, as sleeves were regarded as barbarian apparel typical of northern climates. Tunics did not incorporate sleeves until the third century, when imperial portraits depict Roman emperors returning from eastern campaigns clad in sleeved tunics (*tunica manicata*).

In cold seasons, multiple tunics were worn simultaneously for warmth, gathered at the waist by a cord or cincture.

In the apostolic era, citizens donned an outer mantle known as the **himation** (*ἱμάτιον, τό*) over the inner tunic. The himation was a rectangular woven cloak draped loosely across the shoulders and wrapped about the body. Its textile weave, coloration, and decorative fringes varied according to the wearer's sex, vocation, and social standing. Because of its loose draping, the mantle impeded vigorous physical movement and was routinely removed during physical exertion. For instance, when blind Bartimaeus sprang up to approach Jesus in Mark 10:46–52, he cast aside his himation. The Gospel of Matthew (9:20–22) records that the woman afflicted with an issue of blood received healing when she touched the hem of His himation. In the Book of Revelation (3:5; 3:18; 4:4), the righteous are clothed in white himatia, evoking the luminous heavenly attire bestowed upon those who had received the white tunic at Holy Baptism. The himation never entered ecclesiastical vesture, as its loose folds rendered it impractical for the active manual duties of the liturgical ministers. The Holy Gospels record that Jesus was led to His Crucifixion clad in a himation over an inner tunic. The Roman soldiers partitioned His himation into four parts, but because His tunic was woven in one continuous piece from top to bottom, they cast lots for it (John 19:23–24). The seamless tunic of Christ was sleeveless and reached to the ankles. While patristic authors reference seamless weaving techniques, the classical methods for producing seamless garments were largely lost following the fall of the Western Roman Empire. During the first four centuries of the Church, catechumens were baptized unclothed; for modesty, baptisms were conducted in separate groups of men, women, and children, female deaconesses assisting at the baptism of women.[^683]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Sticharion Continued Part 3 – Baptismal Symbolism and Hierarchical/Diaconal Usage) [Demos, p. 1119 / TD-140]

Upon emerging from the baptismal waters, the newly illuminated were immediately clothed in a pure white tunic (in Latin, *tunica alba*, whence *alb*). For this reason, the sticharion represents the robe of incorruption and recalls both the cleansing of Holy Baptism and the glorious resurrection of the body on the Last Day.

The sticharion worn by the Reader and Chanter constitutes his primary liturgical vestment. At his tonsure, the bishop vests him briefly in the Reader's small phelonion (*μικρὸν φαιλόνιον* / *kravatsion* / *кукуліонъ*), which drapes over the shoulders and symbolizes his dedication to the divine service of God. The sticharion fashioned for readers, subdeacons, and deacons features wide, loose sleeves, whereas the priestly and episcopal under-sticharion features tapered sleeves fastened at the wrists by cuffs (**epimanikia** / *ἐπιμανίκια*). Ecclesiastically designated the robe of salvation and the garment of joy, it symbolizes a clean and peaceful conscience, a blameless life, and the spiritual joy in the Lord which flows in him who wears it.

In architectural and liturgical manuals, the diaconal sticharion is described as a tunic-like vestment resembling the Western dalmatic when worn by servers, readers, subdeacons, and deacons. When worn by bishops and priests beneath the chasuble or phelonion, it corresponds to the alb, though it is not restricted to white fabric.

The liturgical vestment *Sticharion* is not to be confused with the hymnographic term **Stichiron** (*στιχηρόν*), which designates a hymn chanted with a psalm verse.

When worn as an undergarment by priests and hierarchs, the sticharion is traditionally fashioned from white silk, linen, or gold brocade.

When worn as an outer vestment by deacons and subdeacons, the sticharion is richly ornamented in the liturgical color of the feast or day. It is slit down the sides beneath the sleeves, the lateral seams being fastened with loops and metallic buttons or ornamental bells.

Certain historical traditions retain the designation **dalmatikon** (*δαλματικόν*) for the diaconal sticharion, recalling its adoption in the fourth century from the imperial Roman dalmatic.

When donned by deacons, subdeacons, and altar servers, the outer sticharion presents a flowing robe with wide sleeves, fastened at the collar and along the sides. In design, it corresponds to the dalmatic and tunicle of the Western Church. An embroidered cross is customarily appliquéd to the back between the shoulder blades.[^684]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Sticharion Concluded Part 4 – Vesting Prayer – Stichiron / Stichira Part 1) [Demos, p. 1120 / TD-141]

During the Order of **Prothesis** (Preparation of the Holy Gifts), the priest takes his sticharion, blesses the cross upon the back, and recites the vesting prayer: **"Blessed is our God, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen. My soul shall exult in the Lord; for He hath clothed me with the garment of salvation and covered me with the robe of gladness; as a bridegroom He hath placed a crown upon me, and as a bride He hath adorned me with ornaments"** (Isaiah 61:10; *«᾿Αγαλλιάσεται ἡ ψυχή μου ἐπὶ τῷ Κυρίῳ· ἐνέδυσε γάρ με ἱμάτιον σωτηρίου, καὶ χιτῶνα εὐφροσύνης περιέβαλέ με· ὡς νυμφίῳ περιέθηκέ μοι μίτραν, καὶ ὡς νύμφην κατεκόσμησέ με κόσμῳ»* [Isaiah 61:10]; *«Πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. ᾿Αμήν»*).

In the form worn by deacons, subdeacons, and altar servers, the sticharion remains a long outer robe with wide, loose sleeves, secured at the collar and held along the lateral flanks by buttons or cords, structurally analogous to the dalmatic and tunicle of the Western Church.

The Latin liturgical counterpart to the priestly sticharion is the alb, an ankle-length white linen garment donned by the priest or bishop beneath the chasuble, the Western equivalent of the Byzantine **Phelonion** (*φαιλόνιον*).

**STICHIRON**, pl. **STICHIRA** (*στιχηρόν, τό*, pl. *στιχηρά, τά*; Slav. *stikhira*, pl. *stikhiry* / *стихира*, *стихиры*): A liturgical hymn chanted in alternate succession with a psalm verse (**Stichos** / *στίχος*). Stichira are sung at **Vespers** (at **"Lord, I have cried"** [*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*]) and at **Matins** (**Orthros**) at the **Praises** (*Ainoi* / *Αἶνοι*). At the **Aposticha** (*ἀπόστιχα*) of Vespers, the first stichiron is chanted without a preceding psalm verse. The Sunday evening Aposticha form an alphabetical acrostic across the weeks, though the initial stichiron of each Tone does not form part of the acrostic series.

Festal stichira accompany the fixed psalmody of Vespers and the Praises of Matins throughout all liturgical seasons of the year, including the Sundays and weekdays of the Great Fast and the recurring eight-week cycle of the **Tones** (Tone 1 to Tone 8) commencing at Holy Pascha.[^685]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Stichirarion – Stichologia – Stichos – Stole – Strophe – Synapte – Synaxarion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1121 / TD-142]

The elaborate melodies of these stichira, codified in the **Stichirarion** (*Στιχηράριον, τό*), exhibit a more ornate and melismatic character than the syllabic troparia preserved in the **Heirmologion** (*Εἱρμολόγιον, τό*).

**STICHOLOGIA** (*στιχολογία, ἡ*; Slav. *stikhosloviye* / *стихословіе*): The continuous, sequential chanting or recitation of the Psalter or biblical canticles verse by verse (*stichos*).

**STICHOS** (*στίχος, ὁ*; Slav. *stikh* / *стихъ*; lit. "a row, line, or rank"): A liturgical verse from the Psalter or Holy Scripture utilized as an incipit or psalm refrain preceding a stichiron or troparion.

**STOLE**: In Western terminology, the clerical neck vestment; for Eastern counterparts, see **ORARION** (deacon), **EPITRACHILION** (priest), and **OMOPHORION** (bishop), or general **VESTMENTS**.

**STROPHE** (*στροφή, ἡ*; Greek *strofe*; lit. "a turning"): In classical Greco-Roman lyric poetry, a metric stanza. Historically derived from the movements of the dramatic chorus, which chanted a strophe while circling the orchestra in one direction, followed by an antistrophe (*ἀντιστроφή*) of identical metric measure as the chorus reversed its circular movement. In ecclesiastical hymnography, strophe is synonymous with the metric stanza or troparion.

**SYNAPTE** (*συναπτή, ἡ*; Slav. *synaptiya* / *ектенія*): Derived from the Greek *synaptein* (*συνάπτειν*, "to join together"); a continuous series of liturgical petitions joined together into a single litany, each petition soliciting a specific spiritual or material blessing from God, followed by the people's response: **"Lord, have mercy"** (*Κύριε, ἐλέησον*). See **LITANY** and **EKTENIA**.

**SYNAXARION** (*Συναξάριον, τό*; Slav. *Sinaksar* / *Прологъ*): A liturgical book containing historical notices and brief biographies of the saints commemorated on each calendar day throughout the twelve monthly volumes of the **Menaion** (*Μηναῖον*). The synaxarion entry is read at Matins (**Orthros**) immediately following the Kontakion and Oikos after the Sixth Ode of the Canon.

The liturgical application of the term *Synaxarion* shifted through different historical epochs. Originally, it designated the systematic index or calendar of biblical pericopes appointed for reading in church, corresponding to the Latin *Capitulare* and *Comes*. Subsequently, the synaxarion came to incorporate the complete pericopes of the Holy Gospel and the **Apostolos** (*᾿Απόστολος*).[^686]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Synaxarion Concluded Part 2 – Synaxis – Synthronon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1122 / TD-143]

In the Divine Liturgy, these readings were eventually excerpted into distinct liturgical volumes: the Book of the Holy Gospels (**Evangelion** / *Εὐαγγέλιον*) and the Book of the Epistles (**Apostolos** / *᾿Απόστολος*). The designation *Synaxarion* was retained for the lectionary index of the Divine Office, which in turn was augmented with the complete hagiographical narratives of the saints. The synaxarion evolved into a comprehensive martyrology and compendium of the sacred events commemorated by the Church. Numerous medieval synaxaria survive in manuscript witness, composed and edited by Byzantine hagiographers, preeminent among whom was Symeon Metaphrastes (tenth to twelfth centuries). In modern liturgical usage, the synaxarion no longer forms a standalone volume, its texts having been distributed across the twelve monthly volumes of the **Menaion** (*Μηναῖον*).

**SYNAXIS** (*σύναξις, ἡ*; Slav. *Sobor* / *Соборъ*; lit. "gathering" or "assembly"): A liturgical assembly of the faithful convoked on the day following a Great Feast of the Lord or of the Theotokos to honor a sacred personage centrally instrumental in the salvific event celebrated on the previous day. For example, the Synaxis of Saint John the Baptist and Forerunner (January 7) follows the Theophany of Our Lord (January 6); the Synaxis of the Most Holy Theotokos (December 26) follows the Holy Nativity of Our Lord (December 25); and the Synaxis of the Archangel Gabriel (March 26) follows the Annunciation (March 25). The term corresponds in concept to the Latin *collecta* (from *colligere*, "to gather together") and relates etymologically to *synagoge* (place of reunion).

**SYNTHRONON** (*σύνθρονον, τό*; Slav. *Gorneye mesto* / *Горнє мѣсто* / *сопрестоліе*): The semicircular tiered bench and cathedra situated in the central apse of the Sanctuary behind the Holy Table (**Altar** / *῾Αγία Τράπεζα*). In the Patriarchal Church of Saint George at the Phanar, it comprises an elevated marble cathedra flanked by eleven wooden thrones arranged in an arc along the eastern wall for the members of the Holy Synod of Bishops. In patriarchal celebrations, only the Ecumenical Patriarch ascends the central cathedra, while the concelebrating synodal hierarchs occupy the flanking stalls. Since portions of the marble masonry date to the early fifth century, patriarchal tradition associates its historical provenance with Saint John Chrysostom. The synthronon represents the ecclesial unity of the local church assembled around the bishop as chief celebrant of the Holy Eucharist, and manifests the apostolic collegiality of the episcopal synod. The episcopal cathedra signifies the pastoral and teaching au-[^687]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Synthronon Concluded – Taxis – Tabernacle – Teleti – Templon – Thalassidion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1123 / TD-144]

thority of the bishop.

**TAXIS** (*τάξις, ἡ*; Slav. *chin* / *чинъ* or *ustav* / *уставъ*): The canonical order, rubrical arrangement, or structured sequence of a divine service.

**TABERNACLE** (*᾿Αρтоφόριον, τό*; Slav. *Darokhranilnytsia* / *Дарохранильниця*): The sacred receptacle situated upon the Holy Table wherein the Reserved Holy Mysteries are reverently preserved for the communion of the sick. See **ARTOFORION**.

**TELETI** (*τελετή, ἡ*; Slav. *chinoposlidovanye* / *чинопослѣдованіе*): A sacred rite, liturgical ceremony, or celebration of an ecclesiastical mystery. See **CEREMONY**.

**TEMPLON** (*τέμπλον, τό*, pl. *τέμπλα, τά*; Slav. *templon* / *темплонъ*; lit. "temple" or "shrine"): The low marble or carved wood chancel barrier that separated the Nave (**Naos** / *ναός, ὁ*) from the Sanctuary (**Bema** / *βῆμα, τό*) in early Christian and Byzantine basilicas, evolving into the multi-tiered **Iconostasis** (*εἰκονοστάσιον, τό*). First appearing in the fifth century, the classical templon was waist-high, consisting of parapet slabs (*thorakia*), colonnettes, and a continuous horizontal architrave (*epistyle* / *ἐπιστύλιον*). Three portals pierced the barrier: a central opening designated the **Beautiful Gate** (**Holy Doors** / *῾Ωραῖα Πύλη* / *Ἅγιαι Θύραι*) reserved for the celebrants, and two flanking portals on the north and south. Originally the templon left the sanctuary visually open to the faithful; over successive centuries, icons were suspended from the architrave, curtains were hung between the colonnettes, and the structure developed into the solid iconostasis. Liturgically, the central Holy Doors symbolize the virginal womb of the Most Holy Theotokos and the gate of heavenly paradise. In many traditions, a liturgical curtain is drawn during specific intervals of the Divine Liturgy. The lateral doors are customarily designated the **Angel Doors** because the Archangels Michael and Gabriel are depicted upon them, or the Diaconal Doors because the deacons enter and exit the Sanctuary through them.

**THALASSIDION** (*θαλασσίδιον, τό* or *χωνευτήριον, τό*; Latin *piscina* / *sacrarium*; Slav. *sukhdol* / *суходолъ*): A special drain basin located in the sacristy, cited in the current[^688]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Thalassidion Concluded Part 2 – Lavabo Prayer – Thavmatourgos – Theos Kyrios Part 1) [Demos, p. 1124 / TD-145]

**Ieratikon** (**Sluzhebnik** / *῾Ιερατικόν* / *Служебникъ*) as a mandatory installation within the **Diakonikon** (Sacristy / *διακονικόν, τό*). The thalassidion is a consecrated sink draining directly into clean earth beneath the church foundations, into which holy water, water utilized for washing sacred vessels or liturgical linens, and the water of Holy Baptism are reverently poured. The thalassidion is likewise the basin at which the celebrants wash their hands prior to vesting and celebrating the Divine Liturgy. Under strict canonical regulation, the consecrated Eucharistic Gifts are never poured into the thalassidion, but must be consumed entirely by the priest or deacon at the Prothesis table following the Divine Liturgy. While washing his hands, the celebrant recites the Lavabo prayer from the Septuagint Psalter: **"I will wash my hands in innocency, and will compass Thine altar, O Lord; that I may hear the voice of Thy praise, and tell of all Thy marvelous works. O Lord, I have loved the beauty of Thy house, and the place where Thy glory dwelleth. Destroy not my soul with the ungodly, nor my life with men of blood, in whose hands are iniquities; their right hand is filled with gifts. But as for me, I have walked in mine innocence; redeem me, O Lord, and have mercy upon me. My foot standeth in uprightness; in the congregations will I bless Thee, O Lord"** (Psalm 25:6–12 [MT 26:6–12]; *«Νίψομαι ἐν ἀθῴοις τὰς χεῖράς μου, καὶ κυκλώσω τὸ θυσιαστήριόν Σου, Κύριε, τοῦ ἀκοῦσαί με φωνῆς αἰνέσεώς Σου, καὶ διηγήσασθαι πάντα τὰ θαυμάσιά Σου. Κύριε, ἠγάπησα εὐπρέπειαν οἴκου Σου, καὶ τόπον σκηνώματος δόξης Σου. Μὴ συναπολέσῃς μετὰ ἀσεβῶν τὴν ψυχήν μου, καὶ μετὰ ἀνδρῶν αἱμάτων τὴν ζωήν μου, ὧν ἐν χερσὶν αἱ ἀνομίαι· ἡ δεξιὰ αὐτῶν ἐπλήσθη δώρων. Ἐγὼ δὲ ἐν ἀκακίᾳ μου ἐπορεύθην· λύτρωσαί με, Κύριε, καὶ ἐλέησόν με. Ὁ ποῦς μου ἔστη ἐν εὐθύτητι· ἐν ἐκκλησίαις εὐλογήσω σε, Κύριε»* [Ψαλμὸς 25:6–12 / MT 26:6–12]).

In early basilicas, the thalassidion was situated directly beneath the Holy Table; in contemporary practice, it is located within the diakonikon. Ancient monastic custom appointed that the habit of a newly tonsured monastic be placed upon the thalassidion prior to the rite; following its relocation to the diakonikon, the monastic habit is placed directly upon the Holy Table. When a monk or nun receives the tonsure, the shorn hair is reverently deposited into the thalassidion.

**THAVMATURGOS** (*θαυματουργός, ὁ*; Slav. *chudotvorets* / *чудотворець*): Wonderworker or miracle-worker; an ecclesiastical epithet accorded to holy saints through whom God has worked miracles (e.g., Saint Nicholas the Wonderworker).

**THEOS KYRIOS** (*Θεὸς Κύριος*; Slav. *Bog Gospod’* / *Богъ Господь*): Meaning[^689]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Theos Kyrios Concluded – Theotokarion – Theotokion – Theotokos – Theia Akolouthia – Throne Part 1) [Demos, p. 1125 / TD-146]

**"God is the Lord"** (*Θεὸς Κύριος* / Slav. *Bog Gospod’* / Psalm 117:27, 26 [MT 118:27, 26]); the solemn proclamation and verses chanted at Matins (**Orthros**) immediately following the Great Litany and preceding the Dismissal Troparia.

**THEOTOKARION**, pl. **THEOTOKARIA** (*Θεοτοκάριον, τό*, pl. *Θεοτοκάρια, τά*; Slav. *Bohorodychnyk* / *Богородичникъ*): A liturgical hymnographic collection containing canons and suites of troparia composed in honor of the Most Holy Theotokos, organized according to the eight Tones and appointed for devotion throughout the ecclesiastical year (such as the *Theotokarion* compiled by Saint Nicodemus the Hagiorite).

**THEOTOKION**, pl. **THEOTOKIA** (*Θεοτοκίον, τό*, pl. *Θεοτοκία, τά*; Slav. *bohorodychnyj* / *богородиченъ*): A hymn (troparion) composed in honor of the Most Holy Theotokos, the Mother of God (lit. "Birth-giver of God"). The Theotokion customarily concludes a larger liturgical cycle of hymnography, such as the troparia at the conclusion of Vespers, the stichira at **"Lord, I have cried"** (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*), or the Aposticha. A series of stichira or troparia concludes with a Theotokion following the Little Doxology: **"Glory to the Father, and to the Son, and to the Holy Spirit, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen"** (*Δόξα Πατρί, καὶ Υἱῷ, καὶ Ἁγίῳ Πνεύματι· καὶ νῦν, καὶ ἀεί, καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν*). The Theotokion is chanted in the same Tone as the preceding Doxastikon. At Saturday Great Vespers, however, the Theotokion of the Entrance is the solemn **Dogmatikon** (*Δογματικόν* / Slav. *Dogmatik* / *Догматикъ*) of the Tone of the week, regardless of the Tone of the Doxastikon. The same Theotokion is repeated at Vespers on the following Friday. In Slavic usage, this entrance Theotokion is designated the Dogmatikon, whereas in Byzantine practice the term *Dogmaticon* is likewise applied to the corresponding Theotokion at Small Vespers.

**THEOTOKOS** (*Θεοτόκος, ἡ*; Slav. *Bohorodytsya* / *Богородиця*; lit. "Birth-giver of God"): The supreme dogmatic title of the Virgin Mary, solemnly affirmed by the Universal Church at the Third Ecumenical Council of Ephesus in 431 A.D. The Council defined that Mary's Son, Jesus Christ, is simultaneously true God and true Man, and that His divine and human natures are united hypostatically in the one Divine Person of the Son, the Second Person of the Holy Trinity.

**THEIA AKOLOUTHIA** (*θεία ἀκολουθία, ἡ*; Slav. *bozhestvennoye posliduvanie* / *божественное послѣдованіе*): A sacred liturgical office or divine service.

**THRONE** (*θρόνος, ὁ*; Slav. *prestol* / *престолъ* or *kafedra* / *каѳедра*): In Eastern Orthodox and Byzantine liturgical tradition, the episcopal cathedra or seat of the presiding hierarch, and by extension the Holy Table (**Altar** / *῾Αγία Τράπεζα*) whereon the Holy Sacrifice of the Eucharist is offered.[^690]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Throne Concluded – Parathrone – Timiotera – Tone) [Demos, p. 1126 / TD-147]

Church developed, the bishop's throne was erected in its present location upon the soleas against the southern wall of the church. The hierarchical integrity of the **Synthronon** (*σύνθρονον*) behind the Holy Table in the Sanctuary has not been compromised by the erection of another throne outside the Sanctuary upon the soleas; it remains the bishop's seat during divine services. Perhaps the most authentic Patriarchal throne in the Church today is that preserved in the Patriarchal Church of Saint George at the Phanar in Constantinople (modern Istanbul). Upon this throne are inscribed three historical epigraphs: 1. Beneath the eaves of the throne gables, an inscription records that the throne was the gift of Ecumenical Patriarch Jeremiah II (Tranos) in 1577 to the former Patriarchal Church of the Panagia Pammakaristos. 2. An inscription at the base of the throne records that it was the craftsmanship of the Athenian master artist Lavrentios. 3. Upon one of the gables above the eaves, an inscription records that the throne was damaged between 1652 and 1654 during the patriarchate of Paisios I (1652–1653, 1654–1655). The throne was subsequently restored under Patriarch Iakovos (1679–1682). This damage caused the loss of precious gems and the original icons of Christ the Pantocrator (*Παντοκράτωρ*, Ruler of All) and the Descent into Hades with the Entombment of Christ, described by Malaxios in 1577; their original placement upon the throne is no longer known. The current icon is a replacement commissioned by Patriarch Paisios I.

In addition to the Synthronon and the throne erected upon the soleas, a third type of throne is situated within the Patriarchal Church of Saint George: the **Parathrone** (*παραθρόνιον, τό*). It is positioned between the larger, ornate Patriarchal Throne and the Kliros. Because the Patriarchate is likewise constituted as a stauropegial monastic community, the parathrone is occupied by the hegumen or by the Grand Protosyngellos (**Megas Protosyngellos** / *Μέγας Πρωτοσύγκελλος*) when he presides in the absence of the Patriarch.

**TIMIOTERA** (*Τιμιωτέρα, ἡ*; Slav. *Chestneyshuyu* / *Честнѣйшую*; lit. "More honorable"): The six strophes of the Ninth Biblical Canticle or Song of the Most Holy Theotokos (Magnificat, Luke 1:46–55), beginning: **"My soul doth magnify the Lord"** (*«Μεγαλύνει ἡ ψυχή μου τὸν Κύριον»*), each verse accompanied by the venerable refrain: **"More honorable than the Cherubim, and more glorious beyond compare than the Seraphim, who without corruption gavest birth to God the Word, the very Theotokos, thee do we magnify"** (*«Τὴν τιμιωτέραν τῶν Χερουβείμ, καὶ ἐνδοξοτέραν ἀσυγκρίτως τῶν Σεραφείμ, τὴν ἀδιαφθόρως Θεὸν Λόγον τεκοῦσαν, τὴν ὄντως Θεοτόκον, σὲ μεγαλύνομεν»*). The Timiotera is chanted at Matins (**Orthros**) following the Eighth Ode of the Katavasiai and preceding the Exapostilarion.

**TONE** (*ἦχος, ὁ*; Slav. *hlas* / *гласъ*): See **MODE**.[^691]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Tonsure – Translation of Relics – Translation to Heaven Part 1) [Demos, p. 1127 / TD-148]

**TONSURE** (*κουρά, ἡ*; Slav. *postryzheniye* / *постриженіе*): In the Eastern Church, tonsure signifies the ceremonial cutting of four locks of hair from the head of the candidate in the form of a cross: at the crown of the head as the celebrant bishop, priest, or hegumen proclaims: **"In the Name of the Father"** (*«Εἰς τὸ ὄνομα τοῦ Πατρός»*); at the back of the head: **"and of the Son"** (*«καὶ τοῦ Υἱοῦ»*); and upon either side of the head: **"and of the Holy Spirit"** (*«καὶ τοῦ Ἁγίου Πνεύματος»*). The rite of tonsure represents a dedication and sacrificial offering of oneself to God and forms an integral component of several sacred mysteries and rites.

In contemporary practice, three distinct types of tonsure are celebrated: baptismal, monastic, and clerical. In all cases, the shorn hair is permitted to grow naturally, the tonsure not being retained as a permanent tonsured hairstyle; the severed hair is reverently consumed by fire within the censer.

In the rite of Holy Baptism, as an initial sacrificial offering made to God by the newly illuminated, tonsure is performed; it is likewise administered when an adult convert is received through Holy Chrismation.

Monastic tonsure—embracing three progressive grades: the Rassophore (*ῥασοφόρος*), the Stavrophore (*σταυροφόρος* / Small Schema), and the Great and Angelic Schema (*μεγαλόσχημος*)—constitutes the rite of initiation into the monastic life.

Tonsure is also administered prior to the chirothesia of a layman to the minor order of Reader (**Anagnostes** / *ἀναγνώστης*). This liturgical sequence has given rise to the common expression that a candidate is "tonsured a reader," although canonically the rite of tonsure immediately precedes the ordination proper effected by the imposition of hands (*chirothesia* / *χειροθεσία*).

**TRANSLATION (OF RELICS)** (*ἀνακομιδὴ λειψάνων, ἡ*; Slav. *pereneseniye moshchey* / *перенесеніе мощей*): The solemn transfer of the relics of a holy saint or martyr from one shrine or tomb to another. For example, on January 27, the Church celebrates the Translation of the Relics of Saint John Chrysostom, which were translated in 438 A.D. from Cucusus (*Κουκουσός*), an isolated village in Armenia where he had reposed in exile, to the Church of the Holy Apostles in Constantinople.

**TRANSLATION (TO HEAVEN)** (*μετάστασις, ἡ*; Slav. *prestavleniye* / *преставленіе*; lit. "transference"): The ascent to heaven of a holy personage prior to physical dissolution; commemorated, for example, on September 26 as the Translation of Saint John the Evangelist and Theologian. According to Theophanes[^692]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Translation to Heaven Concluded – Trapeza – Triadika – Triadikon – Trikiria – Triodion – Trisagion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1128 / TD-149]

the Hymnographer and Caesarius (the brother of Saint Gregory the Theologian), Saint John the Evangelist did not undergo natural physical corruption, but was bodily taken up into heaven—a pious tradition that gained widespread currency in the early medieval era. While the Church has never formulated this pious belief as a binding dogma of faith, it commemorates the departure of Saint John not as a Dormition (*koimesis* / *κοίμησις* / sleep, repose), but as a *metastasis* (*μετάστασις* / translation), honoring the Evangelist as Christ's "beloved disciple" and as the visionary seer of the Apocalypse.

**TRAPEZA** (*τράπεζα, ἡ*; Slav. *trapeza* / *трапеза*; lit. "table"): The monastic refectory or dining hall where monastics assemble for communal meals. See **REFECTORY**.

**TRIADIKA** (*τριαδικά, τά*; Slav. *troichni* / *троичны*): A suite of four hymns composed in honor of the Holy Trinity, appointed in the Sunday **Midnight Office** (**Mesonyktikon** / *Μεσονυκτικόν*) within the **Great Horologion** (*Ὡρολόγιον τὸ Μέγα*). They are chanted toward the conclusion of this office prior to the commencement of Matins (**Orthros**). They are likewise designated the Triadic Magnifications (*Τριαδικὰ Μεγαλυνάρια*).

**TRIADIKON** (*τριαδικόν, τό*; Slav. *troichen* / *троиченъ*): A hymn or troparion composed in honor of the Holy Trinity.

**TRIKIRIA** (*τρικήρια, τά*, sing. *τρικήριον, τό*; Slav. *trykyriy* / *трикирій*): Three-branched candlesticks utilized by a presiding hierarch during pontifical divine services to bless the assembly, symbolizing the three Persons of the Holy Trinity.

**TRIODION** (*Τριῴδιον, τό*; Slav. *Postnaya Triod* / *Постная Тріодь*; lit. "Three Odes"): The term bears two liturgical significations: (1) The liturgical season commencing upon the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee and concluding upon Holy Saturday; and (2) the liturgical service book containing the hymnography, scriptural readings, and proper rubrics for all movable feasts and fasts from the Sunday of the Publican and the Pharisee through Holy Saturday.

**TRISAGION HYMN** or **TRISAGION** (*Τρισάγιος Ὕμνος, ὁ*, *Τρισάγιον, τό*; Slav. *Trisvyatoye* / *Трисвятое*; lit. "Thrice-Holy Hymn"): The liturgical hymn chanted following the Small Entrance and the Dismissal Troparia and Kontakion in the Divine Liturgy. The divine name *Agios* (**Holy**) is repeated thrice in the invocation—**"Holy God, Holy Mighty, Holy Immortal, have mercy on us"** (*«Ἅγιος ὁ Θεός, Ἅγιος Ἰσχυρός, Ἅγιος Ἀθάνατος, ἐλέησον ἡμᾶς»*)—whence the designation Trisagion. On the Sunday of Pascha, Pentecost Sunday, the Nativity of Christ (Christmas), and the Theophany of the Lord, **"All of you who have been baptized into Christ"** (*«Ὅσοι εἰς Χριστὸν ἐβαπτίσθητε»* / Galatians 3:27) is chanted in place of the[^693]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Trisagion Concluded – Trisagion tou Vimatos – Triskelion – Troparion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1129 / TD-150]

Trisagion Hymn. On September 14 (Universal Exaltation of the Precious Cross) and the Third Sunday of the Great Fast (Veneration of the Precious Cross), **"To Thy Cross we bow down in worship, O Master, and Thy holy Resurrection we glorify"** (*«Τὸν Σταυρόν Σου προσκυνοῦμεν, Δέσποτα, καὶ τὴν ἁγίαν Σου Ἀνάστασιν δοξάζομεν»*) is chanted in place of the Trisagion Hymn. According to liturgical witnesses, this hymn dates to the middle of the fifth century. The Trisagion continues to be chanted in Greek in certain Western rites, notably during the *Improperia* (Reproaches) of Good Friday (*Agios o Theos, Sanctus Deus*).

**TRISAGION TOU VIMATOS** (*Τρισάγιον τοῦ Βήματος*): The Thrice-Holy Hymn as chanted when a bishop presides at the Divine Liturgy. In the hierarchal order of the Divine Liturgy, the elaborate antiphonal phrases of the hymn are chanted alternately by the clergy from within the Sanctuary (*Bema* / *Βῆμα*)—whence the designation *tou Vimatos* (of the Sanctuary)—and answered by the chanters or choir upon the soleas.

**TRISKELION**, pl. **TRISKELIA** (*τρισκέλιον, τό*, pl. *τρισκέλια, τά*; Slav. *trekhnozhnik* / *треножникъ*): A portable three-legged stand or lectern utilized for holding liturgical books or sacred icons.

**TROPARION**, pl. **TROPARIA** (*τροπάριον, τό*, pl. *τροπάρια, τά*; Slav. *tropar*, pl. *tropari* / *тропарь*, *тропари*): One of the most ancient designations in Eastern hymnography for an ecclesiastical poetic strophe composed in free meter, as distinguished from a quoted scriptural text. While as a generic term *Troparion* may designate virtually any ecclesiastical hymn, in liturgical taxonomy it specifically denotes three principal categories: (1) Dismissal Troparia (*ἀπολυτίκια*, lit. "apolytikia"; Slav. *otpustytelni* / *отпустительны*), namely troparia of the Resurrection, of the Feast, or of the Saint of the day, summarizing the central theological theme of the commemoration. These are chanted prior to the dismissal at Vespers and repeated throughout the offices of the day—at Matins, Compline, the Hours, and at the Divine Liturgy following the Small Entrance; (2) Troparia Evlogitaria (*Εὐλογητάρια* / Slav. *Neporochny*), a suite of resurrectional strophes, each preceded by the scriptural refrain: **"Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes"** (*«Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε, δίδαξόν με τὰ δικαιώματά σου»* / Psalm 118:12 [MT 119:12]), chanted at Sunday Matins following the seventeenth Kathisma of the Psalter and at the Matins of Holy Saturday; and (3) Troparia of a Canon (*κανών*), the concise strophes following the **Heirmos** (*εἱρμός*) in each Ode of the canon. The verses chanted at the Matins of Holy[^694]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Troparion Concluded – Tropeophoros – Tou Naou – Typika Part 1) [Demos, p. 1130 / TD-151]

Saturday with the verses of Psalm 118 [MT 119] are likewise designated troparia. The term *Troparion* indicates that the hymn is composed in a defined rhythmic style or melodic pattern (Greek *tropos* / *τρόπος*, mode, manner, or turn). A Troparion provides a concise theological distillation of the sacred event or holy saint celebrated in the services. In early hymnography, it served as an antiphonal response and refrain to the psalm verses chanted by the cantor. The name Troparion was thus originally applied to short prayers composed in rhythmic prose and inserted after each verse of a psalm.

In the fifth century, when troparia expanded into strophic forms of greater length, these poetic prayers were chanted only after the final three to six verses of a psalm. Hymns of this character formed part of Matins and Vespers in ecclesiastical and monastic centers of the fifth century. In this era, the divine office consisted of the Psalter, the nine Biblical Odes or Canticles, established liturgical formulas deriving from apostolic antiquity, and troparia composed by contemporary hymnographers. This practice, however, did not obtain in strictly secluded desert monastic communities: anchorites and hermits of the strict desert tradition, according to surviving accounts, rejected all forms of florid melodic chanting.

**TROPEOPHOROS** (*τροπαιοφόρος, ὁ*; Slav. *pobedonosets* / *побѣдоносецъ*; lit. "trophy-bearer" or "victorious"): An ecclesiastical epithet accorded to holy martyrs who triumphed over suffering and death through steadfast witness to Christ (notably Saint George the Great Martyr and Trophy-Bearer).

**TOU NAOU** (*τοῦ ναοῦ*; lit. "of the temple" or "of the church"): The designation for the Dismissal Troparion (*ἀπολυτίκιον*, lit. "apolytikion") honoring the patron saint or sacred mystery to which a church edifice is dedicated and after which it is named. The complete Greek term is *Ἀπολυτίκιον τοῦ ναοῦ* (Dismissal Troparion of the Temple). The Dismissal Troparion of the Temple is chanted among the Dismissal Troparia following the Small Entrance at the Divine Liturgy prior to the Kontakion.

**TYPIKA** (*Τυπικά, τά*; Slav. *Izobrazitelna* / *Изобразительны* or *Obidnytsia* / *Обѣдница*): The term possesses two liturgical senses: first, it serves as the plural of the noun **Typikon**; second, it designates the Reader's service or Mid-day office appointed to be celebrated whenever the Eucharistic Divine Liturgy is not served, whether because the Typikon prohibits the celebration of the Liturgy (as on aliturgical weekdays of the Great Fast), or during[^695]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Typika Concluded – Typikon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1131 / TD-152]

the lesser fasts on weekdays for which only a simple commemoration is appointed in the Menaion. The Typika may likewise be read in place of the Divine Liturgy if no priest is present, or if the priest is prevented from celebrating (whether on account of necessary ascetical preparation, unforeseen impediments, illness, or emergency).

The Typika office, which in ancient monastic practice was chanted prior to the **Beatitudes** (*Μακαρισμοί, οἱ*) at the commencement of the Divine Liturgy, consists principally of Psalm 102 [MT 103]: **"Bless the Lord, O my soul"** (*«Εὐλόγει, ἡ ψυχή μου, τὸν Κύριον»*) and Psalm 145 [MT 146]: **"Praise the Lord, O my soul"** (*«Αἴνει, ἡ ψυχή μου, τὸν Κύριον»*). The service incorporates the fundamental psalmody and prayers of the Liturgy of the Catechumens. Under ordinary rubrics, the Typika is read following the Third and Sixth Hours (in the temporal position where the Divine Liturgy would otherwise be celebrated). During the Great Fast, the Ninth Hour precedes the Typika and the structural order is adapted.

During the reading of the Typika, proper troparia may be intercalated between the verses of the Beatitudes, as during the celebration of the Divine Liturgy. During the Great Fast, however, this hymnographic interpolation is omitted; instead, the Beatitudes are chanted by the choir with the penitential refrain intercalated between each verse: **"Remember us, O Lord, when Thou comest into Thy kingdom"** (*«Μνήσθητι ἡμῶν, Κύριε, ὅταν ἔλθῃς ἐν τῇ βασιλείᾳ Σου»* / Luke 23:42).

In the parochial celebration of the Divine Liturgy, the traditional Typika psalms have largely been superseded by the three Antiphons: the First Antiphon refrain: **"Through the intercessions of the Theotokos, O Savior, save us"** (*«Ταῖς πρεσβείαις τῆς Θεοτόκου, Σῶτερ, σῶσον ἡμᾶς»*), and the Second Antiphon: **"Save us, O Son of God... who sing to Thee: Alleluia"** (*«Σῶσον ἡμᾶς, Υἱὲ Θεοῦ... ψάλλοντάς Σοι· Ἀλληλούϊα»*). The Beatitudes have likewise been superseded by the Third Antiphon, consisting of the Dismissal Troparion chanted with the Entrance Verse.

**TYPIKON**, pl. **TYPIKA** (*Τυπικόν, τό*, pl. *Τυπικά, τά*; Slav. *Ustav* / *Уставъ*; from Greek *typos* / *τύπος*, meaning pattern, model, rule, or form): The authoritative canonical liturgical codex regulating, arranging, and outlining the ritual order[^696]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Typikon Concluded – Verse – Vespers – Vestment[s] Part 1) [Demos, p. 1132 / TD-153]

(*τάξις, ἡ*, class or order) of every divine service throughout the ecclesiastical calendar. The normative Typikon governing contemporary Byzantine practice is George Violakis's *Typikon of the Great Church of Christ* (*Τυπικὸν τῆς τοῦ Χριστοῦ Μεγάλης Ἐκκλησίας*, Constantinople, 1888).
