This monumental work serves as the foundation for modern annual liturgical manuals, such as the *Diptycha* (*Δίπτυχα*) issued by the Apostoliki Diakonia of the Church of Greece, the *Taxis ton Ieron Akolouthion* (*Τάξις τῶν Ἱερῶν Ἀκολουθιῶν*) of George G. Bekatoros (Athens), and the *Liturgical Guide Book* of Frank Desby (Columbus, Ohio). The rubrical dispositions of the Violakis Typikon have from time to time received authoritative modifications by the Ecumenical Patriarchate of Constantinople.

The term *Typikon* likewise designates the monastic rule or founding constitution governing the ascetical life and liturgical observance of a particular monastery (*ktetorikon typikon* / *κτητορικὸν τυπικόν*).

For comprehensive scholarly analyses of the historical evolution of the Typikon, see the studies by Fr. Alexander Schmemann (*Introduction to Liturgical Theology*, pp. 213–220) and Fr. Alkiviadis Calivas (*Great Week and Pascha in the Greek Orthodox Church*, p. 10).

**VERSE** (*στίχος, ὁ*; Slav. *stikh* / *стихъ*): A line of poetic or scriptural text. In Byzantine hymnography, a *stichos* is typically a psalm verse chanted in recitative (*chyma* / *χύμα*) prior to a hymn chanted with an expansive melodic rhythm. Exemplified by the evening stichira of **"Lord, I have cried"** (*Κύριε ἐκέκραξα*) at Vespers, and the stichira of the Praises (*Ainoi* / *Αἶνοι*) at Matins (**Orthros**).

**VESPERS** (*Ἑσπερινός, ὁ*; Slav. *Vechirnya* / *Вечерня*; from *hespera* / *ἑσπέρα*, evening): The foundational opening service of the liturgical day, celebrated at eventide. In the ecclesiastical calendar, the liturgical day begins at sunset of the preceding civil day (Genesis 1:5: "And there was evening and there was morning, one day") and concludes at sunset of the calendar day itself.

**VESTMENT(S)** (*ἄμφιον, τό*, pl. *ἄμφια, τά*; Slav. *oblacheniye* / *облаченіе* or *rizí* / *ризы*): See also **AMFIA**. The sacred vestments worn by the ordained clergy during divine services, distinguished from non-liturgical daily clerical attire worn beneath the vestments.[^697]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Colors – Reader – Subdeacon and Deacon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1133 / TD-154]

With regard to liturgical vestment colors, the ancient Typikon specifies only "light" (*leuka* / *λευκά*) or "dark" (*skoteina* / *σκοτεινά*) vestments; the elaborate modern color schemes reflect subsequent diocesan custom rather than universal statutory mandate.

Under the general heading of vestments, the liturgical vestures are classified according to the clerical orders that wear them:

LITURGICAL VESTMENTS:

**Reader** (**Anagnostes** / *ἀναγνώστης, ὁ*; Slav. *Chtets* / *чтецъ*): The essential liturgical duty of the reader is to recite the lections and psalms from the Old Testament (Paremias) and the Epistle (Apostolos) lessons from the New Testament during divine services. He likewise recites non-sacramental prayers (such as the Trisagion Prayers) and chants the psalm verses for appointed antiphons. The candidate is set apart through a dedicated liturgical rite of tonsure and chirothesia, though in contemporary circumstances a layman may receive an ad-hoc blessing from the priest to read on a given occasion.

Readers are permitted to wear the inner cassock (*anteri* / *ἀντερί*) according to local parish tradition, customarily donned while assisting at services. At his ordination, the newly tonsured reader is vested in the Reader's short phelonion (*μικρὸν φαιλόνιον* / *kravatsion* / *кукуліонъ* / Slav. *malyi felon*), in which he recites the Epistle lection for the first time. This vestment is immediately removed and is not donned again; it is replaced by the Reader's **Sticharion** (*στιχάριον*), which the reader wears thereafter whenever reciting the Epistle. This practice is not universally observed, however, and many bishops permit a reader to perform his duties vested solely in the cassock.

**Subdeacon and Deacon** (*Ὑποδιάκονος καὶ Διάκονος*): Each wears the **Sticharion** (*στιχάριον, τό*) and the **Orarion** (**Stole** / *ὠράριον, τό*). The diaconal sticharion, historically corresponding to the Western dalmatic and tunicle, is a long, straight-cut outer tunic with broad, flowing sleeves covering the entire person. Representing the white robe bestowed at Holy Baptism, it is fashioned from rich[^698]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Deacon: Sticharion – Orarion – Epimanikia – Cassock – Kalymmachion) [Demos, p. 1134 / TD-155]

brocade fabric and is more ornamented than that of the reader. It is designated "the robe of salvation and the garment of joy," symbolizing a pure and tranquil conscience, a blameless life, and the spiritual joy in the Lord that flows into him who wears it.

The **Orarion** (**Stole** / *ὠράριον, τό*; Slav. *orar* / *орарь*) is the distinctive vestment worn by the deacon (across all ranks: subdeacon, deacon, protodeacon, or archdeacon). In certain Slavic traditions, a deacon may be awarded the double orarion as an ecclesiastical distinction; in Byzantine Greek praxis, however, all grades of the diaconate wear the double orarion. The orarion is a long, narrow band of brocade draped over the left shoulder, wrapped about the chest to the waist in back, and brought over the left shoulder to the front. The Subdeacon wears his stole crossed permanently across the chest and back for ease of manual service. The Deacon, however, binds his orarion crosswise about his body shortly before the consecration of the Holy Gifts in the Anaphora, typifying the wings of the Seraphim who minister before the heavenly Altar (Isaiah 6:2–3), as the deacons themselves, as sacred ministers, typify the Cherubim and Seraphim. The angelic Trisagion hymn—**"Holy, Holy, Holy"** (*«Ἅγιος, Ἅγιος, Ἅγιος»*)—is frequently embroidered upon the orarion. The stole is bound crosswise about the Subdeacon at his ordination, symbolizing that through purity of heart and bodily continence he dons the vesture of chastity.

The Deacon likewise wears the **Cuffs** (**Epimanikia** / *ἐπιμανίκια, τά*; Slav. *poruchi* / *поручи*), which are fastened with laces about the wrists beneath the sleeves of the sticharion (in contrast to priests and bishops, who wear them over the sleeves of the inner sticharion). They admonish the deacon not to place trust in his own human strength, but in the right hand of the Lord, Almighty and merciful God, and in His divine strength and assistance.

Following ordination, the deacon receives the blessing to wear the outer cassock (**Exorason** / *ἐξώρασον*) over the inner cassock (**Anteri** / *ἀντερί*). In Greek practice, he may wear the black cylindrical clerical head-covering designated the **Kalymmachion** (**Kamilavka** / *καλυμμαύχιον, τό* / Slav. *kamilavka*)[^699]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Concluded – Priest: Sticharion – Epitrachelion) [Demos, p. 1135 / TD-156]

without the monastic veil (*epanokalymmavchon*) proper to a bishop or hieromonk.

**Priest** (**Hiereus** / *ἱερεύς, ὁ*; Slav. *Ierey* / *іерей* or *Sviashchenyk* / *священикъ*): The sacerdotal vestments are enumerated in the canonical sequence in which they are donned:

(1) The **Sticharion** (*στιχάριον, τό*; Slav. *podriznik* / *подризникъ* or *stikhar*): The priestly sticharion has narrow, tapered sleeves and is fashioned from white or light-colored linen or silk, corresponding to the Western alb.

When vesting in the Sticharion, the priest takes it in his left hand, bows toward the East, and blesses the cross upon the back, proclaiming: **"Blessed is our God, always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεὸς ἡμῶν, πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*). He continues: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον»*). He then recites the vesting prayer: **"My soul shall rejoice in the Lord, for He hath clothed me with the garment of salvation, and with the tunic of gladness hath He encompassed me; as a bridegroom He hath set a crown upon me, and as a bride hath He adorned me with ornament; always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Ἀγαλλιάσεται ἡ ψυχή μου ἐπὶ τῷ Κυρίῳ· ἐνέδυσε γάρ με ἱμάτιον σωτηρίου, καὶ χιτῶνα εὐφροσύνης περιέβαλέ με· ὡς νυμφίῳ περιέθηκέ μοι μίτραν, καὶ ὡς νύμφην κατεκόσμησέ με κόσμῳ [Isaiah 61:10]. Πάντοτε· νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

(2) The **Epitrachelion** (**Priestly Stole** / *ἐπιτραχήλιον, τό*; Slav. *epitrakhil* / *єпитрахиль*; from *epi*, upon, and *trachelos*, neck): The epitrachelion consists of a broad band of brocade worn about the neck and hanging straight down in front, permanently joined along its entire length. It signifies the outpouring of sacerdotal consecrating grace upon the priesthood (Psalm 132:2 [MT 133:2]). The priest, like the deacon, cannot celebrate any divine office without his stole. Vested in the Epitrachelion alone, without the **Phelonion** (*φαιλόνιον*), he celebrates the less solemn offices: Small Vespers, ordinary Compline, the Midnight Office (Greek *Mesonyktikon* / Slav. *Polunoshchnitsa*), and the Hours (when no Gospel is appointed to be read). At diverse prayer services (*treby*) in private dwellings, such as the prayer at the birth of a child or the blessing of homes, the priest likewise wears the Epitrachelion.[^700]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Priest: Epitrachelion Concluded – Zone – Epimanikia Part 1) [Demos, p. 1136 / TD-157]

When vesting with the **Epitrachelion** (*ἐπιτραχήλιον*), the priest signs a cross over it and prays: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Blessed is God Who poureth forth His grace upon His priests, as ointment upon the head, which runneth down over the beard, the beard of Aaron, which runneth down to the hem of his garment"** (Psalm 132:2 [MT 133:2]); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεός, ὁ ἐκχέων τὴν χάριν αὐτοῦ ἐπὶ τοὺς ἱερεῖς αὐτοῦ· ὡς μύρον ἐπὶ κεφαλῆς, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ πώγωνα, τὸν πώγωνα τοῦ Ἀαρών, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ τὴν ᾤαν τοῦ ἐνδύματος αὐτοῦ· Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **ZONE** (**Girdle** or **Belt** / *ζώνη, ἡ*; Slav. *poyas* / *поясъ*): A textile belt wherewith the priest girds himself over his Epitrachelion and Sticharion to secure them in place for convenience during divine service. It symbolizes the gift of spiritual strength wherewith God assists him in his sacerdotal ministry, and admonishes him unto purity and blamelessness of life. It likewise denotes the priest's readiness to serve the Lord and that he is bound unto Christ. In putting on this girdle, he blesses the cross embroidered upon it and prays: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Blessed is God Who girdeth me with strength, and hath made my way blameless"** (Psalm 17:33 [MT 18:32]); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεός, ὁ περιζωννύων με δύναμιν, καὶ ἔθετο ἄμωμον τὴν ὁδόν μου· Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **EPIMANIKIA** (**Liturgical Cuffs** / *ἐπιμανίκια, τά*, sing. *ἐπιμανίκιον, τό*; also termed *ἐπιμάνικα* or *ὑπομάνικα*; Slav. *poruchi* / *поручи*): These are the ceremonial cuffs that are likewise worn by the deacon; however, whereas the deacon laces them about his wrists beneath the wide sleeves of the outer sticharion, the priest wears them laced securely over the tapered sleeves of the priestly sticharion. When vesting with the cuffs, the priest recites the appointed prayers: first, for the right cuff, he prays: **"Thy right hand, O Lord, is glorified in strength; Thy right hand, O Lord, hath shattered the enemy, and in the multitude of Thy glory Thou hast crushed the adversaries"** (Exodus 15:6–7); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Ἡ δεξιά Σου, Κύριε, δεδόξασται ἐν ἰσχύϊ· ἡ δεξιά Σου χείρ, Κύριε, ἔθραυσεν*[^701]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Priest: Epimanikia Concluded – Epigonation / Palitsa / Nabedrennik Part 1) [Demos, p. 1137 / TD-158]

*ἐχθρούς· καὶ τῷ πλήθει τῆς δόξης Σου συνέτριψας τοὺς ὑπεναντίους. Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*). And then for the left cuff, he prays: **"Thy hands have made me and fashioned me; give me understanding, and I shall learn Thy commandments"** (Psalm 118:73 [MT 119:73]); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Αἱ χεῖρές Σου ἐποίησάν με καὶ ἔπλασάν με· συνέτισόν με καὶ μαθήσομαι τὰς ἐντολάς Σου· Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*). The liturgical cuffs symbolize the bonds endured by Christ at His saving Passion, reminding the sacred minister of the Church that he must rely not upon his own human strength, but upon the help of God.

The **EPIGONATION** (*ἐπιγονάτιον, τό*, or *ὑπογονάτιον, τό*; Slav. *palitsa* / *палица*; from *epi*, upon, and *gonu*, knee): A stiffened, lozenge- or diamond-shaped piece of brocade suspended by a ribbon from the belt (Zone) over the right hip as an ecclesiastical award of distinction for a deserving priest (protoiereus, archpriest, or economos). Its historical origin is traced to the Byzantine imperial practice whereby emperors awarded ceremonial swords to military commanders in recognition of valor in defense of the empire. Such swords were accompanied by an elaborate thigh-shield suspended beneath the hilt to shield the warrior's leg from bruising caused by the motion of the scabbard. Spiritually, the Epigonation represents the Sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God (Ephesians 6:17). It is likewise interpreted mystically as symbolizing the linen towel wherewith the Lord girded Himself to wash the feet of the disciples at the Mystical Supper (John 13:4–5). In Slavic traditions, a priest awarded the Epigonation (*palitsa*) may previously have received the rectangular thigh-shield (Slav. *nabedrennik* / *набедренникъ*). The *nabedrennik* is likewise a stiffened brocade vestment, rectangular in shape, worn suspended over the right hip by a strap passed over the left shoulder. The *nabedrennik* is not utilized in the Byzantine Greek tradition. In Slavic usage, if a priest possesses only the *palitsa*, it is worn upon the right hip; if he has also been awarded the *nabedrennik*, the *palitsa* is transferred to the left hip, while the *nabedrennik* occupies the right hip.

When vesting with the Epigonation, the priest blesses it and prays: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Gird Thy sword upon Thy thigh, O Mighty One, in Thy splendor and in Thy beauty! String Thy bow, go[^702]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Priest: Epigonation Concluded – Phelonion – Bishop: Sticharion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1138 / TD-159]

forth and prosper in the cause of truth, meekness, and righteousness; and Thy right hand shall lead Thee marvelously"** (Psalm 44:4–5 [MT 45:3–4]); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Περίζωσαι τὴν ῥομφαίαν σου ἐπὶ τὸν μηρόν σου, Δυνατέ, τῇ ὡραιότητί σου καὶ τῷ κάλλει σου· καὶ ἔντεινε, καὶ κατευοδοῦ, καὶ βασίλευε ἕνεκεν ἀληθείας, καὶ πραότητος, καὶ δικαιοσύνης· καὶ ὁδηγήσει σε θαυμαστῶς ἡ δεξιά σου. Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **PHELONION** (**Felonion** / *φελόνιον* or *φαιλόνιον, τό*; Slav. *felon* / *фелонь* or *riza* / *риза*): The distinctive outer vestment of the priest. It is a long, sleeveless conical vestment resembling a cape, shortened in the front to facilitate the unhindered movement of his hands, with an opening for the head. The Phelonion is donned over all the other sacerdotal vestments.

It symbolizes the seamless robe and the purple mantle wherewith Christ was clothed during His Passion. It stands as a reminder that the priest is clothed with righteousness (Psalm 131:9 [MT 132:9]) and thereby shielded from all iniquities. For many centuries, the phelonion (specifically the polystavrion) was likewise worn by bishops until it was replaced among hierarchs by the **Sakkos** (*σάκκος*). The Eastern phelonion corresponds historically and functionally to the Western chasuble.

When vesting with the Phelonion, the priest reverences the cross upon the back of the vestment as he prays: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Thy priests, O Lord, shall be clothed with righteousness, and Thy holy ones shall rejoice with joy"** (Psalm 131:9 [MT 132:9]); **"always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Οἱ ἱερεῖς Σου, Κύριε, ἐνδύσονται δικαιοσύνην, καὶ οἱ ὅσιοί Σου ἀγαλλιάσει ἀγαλλιάσονται· Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

**BISHOP** (**Hierarch** / *Ἐπίσκοπος, ὁ*; Slav. *Yepyskop* / *Єпископъ* or *Archierey* / *Архіерей*): The bishop wears all the sacerdotal vestments of a priest, with the exception of the Phelonion, which is replaced by the Sakkos, and is adorned with additional pontifical insignia.

As the Bishop vests with the **Sticharion** (*στιχάριον*), the First Deacon presents it, bowing, and proclaims: **"Bless, Master, the holy sticharion. Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy."** (*«Εὐλόγησον, δέσποτα, τὸ ἱερὸν στιχάριον. Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον»*). Then he[^703]

continues: **"My soul shall rejoice in the Lord; for He hath clothed me with the garment of salvation, and with the tunic of gladness hath He encompassed me; as a bridegroom He hath set a crown upon me, and as a bride hath He adorned me with ornament"** (Isaiah 61:10) (*«Ἀγαλλιάσεται ἡ ψυχή μου ἐπὶ τῷ Κυρίῳ· ἐνέδυσε γάρ με ἱμάτιον σωτηρίου, καὶ χιτῶνα εὐφροσύνης περιέβαλέ με· ὡς νυμφίῳ περιέθηκέ μοι μίτραν, καὶ ὡς νύμφην κατεκόσμησέ με κόσμῳ»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

As the Bishop vests with the **Epitrachelion** (*ἐπιτραχήλιον*), the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Blessed is God Who poureth forth His grace upon His priests, as ointment upon the head, which runneth down upon the beard, the beard of Aaron, which runneth down to the fringe of his garment"** (Psalm 132:2 [MT 133:2]) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεός, ὁ ἐκχέων τὴν χάριν αὐτοῦ ἐπὶ τοὺς ἱερεῖς αὐτοῦ, ὡς μύρον ἐπὶ κεφαλῆς, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ πώγωνα, τὸν πώγωνα τοῦ Ἀαρών, τὸ καταβαῖνον ἐπὶ τὴν ᾤαν τοῦ ἐνδύματος αὐτοῦ»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

As the Bishop vests with the **Zone** (**Belt** / *ζώνη*), the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Blessed is God Who girdeth me with strength, and hath made my way blameless"** (Psalm 17:33 [MT 18:32]) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Εὐλογητὸς ὁ Θεός, ὁ περιζωννύων με δύναμιν, καὶ ἔθετο ἄμωμον τὴν ὁδόν μου»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

As the Bishop vests with the **Epimanikia** (**Cuffs** / *ἐπιμανίκια*), the First[^704]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Bishop: Epimanikia Concluded – Sakkos) [Demos, p. 1140 / TD-161]

Deacon proclaims first for the Right Cuff: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Thy right hand, O Lord, is glorified in strength; Thy right hand, O Lord, hath shattered the enemy, and in the multitude of Thy glory Thou hast crushed the adversaries"** (Exodus 15:6–7) (*«Ἡ δεξιά Σου, Κύριε, δεδόξασται ἐν ἰσχύϊ· ἡ δεξιά Σου χείρ, Κύριε, ἔθραυσεν ἐχθρούς· καὶ τῷ πλήθει τῆς δόξης Σου συνέτριψας τοὺς ὑπεναντίους»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer with: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The First Deacon then proclaims for the Left Cuff: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Thy hands have made me and fashioned me; give me understanding, and I shall learn Thy commandments"** (Psalm 118:73 [MT 119:73]) (*«Αἱ χεῖρές Σου ἐποίησάν με καὶ ἔπλασάν με· συνέτισόν με καὶ μαθήσομαι τὰς ἐντολάς Σου»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*). The cuffs symbolize the bonds endured by Christ during His Passion, reminding the sacred minister of the Church that he must place confidence not in his own human power, but in the divine help of God.

The **SAKKOS** (*σάκκος, ὁ*; Slav. *sakkos* / *саккосъ*; lit. "sackcloth" or garment of penitential humility): In divine services, with the sole exception of the Phelonion, the bishop wears all the vestments of a priest. Originally, bishops likewise wore the phelonion (embellished as the polystavrion), but it was subsequently replaced by the Sakkos, a tunic-like garment with short half-sleeves, modeled after the imperial dalmatic of Byzantine emperors; as such, it constitutes a mark of high ecclesiastical honor and episcopal dignity. Symbolically, while recalling the mockery of the purple robe placed upon the Savior, it admonishes the bishop to ascend unto eminent holiness of life. As the Bishop vests with the Sakkos, the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Thy high priests, O Lord, shall be clothed with righteousness, and Thy holy ones shall rejoice with joy"** (Psalm 131:9 [MT 132:9]) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Οἱ ἀρχιερεῖς Σου, Κύριε, ἐνδύσονται δικαιοσύνην, καὶ οἱ ὅσιοί Σου ἀγαλλιάσει ἀγαλλιάσονται»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).[^705]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Bishop: Epigonation / Palitsa / Nabedrennik – Omophorion Part 1) [Demos, p. 1141 / TD-162]

The **EPIGONATION** (*ἐπιγονάτιον, τό*, or *ὑπογονάτιον, τό*; Slav. *palitsa* / *палица*): A stiffened diamond-shaped piece of brocade suspended from the Zone (Belt) over the right hip, worn universally by all bishops as a mandatory pontifical vestment, whereas for priests it is granted only as an ecclesiastical award of distinction. Its origin is traced to the custom of Byzantine emperors presenting ceremonial swords to military generals for valor in defending the empire. These swords were accompanied by a padded thigh-shield suspended beneath the weapon to protect the thigh against friction while marching. Mystically, the Epigonation represents the Sword of the Spirit, which is the Word of God (Ephesians 6:17). It is likewise explained as symbolizing the towel wherewith the Lord girded Himself to wash the feet of the disciples (John 13:4–5). In the Slavic traditions, those priests honored with the Epigonation (*palitsa*) may also have been awarded the rectangular thigh-shield (Slav. *nabedrennik* / *набедренникъ*), which is worn over the right hip suspended from a strap over the left shoulder. The *nabedrennik* is not utilized in the Byzantine Greek tradition. In Slavic usage, if a priest possesses only the *palitsa*, it is worn upon the right hip; if he has also been awarded the *nabedrennik*, the *palitsa* is transferred to the left hip, while the *nabedrennik* is worn upon the right.

As the Bishop vests with the Epigonation, the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Gird Thy sword upon Thy thigh, O Mighty One, in Thy splendor and in Thy beauty! String Thy bow, go forth, prosper, and reign, for the sake of truth, meekness, and righteousness; and Thy right hand shall lead Thee marvelously"** (Psalm 44:4–5 [MT 45:3–4]) (*«Περίζωσαι τὴν ῥομφαίαν σου ἐπὶ τὸν μηρόν σου, Δυνατέ, τῇ ὡραιότητί σου καὶ τῷ κάλλει σου· καὶ ἔντεινε, καὶ κατευοδοῦ, καὶ βασίλευε ἕνεκεν ἀληθείας, καὶ πραότητος, καὶ δικαιοσύνης· καὶ ὁδηγήσει σε θαυμαστῶς ἡ δεξιά σου»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The distinctive vestment of the Bishop is the **OMOPHORION** (*ὠμοφόριον, τό*; Slav. *omofor* / *омофоръ*; from *omos*, shoulder, and *phero*, to bear)—a long, broad band of silk or brocade arranged upon the shoulders in such manner that one end hangs down in front and the other behind. Two forms of the Omophorion are utilized in divine services: the Great (or Long) Omophorion and the Small (or Short) Omophorion. Typically, the Great Omophorion is[^706]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Bishop: Omophorion Concluded – Pectoral Cross Part 1) [Demos, p. 1142 / TD-163]

worn when the bishop is vested for the Divine Liturgy from the beginning until the reading of the Holy Gospel. Thereafter, he is revested with the Small Omophorion, which is worn draped about the neck with both ends hanging down over the chest. The word *Omophorion*, signifying a "shoulder-covering," originally denoted a strip of woolen sheepskin worn over the shoulders by the aged and infirm for warmth. In later centuries, it was fashioned from the same rich fabric as the sacerdotal vestments; its spiritual origin, however, perpetually evokes the parable of the lost sheep which the Good Shepherd found and laid upon His own shoulders (Luke 15:4–6). Thus the bishop is reminded that he is entrusted to shepherd his flock safely and to seek untiringly the conversion and restoration of the erring. The Omophorion is the principal symbol of the bishop's apostolic, spiritual, and canonical jurisdiction; clergy and ecclesiastical communities subject to a hierarch's jurisdiction are said to be "under his Omophorion."

The functional equivalent of the Omophorion in Western (Roman) Latin usage is the archiepiscopal *pallium*, although the reception and wear of the pallium are restricted to metropolitan archbishops under strict canonical rubrics, whereas every consecrated Eastern bishop wears the Omophorion by virtue of his episcopal order.

The flourishing of the Byzantine Empire and the symphonia between Church and State exerted a profound influence upon the development of Eastern pontifical vestments, which have preserved their majestic form essentially intact unto the present day.

As the Bishop vests with the Omophorion, the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. When Thou didst take upon Thy shoulders our human nature which had gone astray, O Christ, Thou didst ascend and offer it unto God Thy Father"** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Ἐπὶ τῶν ὤμων, Χριστέ, τὴν πλανηθεῖσαν ἄρας φύσιν, ἀναληφθεὶς τῷ Θεῷ καὶ Πατρὶ προσήγαγες»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **PECTORAL CROSS** (*σταυρὸς τοῦ στήθους* or *ἐπιστήθιος σταυρός, ὁ*; Latin *pectus*, chest; Slav. *nagrudniy krest* / *нагрудный крестъ*): The next pontifical ornament donned by the bishop, suspended about the neck by an ornate chain and resting upon the breast.

As the Bishop vests with the Pectoral Cross, the First Dea-[^707]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Bishop: Pectoral Cross Concluded – Engolpion / Panagia / Medallions Part 1) [Demos, p. 1143 / TD-164]

con proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Whosoever will come after Me, let him deny himself, and take up his cross, and follow Me"** (Mark 8:34) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Εἴ τις θέλει ὀπίσω Μου ἐλθεῖν, ἀπαρνησάσθω ἑαυτὸν καὶ ἀράτω τὸν σταυρὸν αὐτοῦ, καὶ ἀκολουθείτω Μοι»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **ENGOLPION** (*ἐγκόλπιον, τό*; from *en*, in/upon, and *kolpos*, chest or bosom; Slav. *nagrudnik* / *нагрудникъ*): The sacred badge worn upon the breast by both archpriests (formerly) and bishops, admonishing them that they must not only carry Christ within their hearts, but boldly confess Him before all mankind. Historically, the term *engolpion* included pectoral crosses and reliquary medallions; in modern usage, however, the designation applies primarily to an oval or circular medallion bearing an icon in enamel or relief, worn suspended by a chain over the chest by Eastern Orthodox and Eastern Catholic hierarchs. Certain engolpia are hollowed to serve as reliquaries (*enkolpia-reliquaries*). All bishops wear a specific form of engolpion depicting the Mother of God, designated the **Panagia** (*Παναγία*).

When the Bishop vests with the **First Medallion** (**First Engolpion** / *Ἐγκόλπιον α΄*) or the **Panagia** (*Παναγία, ἡ*; lit. "All-Holy Lady")—a circular icon of our Savior and the Most Holy Theotokos hanging from a golden chain over the breast—it reminds the hierarch that he must perpetually bear in his heart the Lord and His all-pure Mother, the supreme Intercessor before God; and to this end, his heart must remain undefiled and his spirit upright. When the bishop vests with this first engolpion, the Second Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν»*). The First Deacon then responds: **"Lord, have mercy."** (*«Κύριε, ἐλέησον»*), and continues: **"Create in me a clean heart, O God, and renew a right spirit within me"** (Psalm 50:12 [MT 51:10]) (*«Καρδίαν καθαρὰν κτίσον ἐν ἐμοί, ὁ Θεός, καὶ πνεῦμα εὐθὲς ἐγκαίνισον ἐν τοῖς ἐγκάτοις μου»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

When the Bishop vests with the **Second Medallion** (**Second Engolpion** / *Ἐγκόλπιον β΄*), which typically depicts Christ the Savior and is worn by metropolitans and patriarchal hierarchs, the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the[^708]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Continued – Bishop: Second Medallion Concluded – Miter – Pastoral Staff / Posokh Part 1) [Demos, p. 1144 / TD-165]

Lord. Lord, have mercy. My heart hath poured forth a good word; I speak of My works unto the King"** (Psalm 44:2 [MT 45:1]) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Ἐξηρεύξατο ἡ καρδία μου Λόγον ἀγαθόν, λέγω ἐγὼ τὰ ἔργα μου τῷ βασιλεῖ»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The liturgical head-covering of the bishop is the Episcopal Crown or **MITER** (**Mitra** / *μίτρα, ἡ*; Slav. *mitra* / *митра*).

The **MITER** (*μίτρα*), also spelled *mitre*, is a richly embroidered and adorned domed crown or diadem representing the crown of thorns worn by Christ during His holy Passion, and serving as an insignia of the pastoral authority bestowed upon the bishop as an apostle and shepherd of the Church. It is worn by all hierarchs in episcopal orders and is patterned architecturally upon the imperial crown (*kamelaukion*) of the Byzantine emperors.

It is historically improbable that the Christian episcopal miter was derived from the Old Testament high-priestly headdress (*miznephet*), since crown-like miters were not adopted by Eastern bishops until after the fall of Constantinople in the fifteenth century. While in Byzantine Greek usage the miter is reserved exclusively to hierarchs (bishops, metropolitans, and patriarchs), in Slavic praxis the privilege of wearing a miter has in recent centuries been granted to certain distinguished archimandrites and protopresbyters as an ecclesiastical decoration.

As the Bishop puts on the Miter, the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Thou hast set upon his head a crown of precious stones; he asked life of Thee, and Thou gavest him length of days"** (Psalm 20:4–5 [MT 21:3–4]) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Ἔθηκας ἐπὶ τὴν κεφαλήν αὐτοῦ στέφανον ἐκ λίθων τιμίων· ζωὴν ᾐτήσατό Σε καὶ ἔδωκας αὐτῷ μακρότητα ἡμερῶν»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **PASTORAL STAFF** (**Crozier** / *ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος, ἡ*; Slav. *posokh* / *посохъ* or *zhezl* / *жезлъ*) is bestowed upon bishops, and in certain Slavic traditions upon Archimandrites and Hegumens, as a visible symbol of their pastoral and spiritual authority over the monasteries[^709]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vestments Concluded – Bishop: Pastoral Staff Concluded – Dikerotrikera – Eagle / Aetos / Orlets) [Demos, p. 1145 / TD-166]

or eparchies committed to their charge, and as an emblem of their sacred duty to pasture the flock of Christ. The pastoral staff is fashioned of metal or carved wood, and its summit is shaped to resemble two entwined serpents facing an orb and cross, recalling the Savior's admonition: **"Be ye wise as serpents and simple as doves"** (Matthew 10:16). Just as a serpent each year sheds its old skin by pressing through narrow and thorny places, even so must the bishop guide others and walk himself along the narrow and arduous path of ascetic virtue leading unto the renewal of the soul.

When the Bishop receives the Pastoral Staff, the Second Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord."** (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν»*). The First Deacon responds: **"Lord, have mercy."** (*«Κύριε, ἐλέησον»*). The Second Deacon then proclaims: **"The Lord shall send Thee a rod of strength out of Zion: and rule Thou in the midst of Thine enemies"** (Psalm 109:2 [MT 110:2]) (*«Ῥάβδον δυνάμεως ἐξαποστελεῖ Σοι Κύριος ἐκ Σιών, καὶ κατακυρίευε ἐν μέσῳ τῶν ἐχθρῶν Σου»*). The Second Deacon then completes the prayer, saying: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

When the Bishop receives the two- and three-branched candelabras (**Dikerotrikera** / *δικηροτρίκηρα, τά*, comprising the *dikirion* / *δικήριον* and *trikirion* / *τρικήριον*), the First Deacon proclaims: **"Let us pray to the Lord. Lord, have mercy. Let your light so shine before men, that they may see your good works, and glorify your Father Who is in heaven"** (Matthew 5:16) (*«Τοῦ Κυρίου δεηθῶμεν. Κύριε, ἐλέησον. Οὕτω λαμψάτω τὸ φῶς ὑμῶν ἔμπροσθεν τῶν ἀνθρώπων, ὅπως ἴδωσιν ὑμῶν τὰ καλὰ ἔργα καὶ δοξάσωσι τὸν Πατέρα ὑμῶν τὸν ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς»*). The Second Deacon concludes the prayer with: **"Always, now and forever, and unto the ages of ages. Amen."** (*«Πάντοτε, νῦν καὶ ἀεὶ καὶ εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας τῶν αἰώνων. Ἀμήν»*).

The **EAGLE** (**Aetos** / *ἀετός, ὁ*; Slav. *orlets* / *орлецъ*): A small circular or oval liturgical rug depicting an eagle with outspread wings soaring over a fortified city. The bishop stands upon this rug during divine services, and its use is reserved strictly and exclusively unto him. A larger Eagle-rug is placed beneath the bishop-elect during the rite of episcopal consecration, as prescribed in the Pontifical Archieratikon. The representation of the city denotes the bishop's pastoral oversight and canonical governance over his diocese, while the soaring eagle signifies the celestial loftiness and purity of orthodox theological doctrine. Thus the eagle-rug spread beneath the bishop symbolizes that in his life, virtue, and sacred teaching, he must emulate the eagle, which with keen vision soareth above all earthly and corruptible things, fixing its gaze upon heavenly realities.[^710]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Clerical Non-Liturgical Attire: Mandyas – Tables of the Law – Fountains – Cassock Part 1) [Demos, p. 1146 / TD-167]

### Non-Liturgical Attire of the Clergy

The **MANDYAS** (*Μανδύας, ὁ*; mantle / Slav. *mantiya* / *мантія*): The mantle of the bishop is also a monastic garment, which, like a cloak, covers the whole body with the exception of the head. All monks, when present at divine services, must be robed in their mantles. The mantle is not technically speaking a liturgical vestment, but at Vespers and Matins (*Orthros*) it is worn when a bishop first arrives for the divine service and presides from the episcopal throne; it is later removed with assistance by the priests or deacons before he is vested for the Divine Liturgy. In Byzantine Greek practice, the bishop descends from the throne after the chanting of the Katavasiai, enters the sanctuary through the Holy Doors (*Royal Doors* / *Beautiful Gate*) where, after reverencing the Book of the Gospels lying upon the Holy Table, he proceeds to the Diakonikon where the mantle is removed, and if he is to celebrate the Divine Liturgy, he begins vesting with full episcopal vestments. In Slavic practice, after the bishop descends from the throne as described above, he proceeds to the center of the soleas where the mantle is removed and, if he is to celebrate, he is publicly vested for the Divine Liturgy with the assistance of the deacon or priest.

One peculiarity of a bishop's mantle is that it is not black, like the monastic mantle, but usually purple or of some other color. Upon it are sewn the so-called "Tables of the Law" (*Slav. Skrizhali* / *скрижали*), and, in particular, the "Fountains" (*Slav. Istotchniki* / *источники*). The *Tables* (square plaques of fabric sewn at the neck and feet) typify the Old and the New Covenants, from which the ministers of God must draw their doctrine. The *Fountains* are ribbons or stripes of textile, usually red and white in hue, sewn horizontally round about the mantle, representing the streams of divine teaching that flow from the mouth of the bishop. Small bells are attached to the mantle of a bishop, and to his Sakkos, as they were upon the hem of the robe of Aaron, the High Priest of the Old Testament.

The **CASSOCK** (*Ῥάσον, τό*; Slav. *ryasa* / *ряса*): Historically derived from the *chiton* that was worn beneath the *himation* in ancient Greece or the tunic that in ancient Rome was worn beneath the toga. In the Orthodox[^711]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Clerical Non-Liturgical Attire: Cassocks – Kalymafchion – Epanokalymafchon – Pectoral Cross – Skouphos Part 1) [Demos, p. 1147 / TD-168]

Church of today, there are two distinct kinds of cassock as follows:

1. The **EXORASON** (*Ἐξώρασον, τό*; Outer Cassock / Slav. *Ryasa* / *ряса*): A usually black, long, loose-fitting, and flowing ankle-length garment with wide sleeves.

2. The **ANTIRI** (*Ἀντίρι, τό*; Inner Cassock / *ὑπορράσον*, more often simply designated the *Rason*; Slav. *Podriasnik* / *подрясникъ* or *Zostiko*): A usually black garment, gathered at the waist with a narrow cloth belt or sash and tailored with a high collar buttoned in front. Unlike the Roman Catholic soutane or cassock, the Antiri does not have a long row of buttons extending down the front. It is usually worn beneath the liturgical vestments of all sacred clergy. The Antiri symbolizes the death of the clergyman to this world, his mystical burial, and his subsequent dedication unto God and His heavenly kingdom. It should be noted that the wearing of the inner and outer cassock is common to bishops, priests, deacons, monks, and nuns, and that the privilege of wearing a cassock is sometimes granted by a bishop unto seminarians, monastic novices, subdeacons, and readers. As for liturgical vestment colors, when the *Typikon* specifies anything concerning them, it distinguishes only "light" (*bright*) or "dark" vestments, so that local canonical tradition remains the governing standard.

The **KALYMAFCHION** (*Καλυμαύχιον, τό*; also termed *kamilavka*, *kalimafi*, or *kamelaukion*; Slav. *kamilavka* / *камилавка*): The cylindrical clerical head-covering fashioned of stiffened fabric. In Byzantine Greek usage, it is cylindrical with a flattened conical brim projecting around the top rim. In Slavic usage, it is flared outward toward the top and flat without a brim, while in Serbian usage it is cylindrical and flat at the top.

The **EPANOKALYMAFCHON** (*Ἐπανοκαλύμαυχον, τό*; Slav. *klobuk* / *клобукъ*): A black veil worn by bishops and archimandrite monks. The veil is attached to the top of the cylindrical black kamilavka (*kalymafchion*). The latter worn without a veil may be worn by married priests and other secular clergy.

The **PECTORAL CROSS** (*Σταυρός, ὁ*; Slav. *nagrudny krest* / *нагрудный крестъ*): In modern Byzantine Greek usage, the pectoral cross is awarded only when a priest is elevated to the dignity of *Economos* (*Ikonomos*) or Protopresbyter; unlike the Slavic tradition, which establishes an ascending hierarchy of silver, gold, and jeweled pectoral crosses, Greek praxis does not distinguish separate classes of sacerdotal crosses.

The **SKOUPHOS** (*Σκοῦφος, ὁ*; also spelled *skoupho*, *skufiya*; Slav. *skufiya* / *скуфія*):[^712]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Clerical Non-Liturgical Attire: Skouphos Concluded – Vestry / Skevophylakion – Vigil – Vima / Sanctuary Part 1) [Demos, p. 1148 / TD-169]

A soft fabric cap which in Byzantine Greek tradition is usually flat or pleat-folded on top, while in Slavic tradition it is sewn pointed and peaked. It is worn for daily non-liturgical use, particularly by monastics and awarded clergy.

The **VESTRY** (*Σκευοφυλάκιον, τό* or *Ἱεροφυλάκιον, τό*; sacristy / Slav. *sosudokhranilishche* / *сосудохранилище*): These Greek designations signify the sacred depository where the sacred vestments, holy vessels, and liturgical implements are securely preserved. In early Christian antiquity, the *Skevophylakion* was a separate architectural annex erected adjacent to the sanctuary wherein these holy items were kept when not in use. When required for the Divine Liturgy, they were solemnly carried into the temple in public procession. The Great Entrance of the Divine Liturgy today mystically recalls that ancient transfer.

The **VIGIL** (*Ἀγρυπνία, ἡ*; Slav. *Vsenoshchnoe Bdenie* / *Всенощное бдѣніе* / All-Night Vigil): Watchfulness, alertness, or vigilance; derived from the Greek verb *ἀγρυπνέω* (*agrypneo*), signifying to remain wakeful, watchful, or intently attentive upon a sacred duty. Hence, *agrypnia* denotes literally "without sleep" or "sleepless devotion" (Latin *invigilare*). In Byzantine practice, the All-Night Vigil comprises Great Vespers with the Liti and Artoklasia, Matins (*Orthros*), the First Hour, Third Hour, and Sixth Hour, culminating in the Divine Liturgy. If the Divine Liturgy is celebrated immediately following these offices, the First through the Sixth Hours may be omitted or recited separately. The All-Night Vigil, on account of its great length, is not customarily celebrated in ordinary parochial churches, though it is sometimes observed on major solemnities. In monasteries, it is regularly celebrated on Great Feasts of the Lord and the Mother of God, and on the patronal feast of the monastic community.

The **VIMA** (*Βῆμα, τό*; Sanctuary / Slav. *Svyatilishche* / *Святилище* or *Altar*): See Sanctuary. It is likewise known under the designations: *Agion Vima* (*Ἅγιον Βῆμα*), Holy Sanctuary, Bema, Holy Table (*Altar*), or *Ieron* (*Ἱερόν*).

1. Traditionally, the Vima is constructed in the eastern apse of an Orthodox temple structure. The church edifice is oriented in this manner because Christians are instructed from apostolic times to pray facing toward the rising sun in the East, which serves as a cosmic icon of the spiritual Son of God, Jesus Christ, the Orient from on high (*Sol Justitiae*), Who shall likewise appear from the East to illumine the universe at His Second Coming. For practical topographical reasons, certain individual churches may depart from strict eastern orientation. The Vima is an elevated platform raised several steps above the nave, containing[^713]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Vima / Sanctuary Concluded – Narthex / Nave / Sanctuary – Templon / Iconostasion – Ypakoe Part 1) [Demos, p. 1149 / TD-170]

the Holy Table, the Prothesis (*table of oblation*), and the Synthronon, forming the third and innermost precinct in the traditional Byzantine temple. Just as the Narthex was historically the station of the penitents and the catechumens, and the Nave was the congregation hall of the baptized faithful, so the Sanctuary is the sacred precinct of the ordained clergy. Although penitents and catechumens in modern times stand integrated with the faithful within the nave, the sanctuary continues to be reserved unto the sacred ministers of the Church.

The Vima is separated from the Nave by the **Iconostasion** (*Eikonostasion* / icon screen). Up until the fourteenth century, a low waist-high marble chancel railing, designated the **Templon** (*τέμπλον*), separated these areas. Although the iconostasis physically partitions the sacred space within the temple, its mystical purpose is to unite humanity with the Divine: through the holy icons of Christ, the Mother of God, the angelic orders, and the saints, the earthly congregation joins in offering perpetual adoration unto the Holy Trinity.

Regardless of the physical orientation of a local church building, the sanctuary remains an awe-inspiring, holy place. None may enter the Vima unworthily or without the blessing of the priest for an assigned ministry. Sacred clergy and blessed servers alone may enter within the sanctuary, and only after preparing themselves with prayer to stand before the Holy Table and minister alongside the unseen angelic hosts who perpetually surround the Holy of Holies. While all the baptized participate in the royal priesthood of believers according to their respective orders, the sacred ministers execute their stewardship before Christ that all may "glorify God in every place of His dominion" (Psalm 102:22 [MT 103:22]).

The elevated platform in the ancient synagogue, likewise designated a *bema*, served as the place from which the Torah (*Pentateuch*) and the Prophets were proclaimed. In ancient Greek temples, the sacrificial altar (*bōmos* / *βωμός*) was similarly situated facing toward the East.

The **YPAKOE** (*Ὑπακοή, ἡ*; Slav. *Ipakoj* / *ѵпакои* / *ypakoe*): Perhaps the most ancient designation employed in the Christian East for a composed poetic liturgical hymn.[^714]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Ypakoe Concluded – Yperagia – Ypomnema – Zeon Part 1) [Demos, p. 1150 / TD-171]

In contemporary liturgical usage, the **Ypakoe** is the title of the proper Resurrectional troparion chanted during Sunday Matins (*Orthros*). It changes according to the appointed Tone of the week from the Octoechos, occurring immediately after the Evlogitaria (*Resurrectional Troparia with the refrain from Psalm 118:12 [MT 119:12]*: **"Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes"** / *«Εὐλογητὸς εἶ, Κύριε, δίδαξόν με τὰ δικαιώματά Σου»*). The Ypakoe of Pascha is the most renowned instance of this genre, chanted after the Third Ode of the Paschal Canon, during the Paschal Hours, and following the Little Entrance at the Paschal Divine Liturgy.

In the Greek language, *ypakoe* signifies "obedient hearing" or attentive listening (from *hypo*, under, and *akouo*, to hear). The designation indicates a musical and theological response, taking the form of a proper Troparion. Historically and rubrically, the Ypakoe is appointed: (1) at Sunday Compline; (2) at the Sunday Midnight Office; (3) at Sunday Matins before the Antiphons of the Tones and the Matins Gospel; and (4) after the Third Ode of the Matins Canon, taking the place of the Kathisma Hymn (*modified from APL*).

Furthermore, the Ypakoe serves as a short poetic strophe that replaces the third Kathisma hymn at Sunday Matins and upon certain Great Feasts of the Lord. In common parish praxis it is read rather than chanted. In Byzantine Greek usage it is also read during the Sunday Hours in place of the Kontakion. In strict Athonite monastic practice, it is chanted at the Divine Liturgy after the Dismissal Troparia (lit. "Apolytikia", *ἀπολυτίκια*) and before the Kontakia (*AE*).

The **YPERAGIA** (*Ὑπεραγία, ἡ*; Most Holy): The exalted liturgical title given to the Blessed Virgin Mary, Mother of God, signifying "All-Holy" or "More Holy than all creation."

The **YPOMNEMA** (*Ὑπόμνημα, τό*; Synaxarion commentary or notice): In the context of this Typikon, *Ypomnema* denotes an explanatory notice, rubric, or historical memorandum appended to clarify liturgical commemorations and feast-day appointments.

Etymologically derived from *hypo* (under) and *mneme* (memory / remembrance), it signifies a memorial record or postscript. In translation, it designates the additional rubrical commentary and scholarly explanation subjoined to the rubrics of a feast to illuminate its ecclesiastical significance.

The **ZEON** (*Ζέον, τό*; boiling water / Slav. *teplota* / *теплота*): The small vessel or cruet of warm or boiling water brought unto the celebrating priest[^715]

# Appendix: Dictionary of Terms Related to the Typikon

## Extended Dictionary of Typikon Related Terms (Zeon Concluded – Zoni – Zoodochos Pege – Bibliography Part 1: Entries 1–6) [Demos, p. 1151 / TD-172]

by an acolyte or deacon in order that a portion of warm water may be poured into the Holy Chalice (*Agion Potirion*) after the fraction during the preparation of Holy Communion. This vessel may be a simple metal container of brass or silver, or an ornate cruet embellished with enamel, engraving, or gilded ornamentation.

The **ZONE** (*Ζώνη, ἡ*; belt, girdle / Slav. *Poyas* / *поясъ*): The liturgical belt surrounding the Sticharion and securing the Epitrachelion in place. The Zone corresponds historically and functionally to the cincture of the Western Church. Symbolically, the Zone denotes the divine power and fortitude wherewith the sacred priest is girded for holy service (*modified from Byzantines.net*).

The **ZOODOCHOS PEGE** (*Ζωοδόχος Πηγή, ἡ*; Life-Giving Spring): Literally, "Life-Receiving or Life-Giving Spring." This is the feast and title bestowed upon the miraculous shrine and spring dedicated to the Most Holy Theotokos situated at Baloukli (*Eptapyrgos*) outside the walls of Constantinople. The sanctuary was originally founded in the mid-fifth century during the reign of Emperor Leo I the Great (*Makelles*). The Orthodox Church commemorates the consecration of this sacred shrine annually on Bright Friday (the Friday of Renewal Week following Pascha).

The Typikon of the Laura of St. Savvas (Sabbas) the Sanctified (439–532), situated in the wilderness of Judea, was derived from the ancient Typikon of Jerusalem consisting of liturgical ordinances handed down unto St. Savvas by St. Euthymius the Great (377–473) and St. Theoktistos († 467), which derived in turn from the ascetic rules established by St. Chariton the Confessor († 350). The monastic Typikon of St. Savvas, damaged and scattered during ancient devastations of the Palestinian monasteries, was collected and reconstituted by St. Sophronios, Patriarch of Jerusalem (550–638), and subsequently reedited by St. John of Damascus († 749). These historical notices are preserved in ancient Melkite Christian Arabic sources (*see the Melkite Typikon portal at <http://www.typicon.com/>*).
