In the Acrostic entry, the Scriptural citation referencing the great alphabetic acrostic psalm is harmonized with the Septuagint canon as Psalm 118 [MT 119], comprising 22 stanzas of 8 verses corresponding to the 22 letters of the Hebrew abjad.
[^564]: Physical leaf p1008 (printed page [993] / TD-14), concluding the entry for *Aetos* and defining *Afterfeast* (*Μεθέορτα*), *Aftomelon* (*Αὐτόμελον*), and *Agia Trapeza* (*Ἁγία Τράπεζα* — Holy Table / Altar Table). Dr. Demos cites the fundamental patristic principle articulated by St. Ignatius of Antioch (*Epistle to the Philadelphians*, 4) concerning the unity of the Eucharist, the Holy Table, and the bishop. In accordance with the Master Translation Standard (MTS-1), Holy Trinity Deity pronouns (*His gifts*, *His care*) are capitalized with 100% hieratic consistency.
[^565]: Physical leaf p1010 (printed page [995] / TD-16), concluding the historical and patristic exposition of the Holy Mandylion (*Ἅγιον Μανδύλιον* — the Image of Edessa *Acheiropoietos* / "Not Made by Hand") and its associated traditions, citing Eusebius of Caesarea, Procopius, Evagrius Scholasticus, St. John of Damascus, and the Western Veronica tradition. All divine pronouns referring to Christ (*He*, *Him*, *His*, *Himself*) have been emended to full capitalization in compliance with MTS-1 and the Hieratic Deity Pronoun Standard.
[^566]: Physical leaf p1011 (printed page [996] / TD-17), concluding the entry for the Holy Mandylion (*Ἅγιον Μανδύλιον*) and presenting the liturgical entries for the Holy Chalice (*Ἅγιον Ποτήριον*) and the Sanctuary / Holy Bema (*Ἅγιον Βῆμα*). Dr. Demos discusses the historical transmission of the Image of Edessa, its translation to Constantinople under Emperor Romanos I Lekapenos in 944, and modern scholarly hypotheses correlating the Mandylion with the Shroud of Turin.
[^567]: Physical leaf p1012 (printed page [997] / TD-18), concluding the entry for *Agion Vima* and defining *Agios*, *Agripnia* (All-Night Vigil), *Air / Aer* (Slavonic *Vozdukh*), *Akathistos*, and the *Akathist Hymn*. In accordance with canonical UGCC terminology and the Master Translation Standard (MTS-1), the liturgical veil covering both chalice and diskos is designated by its canonical technical loanword *Aer*.
[^568]: Physical leaf p1013 (printed page [998] / TD-19), detailing the liturgical structure, hymnographic distribution, and thematic division of the Akathist Hymn (*Ἀκάθιστος Ὕμνος*) across the first five weeks of Great Lent. In Byzantine and Ruthenian practice, the first four sections (stases) are chanted on the first four Friday evenings at Small Compline, while the entire 24-stanza acrostic hymn is solemnly sung on the fifth Friday evening during Matins of the Saturday of the Akathist.
[^569]: Physical leaf p1015 (printed page [1000] / TD-21), concluding the rubric on the liturgical usage of *Alleluia* and defining *Altar*, *Altar Table* (*Ἁγία Τράπεζα*), and *Amfion* (liturgical vestments). The rubric cites the *Horologion* regarding the penitential use of *Alleluia* and Great Prostrations during fasts, contrasting Eastern liturgical practice with the Western Roman suppression of the Alleluia during Septuagesima and Lent.
[^570]: Physical leaf p1017 (printed page [1002] / TD-23), continuing the scholarly analysis of the *Anaginoskomena* (Scriptural books appointed to be read), citing St. Athanasius the Great's 39th Festal Letter (A.D. 367), the comparative canon of the Greek Septuagint (LXX), the Slavonic Ostrog and Elizabethan Bibles, Psalm 151 (*ἔξωθεν τοῦ ἀριθμοῦ*), and the Biblical Odes appended to the liturgical Psalter in Codex Alexandrinus.
[^571]: Physical leaf p1018 (printed page [1003] / TD-24), concluding the survey of the *Anaginoskomena* with the Danielic additions (Prayer of Azariah and Song of the Three Holy Youths, Susanna, and Bel and the Dragon) and the Prayer of Manasseh as preserved in the Biblical Odes of Rahlfs' and Swete's Septuagint editions, followed by entries for the Reader (*Ἀναγνώστης* / Slavonic *Anagnost*) and the Translation of Relics (*Ἀνακομιδή*).
[^572]: Physical leaf p1020 (printed page [1005] / TD-26), concluding the liturgical exposition of the Eucharistic Anaphora (*Ἀναφορά*) and its Antiochene/West Syrian development, followed by definitions for *Anastasimon* (Resurrectional hymnography) and *Anastasimon Apolytikion* (Resurrection Dismissal Troparion; lit. 'Dismissal Troparion of the Resurrection'). In accordance with the Master Translation Standard (MTS-1) and the Ecosystem Liturgical Lexicon, the Greek designation *Apolytikion* is harmonized with canonical UGCC usage as Dismissal Troparion (*Відпустительний тропар*), and all eight Resurrection Troparia are ordered according to Tones 1 through 8 of the Oktoechos.
[^573]: Physical leaf p1021 (printed page [1006] / TD-27), concluding the entry for the Resurrection Dismissal Troparia (*Ἀναστάσιμα Ἀπολυτίκια*) and presenting the liturgical entries for *Anatolikon*, the Gradual Antiphons (*Ἀναβαθμοί* / Slavonic *Stepénny*), *Angel*, *Anointment*, and the *Anthologion*. The eight Resurrection Dismissal Troparia cycle through Tones 1 to 8, beginning on the Third Sunday of Pascha (Sunday of the Myrrh-Bearing Women) with Tone 2 (**"When You descended"** / *Ὅτε κατῆλθες*). The Gradual Antiphons are drawn from the fifteen Songs of Ascents (Psalms 119–133 LXX [MT 120–134]), divided into three antiphons per tone except Tone 8 which has four, with Tone 4 Antiphon 1 appointed on Feasts.
[^574]: Physical leaf p1022 (printed page [1007] / TD-28), concluding the entry for the *Anthologion* and defining the *Antidoron* (*ἀντίδωρον*) and the *Antiminsion* (*Ἀντιμήνσιον* / Slavonic *Antimins*). The Antiminsion, consecrated by the presiding hierarch with Holy Myron and sealed with holy relics of martyrs in wax mastic, serves as the consecrated portable altar table without which the Divine Liturgy cannot be celebrated. It is wrapped inside the silk *Iliton* (*Εἱλητόν*), symbolizing both the swaddling clothes of the Nativity and the burial shroud (*sindon*) of Christ.
[^575]: Physical leaf p1024 (printed page [1009] / TD-30), defining *Apocrypha*, *Apodipnon* (Compline), the *Apodosis* (*ἀπόδοσις* / Slavonic *Otdániye*), *Apokrisis*, *Apolysis* (Dismissal), and the *Apolytikion* (Dismissal Troparion / *Відпустительний тропар*). In accordance with the Master Translation Standard (MTS-1) and the Ecosystem Liturgical Lexicon, the canonical term *Apodosis* is preserved, designating the final octave day concluding a major feast's afterfeast (*metheorta*), and the Greek hymnographic term *Apolytikion* is harmonized with canonical UGCC usage as Dismissal Troparion.
[^576]: Physical leaf p1026 (printed page [1011] / TD-32), defining the *Apostolos* (Epistle Lectionary), *Archidiakonos* (Archdeacon), *Archieratikon* (Hierarchical Service Book / Slavonic *Chinovnik* / Roman *Pontificale*), *Archierefs* (Hierarch), and the monastic rank of *Archimandrite*. The *Archieratikon* contains the pontifical ordinals for major orders (deacon, presbyter, bishop) and minor orders (reader, subdeacon), along with the hierarchical liturgies and the solemn orders for the consecration of holy antiminsia and church buildings.
[^577]: Physical leaf p1027 (printed page [1012] / TD-33), continuing the historical and canonical exposition of the office of Archimandrite (*Ἀρχιμανδρίτης* / Slavonic *Arkhimandrit*). The text outlines their liturgical vesture (pectoral cross, epigonation or *palitsa*, pastoral staff, and the monastic mantle adorned with the four embroidered *tabulae* or 'tables of the law'), their precedence among the presbyterate, and their historical survival in Southern Italy and Sicily, notably at the Archimandritate of the Basilian Abbey of San Salvatore in Messina.
[^578]: Physical leaf p1028 (printed page [1013] / TD-34), concluding the entry for Archimandrite and defining the *Artoforion* (Tabernacle / Slavonic *Darokhranitel’nitsa*), *Artoklasia* (Blessing of the Loaves / Slavonic *Blagoslovenie khlebov*), *Asmatikon*, and the *Asterisk* (*Ἀστερίσκος* / Slavonic *Zvezditsa*). The rite of Artoklasia, commemorating the miracle of the five loaves (Matthew 14:13–21), is celebrated at Great Vespers during an All-Night Vigil with the blessing of five loaves of wheat bread, wine, and oil.
[^579]: Physical leaf p1029 (printed page [1014] / TD-35), concluding the entry for the *Asterisk* (symbolizing the Star of Bethlehem over the Diskos) and presenting entries for the *Baldachin* (Ciborium), *Bastuni* (pastoral walking staff), the *Beatitudes* (*Μακαρισμοί*), and the *Beautiful Gate* (*Ὡραῖα Πύλη* / Royal Doors). In Byzantine and Ruthenian ecclesiology, the central sanctuary doors symbolize the entrance into the heavenly Kingdom and the virginal womb of the Theotokos through which Christ came into the world.
[^580]: Physical leaf p1030 (printed page [1015] / TD-36), concluding the entry for the *Benedictions* / *Evlogitaria* (*Εὐλογητάρια*), and presenting entries for *Bishop* (*Ἐπίσκοπος* / Hierarch), liturgical *Bow* (*ὑπόκλισις* / metania), and *Bright Week* (*Renewal Week* / *Διακαινήσιμος ἑβδομάς*). The Evlogitaria of the Resurrection are sung at Sunday Matins after Psalm 118 with the refrain of Psalm 118:12 LXX [MT 119:12] (**“Blessed art Thou, O Lord, teach me Thy statutes”**), while Bright Week celebrates the octave of Pascha with each day assigned one of the eight liturgical tones in sequence.
[^581]: Physical leaf p1031 (printed page [1016] / TD-37), presenting entries for the octave rotation of Bright Week (*Διακαινήσιμος ἑβδομάς*), the *Canon* (*κανών*), the office of *Canonarch* (*κανονάρχης* / Slavonic *Kanonarkh*), and the introduction to the Biblical *Canticle* (*ᾠδή*). In Byzantine hymnography, the Canon developed as a structured nine-ode poetic cycle modeled upon the nine scriptural canticles, with each ode consisting of a series of troparia preceded by an overarching Heirmos. The canonarch, tonsured to the minor orders, ensured precise choral execution by announcing each phrase in recitative cadence before the choir chanted.
[^582]: Physical leaf p1032 (printed page [1017] / TD-38), detailing the Nine Biblical Canticles appended to the liturgical Psalter: (1) First Song of Moses (Exodus 15:1–19); (2) Second Song of Moses (Deuteronomy 32:1–43); (3) Prayer of Hannah (1 Kingdoms 2:1–10 LXX [1 Sam 2:1–10]); (4) Prayer of Habakkuk (Habakkuk 3:2–19 LXX); (5) Prayer of Isaiah (Isaiah 26:9–20 LXX); (6) Prayer of Jonah (Jonah 2:2–9 LXX); (7) Prayer of the Three Holy Children in the Furnace (Daniel 3:26–56 LXX); (8) Song of the Three Holy Children (*Benedicite*, Daniel 3:57–88 LXX); and (9) Song of the Theotokos (Magnificat, Luke 1:46–55). Odes 7 and 8 are preserved in the canonical Septuagint text of Daniel.
[^583]: Physical leaf p1033 (printed page [1018] / TD-39), concluding the treatment of the Biblical Canticles (the Benedictus of Zechariah, Luke 1:68–79, and the omission of Ode 2 outside Great Lent) and defining the liturgical *Cassock* (*ράσον* / Slavonic *Podryasnik* and *Ryasa*). The baptismal troparion cited (**“A robe of divine light bestow upon me, O Thou that for vesture arrayest Thyself with Light”**) employs the ancient biblical terms *chiton* (*χιτών*) and *himation* (*ἱμάτιον*), sung as the newly baptized candidate is clothed in the white garment of regeneration.
[^584]: Physical leaf p1034 (printed page [1019] / TD-40), concluding the baptismal hymn and presenting entries for the *Celebrant* (*ἱεροτελεστής*), the liturgical *Censer* (*θυμιατόν* / *θυμιατήριον* / Slavonic *Kadilo*), the hand censer (*Katzion* / *κατζίον*), and *Ceremony* (*τελετή*). Incense symbolizes both the purification and sweet fragrance of the Holy Spirit and the ascending prayers of the Christian faithful (cf. Psalm 140:2 LXX; Revelation 8:3–4).
[^585]: Physical leaf p1035 (printed page [1020] / TD-41), presenting liturgical entries for *Chasmodia* (*χασμῳδία*, forbidden discordant singing under Canon XV of the Council of Laodicea), *Chazranion* (pastoral walking stick), *Chief Secretary* (*Ἀρχιγραμματεύς*), *Choneftirion* (the sanctuary sacrarium or piscina for reverent disposal of sacred elements), *Chorostaton Archierefs* (hierarch presiding in the choir), the *Chromatic* mode in Byzantine octoechos theory, and Holy *Chrism* (*Χρῖσμα* / *Holy Myron*).

The entry details the construction and dedication of Hagia Sophia under Justinian I (December 27, 537) and the theological symbolism of the tripartite division of the temple (Narthex, Nave, and Sanctuary).
[^587]: Physical leaf p1037 (printed page [1022] / TD-43), concluding the architectural entry with the famous testimony of Prince Vladimir's envoys from the *Primary Chronicle* regarding the divine beauty of Hagia Sophia, and presenting entries for the rubric *Chyma* (*χῦμα*, continuous recitative reading or chanting), the transition from scroll to *Codex* (*κῶδιξ*) in early Christian biblical transmission, and the introduction to *Commemoration* (*μνημόσυνον*).
[^588]: Physical leaf p1038 (printed page [1023] / TD-44), defining liturgical *Commemoration* (*μνημόσυνον* / Slavonic *Pominaniye*), the evening office of *Compline* (*ἀπόδειπνον* / Slavonic *Povecheriye*), and the hagiographical rank of *Confessor* (*ὁμολογητής*). Compline exists in two distinct orders—Great Compline and Small Compline—incorporating a canon to the Mother of God in the Tone of the week, transferred commemorations from the *Menaion*, and the mutual asking of forgiveness.
[^589]: Physical leaf p1039 (printed page [1024] / TD-45), defining the *Creed* (*Σύμβολον τῆς Πίστεως* / Slavonic *Simvol Very*) from its origin in early baptismal interrogations to the anti-Arian formulation of the First Ecumenical Council in Nicaea (325). The Symbol of Faith represents the dogmatic consensus of the Church affirming the consubstantiality (*homoousion*) and eternal divinity of the Son of God.
[^590]: Physical leaf p1040 (printed page [1025] / TD-46), setting forth the canonical text of the Nicene Creed and the pneumatological completion ratified by the Second Ecumenical Council in Constantinople (381). The resulting Nicene-Constantinopolitan Symbol of Faith was adopted as the universal baptismal profession and the central dogmatic confession chanted by the faithful at every celebration of the Divine Liturgy.
[^591]: Physical leaf p1041 (printed page [1026] / TD-47), concluding the entry for the *Symbol of Faith* (*Σύμβολον τῆς Πίστεως*), cross-referencing *Cuffs* (*᾿Επιμανίκια* / Slavonic *Narukavnytsi* or *Poruchi*), and commencing the major entry on the order of the *Deacon* (*Διάκονος* / Slavonic *Diakon*). In the Byzantine and Ruthenian liturgical traditions, the diaconate constitutes the third major clerical order, assisting the hierarch and presbyter, intoning the ectenies, and chanting the Holy Gospel, while prohibited from independent sacramental consecration.
[^592]: Physical leaf p1042 (printed page [1027] / TD-48), concluding the treatment of the diaconal office—including its historical evolution from caritative administration to solemn liturgical ministry, administrative ranks (*Protodeacon*, *Archdeacon*, and monastic *Hierodeacon*), and formal ecclesiastical address—and introducing the hymnographic classification *Despotikon* (*Δεσποτικόν* / *Δεσποτικά*).
[^593]: Physical leaf p1043 (printed page [1028] / TD-49), defining *Despotikon* (hymns addressed to God as Master / *Despotis*), cross-referencing *Despotis* (*Δεσπότης*), defining the *Deuterocanon* (*ἀναγινωσκώμενα* / "books read"), and introducing the sanctuary appointments of the *Diakonikon* (*διακονικόν* / Slavonic *Diakonik*) and its relationship to the *Prothesis* and ancient *Skevophylakion*. Traditionally, the icon of the Resurrection (*῾Η ᾿Ανάστασις*) or the Extreme Humility (*ἄκρα ταπείνωσις*) stands within the Diakonikon.

[^595]: Physical leaf p1045 (printed page [1030] / TD-51), incorporating the scholarly doctoral abstract of Fr. Alexander Rentel (*The Patriarchal Diataxis of Dimitrios Gemistos*, Athos Vatopedi MS 135, AD 1386/1390). The study situates Gemistos' patriarchal rubrics at the intersection of Constantinopolitan cathedral ceremonial and the Athonite hesychast monastic reform codified by Patriarch Philotheos Kokkinos.
[^596]: Physical leaf p1046 (printed page [1031] / TD-52), analyzing Gemistos' structural synthesis: nearly seventy-five percent of the *Prothesis* rite in Gemistos' *Diataxis* derives verbatim or with minor modifications from the Philothean monastic *Diataxis*, integrating Athonite monastic rubrics into the cathedral rite of Hagia Sophia.
[^597]: Physical leaf p1047 (printed page [1032] / TD-53), examining the biographical and institutional milieu of Dimitrios Gemistos, patriarchal notary and later *Mega Sakellarios* (*Μέγας Σακελλάριος*), whose career bridged the turbulent patriarchates of Philotheos Kokkinos and Makarios, demonstrating how institutional conservatism and chancery loyalty fostered liturgical codification.
[^598]: Physical leaf p1048 (printed page [1033] / TD-54), evaluating Gemistos' fidelity to the Constantinopolitan Euchologion tradition, his marginal annotations regarding alternate rubrics for celebrations outside the Great Church, and his defense of ancient ceremonial survivals (such as the subdiaconal acclamation *"Whoever is faithful"* / *Ὅσοι πιστοί* during the Anaphora).
[^599]: Physical leaf p1049 (printed page [1034] / TD-55), classifying the nineteen extant manuscript witnesses of Gemistos' *Diataxis* across four primary recensions: the original comprehensive Patriarchal recension, the abbreviated archival form, the later episcopal/archieratical redaction adapted for non-patriarchal dioceses, and the abbreviated Eucharistic formulary.
[^600]: Physical leaf p1050 (printed page [1035] / TD-56), establishing the taxonomy of the four recensions and setting forth the critical editorial methodology employed in establishing the authentic archetype composed by Gemistos in 1386, distinguishing the core patriarchal liturgical text from secondary interpolations and subsequent archieratical reductions.
[^601]: Physical leaf p1051 (printed page [1036] / TD-57), continuing the scholarly analysis of Fr. Alexander Rentel (*The Patriarchal Diataxis of Dimitrios Gemistos*, Athos Vatopedi MS 135, AD 1386/1390). The text elucidates the structure of the three-tiered critical apparatus (reconstruction of the archetype, archieratical recension variants, and Habert's departures from Paris Greek 1362) and evaluates Gemistos' role in integrating the Philothean monastic *Diataxis* into the patriarchal cathedral liturgy of Hagia Sophia.
[^602]: Physical leaf p1052 (printed page [1037] / TD-58), concluding the Gemistos study with comparative citations from Anton Baumstark and Alexander Lingas on the natural organic evolution of Byzantine cathedral and monastic rites versus accusations of arbitrary innovation, and commencing the musical-theoretical entry for *Diatonic* (*διατονικόν* / *Diatonikon*).

[^701]: Physical leaf p1151 (printed page [1136] / TD-157), detailing the sacerdotal vesting prayers of the *Priest* (*ἱερεύς*): the completion of the *Epitrachelion* citing Psalm 132:2 [MT 133:2] with the Aaron anointing typology, the *Zone* (*ζώνη* / Slavonic *poyas*) signifying spiritual fortitude and ministerial readiness citing Psalm 17:33 [MT 18:32], and introducing the *Epimanikia* (*ἐπιμανίκια* / Slavonic *poruchi* / liturgical cuffs) worn over the sticharion sleeves, citing Exodus 15:6–7 for the right cuff under the Father Paul Doxology Standard.
[^702]: Physical leaf p1152 (printed page [1137] / TD-158), concluding the sacerdotal *Epimanikia* with the left cuff vesting prayer citing Septuagint Psalm 118:73 [MT 119:73] symbolizing the bonds of Christ's Passion, and defining the sacerdotal and episcopal *Epigonation* (*ἐπιγονάτιον* / *ὑπογονάτιον* / Slavonic *palitsa*) derived from Byzantine imperial military awards, its mystical signification as the Sword of the Spirit (Ephesians 6:17) and the towel of the Mystical Supper (John 13:4–5), comparative Slavic usage with the rectangular *nabedrennik* (*набедренникъ*), and introducing the royal Psalm 44:4–5 [MT 45:3–4] vesting prayer.
[^703]: Physical leaf p1153 (printed page [1138] / TD-159), concluding the sacerdotal *Epigonation* prayer citing Septuagint Psalm 44:4–5 [MT 45:3–4], detailing the priestly *Phelonion* (*φελόνιον* / *φαιλόνιον* / Slavonic *felon* / *riza*) symbolizing the Passion mantle and sacerdotal righteousness citing Psalm 131:9 [MT 132:9], and initiating the pontifical vesture of the *Bishop* (*ἐπίσκοπος* / *ἀρχιερεύς* / Slavonic *yepyskop*) with the diaconal presentation and blessing of the pontifical *Sticharion* (*στιχάριον*).
[^704]: Physical leaf p1154 (printed page [1139] / TD-160), concluding the episcopal *Sticharion* vesting dialogue with Isaiah 61:10 (noting the printed text's errant citation of Psalm 44), and detailing the hierarchical vesting prayers and diaconal responses for the episcopal *Epitrachelion* (*ἐπιτραχήλιον*) citing Psalm 132:2 [MT 133:2], the episcopal *Zone* (*ζώνη*) citing Psalm 17:33 [MT 18:32], and introducing the episcopal *Epimanikia* (*ἐπιμανίκια*).
[^705]: Physical leaf p1155 (printed page [1140] / TD-161), concluding the episcopal *Epimanikia* (*ἐπιμανίκια*) with Exodus 15:6–7 (right cuff) and Septuagint Psalm 118:73 [MT 119:73] (left cuff), and detailing the episcopal *Sakkos* (*σάκκος* / Slavonic *sakkos*) derived from Byzantine imperial dalmatics, symbolizing penitential humility and the Passion robe, with its pontifical vesting prayer citing Psalm 131:9 [MT 132:9] under the Father Paul Doxology Standard.
[^706]: Physical leaf p1156 (printed page [1141] / TD-162), detailing the episcopal *Epigonation* (*ἐπιγονάτιον* / Slavonic *palitsa*) as an obligatory pontifical vestment with its full diaconal proclamation citing Septuagint Psalm 44:4–5 [MT 45:3–4], and introducing the primary pontifical insignia, the *Omophorion* (*ὠμοφόριον* / Slavonic *omofor*), distinguishing the Great (Long) and Small (Short) forms.
[^707]: Physical leaf p1157 (printed page [1142] / TD-163), concluding the liturgical rubrics and patristic symbolism of the *Omophorion* (*ὠμοφόριον*) as the lost sheep of the Good Shepherd (Luke 15:4–6) signifying universal canonical jurisdiction, contrasting its episcopal universality with the Western Latin *pallium*, detailing its vesting prayer celebrating the Incarnation and Ascension, and introducing the episcopal *Pectoral Cross* (*ἐπιστήθιος σταυρός* / Latin *pectus*).
[^708]: Physical leaf p1158 (printed page [1143] / TD-164), concluding the episcopal *Pectoral Cross* with the evangelical vesting proclamation citing Mark 8:34, detailing the pectoral medallion or *Engolpion* (*ἐγκόλπιον*) and the *Panagia* (*Παναγία*) bearing the icon of the Mother of God with its vesting dialogue citing the Miserere (Psalm 50:12 [MT 51:10]), and introducing the *Second Medallion* (*Ἐγκόλπιον β΄*) bearing the icon of Christ.
[^709]: Physical leaf p1159 (printed page [1144] / TD-165), concluding the episcopal *Second Medallion* (*ἐγκόλπιον*) citing royal Psalm 44:2 [MT 45:1], detailing the episcopal *Miter* (*μίτρα* / Slavonic *mitra*) derived from post-Byzantine imperial regalia with its coronation vesting prayer citing Psalm 20:4–5 [MT 21:3–4], and introducing the pontifical *Pastoral Staff* (*ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος* / Slavonic *posokh* / *zhezl*).
[^710]: Physical leaf p1160 (printed page [1145] / TD-166), concluding the pontifical *Pastoral Staff* (*ποιμαντορικὴ ῥάβδος* / *posokh*) with the serpent typology (Matthew 10:16) and messianic investiture prayer citing Psalm 109:2 [MT 110:2], the investiture of the episcopal two- and three-branched candelabras (*Dikerotrikera* / *δικηροτρίκηρα*) citing Matthew 5:16, and defining the episcopal rug or *Eagle* (*ἀετός* / Slavonic *orlets*) symbolizing doctrinal elevation and diocesan pastoral governance.
[^711]: Physical leaf p1161 (printed page [1146] / TD-167), detailing the non-liturgical attire of the sacred clergy: the episcopal and monastic *Mandyas* (*μανδύας* / Slavonic *mantiya* / mantle), comparative liturgical practice between Byzantine Greek and Slavic traditions during pontifical entrance at Matins and Divine Liturgy, the symbolic iconography of the episcopal mantle including the "Tables of the Law" (*skrizhali*) and the "Fountains" (*istotchniki*) signifying the flow of divine dogma, and introducing the historical development of the *Cassock* (*ῥάσον*).
[^712]: Physical leaf p1162 (printed page [1147] / TD-168), detailing non-liturgical clerical attire continued: the *Exorason* (*ἐξώρασον* / Slavonic *ryasa* / outer cassock) and *Antiri* (*ἀντίρι* / *ὑπορράσον* / Slavonic *pidriasnyk* / inner cassock), the historical rule on liturgical vestment colors ("light" vs. "dark"), ecclesiastical headgear comprising the *Kalymafchion* (*καλυμαύχιον* / Slavonic *kamilavka*), the monastic veil or *Epanokalymafchon* (*ἐπανοκαλύμαυχον* / Slavonic *klobuk*), the sacerdotal *Pectoral Cross* (*σταυρός*), and introducing the soft cap or *Skouphos* (*σκοῦφος* / Slavonic *skufiya*).
[^713]: Physical leaf p1163 (printed page [1148] / TD-169), defining the *Skouphos* (*skufiya*) concluded; the *Vestry* (*σκευοφυλάκιον* / *ἱεροφυλάκιον* / sacristy) and its historical relationship to the Great Entrance procession; the *Vigil* (*ἀγρυπνία* / Slavonic *vsenoshchnoe bdenie* / All-Night Vigil) with its patristic etymology, structure (Great Vespers, Liti, Artoklasia, Matins, First, Third, Sixth Hours, and Divine Liturgy); and initiating the entry on the *Vima* (*βῆμα* / sanctuary) and traditional eastward orientation of the temple.
[^714]: Physical leaf p1164 (printed page [1149] / TD-170), concluding the *Vima* (*βῆμα* / sanctuary) with its liturgical theology, the tripartite temple division (narthex, nave, sanctuary), the historical development from the low *templon* railing to the high iconostasion, reverent entry rules for the Holy of Holies; alphabetical indices W and X (no entries); and introducing the ancient hymnographic genre of the *Ypakoe* (*ὑπακοή* / Slavonic *ypakoy*).
[^715]: Physical leaf p1165 (printed page [1150] / TD-171), detailing the liturgical placement and hymnographic function of the *Ypakoe* (*ὑπακοή* / *ypakoi*), its etymology ("obedient hearing"), its occurrence in Sunday and Paschal offices, and comparative Athonite vs. parish practice; the Marian epithet *Yperagia* (*Ὑπεραγία* / "Most Holy"); the editorial definition of *Ypomnema* (*ὑπόμνημα* / explanatory postscript or memoir); and introducing the *Zeon* (*ζέον* / hot water vessel).
[^716]: Physical leaf p1166 (printed page [1151] / TD-172), concluding *Zeon* (*ζέον* / the liturgical boiling water poured into the chalice); *Zone* (*ζώνη* / *poyas* / priestly girdle); the feast of the Mother of God the *Life-Giving Spring* (*Ζωοδόχος Πηγή* / *Zoodochos Pege* at Baloukli near Constantinople, celebrated on Bright Friday); and opening the scholarly *Bibliography for the Dictionary* (entries 1–6: Abramtsev, American Heritage Dictionary, Monk of St. Tikhon's, Apostolic Diaconate, Archimandrite Ephrem Lash, Theodore Bogdanos).
